# FOUNDATIONS

## Conceptual and Formal Groundwork

<a id="chapter-0-position-and-claim-boundary"></a>

## 0. Position and Claim Boundary

PMS-STRATA begins from a limited but consequential methodological problem. PMS can reconstruct praxis within a declared frame and at a chosen analytical resolution. Such reconstructions often become more complex, however, when an analysis moves between local occurrences and composites, between compressed and finer descriptions, or between an object's established type and a function attributed to it in another context. These movements may be useful and sometimes necessary. They are not self-validating.

The purpose of STRATA is to make such movements explicit, typed, bounded, and open to failure. It extends the method available within PMS without changing PMS Base, without positing an ontology of real strata, and without converting additional analytical structure into additional authority.

The governing reduction is:

```text
more structure
≠
more authority
```

This is not a caution added after the method has already been defined. It is a constitutive condition of the method.

### 0.1 Why PMS-STRATA?

PMS ordinarily asks how praxis becomes structurally legible through difference, directed activation, frame, non-event, attractor, asymmetry, temporality, recontextualization, distance, integration, and self-binding. A reconstruction can remain local: one declared reference object, one frame, one source basis, one temporal scope, and one warranted claim. Nothing in PMS requires every reconstruction to be expanded beyond that position.

Yet some questions cannot be answered responsibly without changing the analytical position from which the object is reconstructed. A sequence of local configurations may need to be examined as a path. A provisionally compressed occurrence may need to be opened under finer resolution. A historically formed object may need to be considered for a bounded function in a later context. Such work introduces transformations among declared positions of analysis.

The problem is not that PMS lacks the operators needed to make praxis legible. STRATA does not repair a missing operator in the Δ–Ψ grammar. The problem is that movement among temporal structures, granularities, composites, and contextual functions can otherwise remain implicit. When that movement remains implicit, the analyst may change the object, resolution, level relation, or function of a claim without changing the claim's wording, evidential burden, or stated limit. A local reconstruction can then appear to authorize a larger one merely because the transition between them has not been exposed.

STRATA is required at precisely this point. It supplies a controlled transformation discipline for questions such as:

- Which source structures are being carried into a composite claim?
- Which distinctions become visible or disappear when granularity changes?
- Which reference object is preserved across the transformation?
- Which function is asserted only in a declared target context?
- Which losses, exclusions, uncertainties, or irrecoverable compressions accompany the move?
- Where must the claim be reduced, stopped, failed, or left uncaptured?

These questions do not create a new subject matter beside praxis. They discipline how a PMS reconstruction changes analytical position while remaining accountable to its sources and boundaries.

### 0.2 The Missing Vertical Discipline

Without an explicit transformation discipline, several distinct movements can be compressed into ordinary descriptive language.

First, an analysis may silently compose. Several occurrences, events, non-events, or transitions are narrated together and then treated as one larger analytical object. The result may be plausible, but its selection rule, temporal ordering, constitutive relations, and losses remain undeclared.

Second, an analysis may silently decompose. What was initially treated as one occurrence or one composite is opened into finer structures, but it is left unclear whether the same reference object is still being reconstructed, whether the additional detail changes a warranted praxis claim, or whether the analysis has merely accumulated resolution.

Third, an analysis may silently shift function. A source object with one established analytical type is described as performing another role in a different context, but the target context, validity scope, and source trace of that role remain unstated. A contextual function may then be mistaken for a new origin type or even for a new PMS primitive.

These are not errors merely because they move across analytical positions. The movement itself can be legitimate. The error lies in treating the movement as costless, self-explanatory, or authority-bearing.

A missing vertical discipline produces four recurrent risks.

**Level mixing.** A statement about one declared relation is transferred to another relative level without identifying the source and target objects that make the level relation meaningful.

**Resolution drift.** Finer description is treated as if detail itself supplied greater truth, even where no warranted reconstruction changes.

**Retrospective macro-typing.** A larger label is placed over heterogeneous source material after the fact, while the source path, internal variation, and counterevidence become difficult to recover.

**Authority inflation.** A reconstruction becomes more elaborate, more formal, or more navigable and is therefore treated as more valid, more general, or more action-guiding than its source claim permits.

STRATA addresses these risks by requiring that a movement be declared as a new testable transformation claim. The declaration does not make the claim admissible. It makes the claim inspectable.

```text
implicit analytical movement
→ hidden change of claim

explicit analytical movement
→ new testable claim with its own burden
```

The distinction is central. A claim boundary determines what kind of assertion STRATA may make at all. A later admissibility test determines whether a particular transformation is sufficiently supported within that boundary. The existence of a method does not pre-authorize any occurrence of its use.

### 0.3 STRATA as a Bounded Transformation Discipline

PMS-STRATA is a bounded transformation discipline within PMS. It specifies how declared praxis structures may be composed, decomposed, and functionally projected while preserving the difference between source and target, type and function, analytical relation and ontological assertion, legibility and authority.

At the core level, STRATA contains exactly three operations: `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, and `PROJECT_AS`. Chapter 0 names their bounded scope but does not define their signatures; that task belongs to Chapter 4. `LIMITS` is a Part that systematizes constraints, not a fourth operation or a meta-level above the three operations.

The term *bounded* has several simultaneous implications.

**Source-bounded.** A transformation may not claim more internal structure, historical continuity, or functional load than its source basis can carry.

**Reference-bounded.** The object to which the reconstruction refers must remain identifiable, or the analysis must state that a new object or claim has been introduced.

**Context-bounded.** A result applies only within the declared frame, temporal scope, granularity relation, relative-level relation, and target context that support it.

**Type-bounded.** An origin type is not silently replaced by a contextual function, and a derived object or function is not promoted into a new PMS primitive.

**Loss-bounded.** Selection, compression, exclusion, uncertainty, and irrecoverable loss remain part of the transformation record rather than disappearing behind the target result.

**Claim-bounded.** A successful transformation warrants only the claim it has actually carried. It does not inherit empirical, causal, normative, legal, political, diagnostic, person-evaluative, intervention, or application authority.

**Failure-bounded.** The method must preserve the possibility of claim reduction, mandatory stop, failed transformation, and non-capture. A transformation that cannot fail is not a controlled STRATA transformation.

STRATA therefore belongs to the reconstructive method of PMS, not to a tribunal over its objects. It does not decide what persons, institutions, or practices are. It does not diagnose inner states, assign moral rank, establish legal or political legitimacy, or generate automatic recommendations. Its task is narrower: to make a transformation's analytical commitments visible and testable.

This boundedness also distinguishes recursive availability from recursive necessity. A structure may be open to another transformation without requiring one. Further composition, finer decomposition, or another functional projection is justified only by a new, bounded question and an adequate source basis. The mere availability of another operation is never a reason to continue.

```text
transformation available
≠
transformation required
≠
transformation admissible
```

### 0.4 No Meta-PMS

STRATA is not a meta-PMS.

It does not stand above PMS Base, judge PMS Base from an external position, or supply a superior grammar under which Δ–Ψ would become subordinate cases. PMS Base remains the governing operator inventory and dependency specification. STRATA depends on that grammar and may neither rename its operators, alter their canonical dependency path, decompose operator types, nor add another base operator by methodological convenience.

This dependency is asymmetric in authority but not revisionary in content:

```text
PMS Base
→ supplies the unchanged operator grammar and guardrails

PMS-STRATA
→ specifies bounded transformations of PMS reconstructions
```

STRATA can reveal that a reconstruction has mixed levels, exceeded its source basis, collapsed an origin type into a target function, or protected itself from a counterexample. Such a finding concerns the transformation claim. It does not grant STRATA authority to rewrite the base grammar.

Nor does a successful multi-step reconstruction create meta-authority. An analysis may become more explicit, more discriminating, and more traceable through several transformations. Those gains remain methodological gains. They do not convert the result into a superior truth claim, an application mandate, or an adjudication of rival frameworks.

The same limit applies to formalization. A coherent YAML record, valid JSON Schema instance, passed decision-tree route, successful package audit, or large canonical corpus can show internal consistency, declared completeness, or traceability of the formal artifact. None of these establishes empirical truth, causal adequacy, semantic validity, normative validity, or application authority.

```text
formal consistency
≠
substantive truth

successful transformation
≠
authority inheritance
```

### 0.5 No Ontology of Layers

STRATA uses the language of granularity, relative level, part, composite, source, and target. This language is analytical. It does not assert that reality is composed of objectively discrete layers waiting to be discovered.

A relative level exists only within a declared relation. An object may be treated as a part of one composite and as a composite relative to another object. The designation does not reveal an absolute position in reality. Similarly, granularity names the resolution of the distinctions used in a reconstruction. It does not name depth, truth, rank, or ontological fundamentality.

For the same reason, terms such as *micro*, *meso*, and *macro* may function only as local shorthand where the comparison relation is explicit. They are not universal classes, fixed sizes, or natural hierarchies. STRATA claims neither a final smallest praxeological element nor a largest legitimate totality.

Composition is likewise a reconstructive achievement, not the discovery of an ultimate whole. Decomposition opens a provisionally compressed object under another resolution; it does not uncover final constituents. Functional projection attributes a bounded role in a target context; it does not reveal that the source object has always or essentially possessed the target type.

The anti-ontological rule can be stated compactly:

```text
relative level
=
position within a declared analytical relation

relative level
≠
objective layer of reality
```

This rule does not deny that real systems may contain durable organizations, nested structures, distributed processes, or materially consequential differences of scale. It limits what STRATA itself may infer from the act of reconstruction. Whether a domain contains objectively distinct structures is an external empirical or theoretical question requiring its own warrant. STRATA cannot settle it by naming an analytical level.

### 0.6 Governing Claim

The maximum positive claim of PMS-STRATA is:

> **PMS-STRATA specifies how praxis structures may be composed, decomposed, and functionally projected across declared granularities and relative levels—while preserving source traceability, origin-type integrity, transformation loss, claim boundaries, stop, and non-capture. It does not turn analytical recursion into ontological totality, greater authority, or immunity from failure.**

This governing claim establishes a methodological specification, not a domain finding. It says what STRATA controls: declared transformations among analytical positions. It does not say that a given transformation is warranted, that every object is transformable, that all relevant structure can be captured, or that the resulting claim is true beyond its source and context.

The governing claim has five consequences.

1. **Every transformation is a claim.** It must identify what is being moved, what analytical relation is changing, and what the target assertion adds.
2. **Every transformation remains dependent.** Its warrant comes from declared source structures, not from the operation name or formal record alone.
3. **Every transformation is lossy or selectively preserving.** Even where a result is admissible, preservation and loss must remain inspectable.
4. **Every transformation is bounded.** Context, scope, source ceiling, claim ceiling, and authority ceiling remain operative.
5. **Every transformation remains fallible.** Counterexamples, rival reconstructions, claim reduction, stop, failed transformation, and non-capture cannot be designed out of the method.

These consequences authorize the later Foundations chapters to define the object model, analytical coordinates, temporal object chain, operation grammar, type-function distinction, admissibility band, shared record, and foundational non-equivalences. They do not authorize those chapters to enlarge the claim of STRATA.

### 0.7 Initial Claim Boundary

PMS-STRATA is not:

- a superior PMS layer;
- a new operator grammar;
- an ontology of levels or strata;
- a universal micro–meso–macro scale theory;
- a proof of recursive completeness;
- a lossless transformation system;
- an unlimited decomposition method;
- an automatic generator of valid composites, macro-objects, or target functions;
- a mechanism for dissolving counterexamples by changing frame, granularity, level, or function;
- a proof that formal translation captures the object without remainder;
- a source of empirical, causal, predictive, normative, legal, political, diagnostic, person-evaluative, intervention, or application authority.

These exclusions are not external disclaimers attached to an otherwise unrestricted method. They identify claims that STRATA cannot make without ceasing to be the bounded discipline defined here.

#### Minimal case: from local reconstruction to higher-level claim

Consider four locally reconstructed scenes, `S1` through `S4`, within one declared frame. In each scene, an expected transition does not occur, and the local PMS reconstruction identifies a recurring relation among frame, non-event, asymmetry, and temporality. The local claim is bounded:

```text
Within S1–S4, the same praxis-relevant non-event structure recurs.
```

A silent vertical move occurs when the analysis continues:

```text
Therefore, the larger process is governed by this structure.
```

The second sentence is not merely a restatement of the first. It introduces a new reference reach and a higher-level claim. It may require selection among scenes, a rule for how their relations form a composite, a declared temporal scope, treatment of omitted or contrary scenes, and a limit on what “governed by” means. Without these declarations, the larger claim borrows the legibility of the local reconstruction while concealing its additional burden.

An explicit STRATA declaration does not automatically validate the larger claim. It makes the change testable:

```text
illustrative declaration — not a Transformation Record schema

source reference: S1–S4
source frame: declared local frame
source claim: recurring non-event structure
proposed target reference: larger process
proposed target claim: bounded higher-level structure

required boundary disclosures:
- preserve the source relation
- disclose selection and loss
- declare the target scope
- prohibit authority inheritance
- keep Stop and Non-Capture available
```

Several results remain possible. The larger claim may be supported within a narrow scope. It may require reduction to a weaker statement. The available scenes may be insufficient, requiring stop. Contrary scenes may prevent stable composition. The larger process may remain non-captured under the present sources and transformation. What is not permitted is to treat the transition from the local claim to the larger claim as already warranted because the local reconstruction was plausible.

The example also shows why a change of analytical position cannot erase an earlier failure. Suppose the local claim is later contradicted in `S3`. Moving to a larger composite does not repair that local failure. The original claim remains failed or reduced, and the higher-level reconstruction becomes a separate claim that must explicitly preserve the counterevidence.

```text
failed source claim
+
new analytical position
=
failed source claim preserved
+
new claim separately tested
```

#### Counterpressure and bounded handoff

The initial boundary remains effective even where STRATA appears productive.

A higher-level reconstruction may be more legible without being more authoritative. A vertical transformation may be available but inadmissible. Multiple plausible transformations may still fail to capture the object adequately. A successful transformation may improve reconstruction while leaving empirical causality, normative judgment, and application entirely undecided.

Accordingly, Chapter 0 grants Chapters 1–8 only a bounded mandate. They may define the objects, coordinates, operations, continuity requirements, admissibility tests, records, and non-equivalences needed to control STRATA transformations. They may not create new PMS operators, ontological layers, universal scales, person types, automatic decisions, or additional authority.

The initial claim boundary is therefore both enabling and restrictive:

```text
STRATA may make analytical transformation explicit.
STRATA may test whether that transformation is bounded and traceable.
STRATA may preserve failure, reduction, stop, and non-capture.

STRATA may not turn analytical recursion into ontological totality,
formal completion into truth,
or additional legibility into authority.
```

<a id="chapter-1-object-model-operator-type-operator-occurrence-and-composite-structure"></a>

## 1. Object Model: Operator Type, Operator Occurrence, and Composite Structure

Every STRATA transformation presupposes an answer to a prior question: **what is the object being transformed?** An operation cannot be made accountable merely by naming `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, or `PROJECT_AS`. The source must first be identified as an eligible analytical object, and the claim must preserve the difference between the abstract PMS operator grammar and the concrete structures reconstructed through that grammar.

This chapter establishes that object model. Its first task is to separate operator sign, operator name, operator type, operator occurrence, and composite structure. Later sections extend the model to configurations, event-like objects, non-event structures, transitions as objects, derived analytical objects, and minimal object identification. The present architecture is therefore foundational but bounded: it defines what kinds of things may become sources or results of STRATA transformations without yet defining analytical coordinates, temporal grammar, operation signatures, continuity tests, admissibility criteria, or record fields.

The governing object rule is:

```text
PMS Base operator type
≠
concrete operator occurrence
≠
composite analytical object
```

A failure to preserve this rule corrupts every later transformation. If an operator type is treated as a concrete object, `DECOMPOSE` can be misused as if the type had empirical parts. If an occurrence is treated as the type itself, a local and revisable reconstruction can be mistaken for a canonical feature of PMS Base. If a composite is treated as one occurrence, its selection, internal relations, heterogeneity, and losses disappear. If a sign or name is treated as the type, notation can be confused with the function it denotes.

The object model blocks these substitutions before any operation begins.

### 1.1 Why an Object Model Is Required

STRATA works on claims about praxis structures. Such claims may concern one concrete occurrence, several related occurrences, a temporally located configuration, a structured change, a non-realization under an expectation, a transition, or a composite formed from several source structures. These objects differ in what must be preserved, what may be opened, and what kind of target claim can be warranted. They cannot be treated as interchangeable merely because the same operator vocabulary appears in their descriptions.

An object model is required for five reasons.

**First, operation eligibility depends on object category.** `DECOMPOSE` may open a provisionally compressed occurrence or composite under finer granularity. It may not open an abstract Δ–Ψ operator type as though the type contained empirical subparts. `COMPOSE` forms a declared analytical object from multiple source structures and constitutive relations. It does not combine operator names into a composite merely by placing them in a list. `PROJECT_AS` concerns an origin-typed source object and a bounded contextual target function. It does not transform the source into another PMS operator type.

**Second, source traceability depends on object identity.** A transformation must remain answerable to the reference object its source claim concerns. Before Chapter 5 specifies the full continuity architecture, Chapter 1 must at least distinguish the abstract type from the concrete reference-bound occurrence and the occurrence from any larger composite in which it is included. Otherwise a transformation can change its object while preserving only its label.

**Third, type integrity depends on category discipline.** PMS Base defines the eleven operator types Δ–Ψ, their canonical signs and names, their repository-reference order, and their dependency path. STRATA may type concrete reconstructions through those operators. It may not alter the operator inventory by treating a local occurrence, a derived object, or a contextual function as a new type.

**Fourth, disagreement depends on separating the object from its typing.** Two reconstructions may concern the same reference object while assigning different operator typings or different analytical loads. Such disagreement does not automatically revise PMS Base, and it does not automatically imply that the reference object has changed. It is a disagreement among bounded occurrence-typing claims. The object model must preserve that possibility.

**Fifth, composite claims require more than aggregation.** Several observations, records, or operator labels do not become a composite merely because they are mentioned together. A composite requires identifiable constituents, a declared boundary, and at least one constitutive relation explaining how the constituents form the claimed analytical object. Internal sequence, hierarchy, recurrence, mutual constraint, distributed function, or another supported relation may be constitutive. The relation must be stated rather than assumed.

The object model therefore distinguishes three different questions:

```text
What notation is used?
→ sign and name

What abstract PMS function is meant?
→ operator type

What concrete source-supported structure is being reconstructed?
→ operator occurrence or composite structure
```

These questions are related, but no answer substitutes for another. The sign and name enable reference to the type. The type supplies a canonical function from PMS Base. The occurrence is a bounded analytical claim about a concrete reference object. The composite is a further analytical object whose identity depends on multiple constituents and their declared relations.

This architecture does not assert that the world is naturally divided into operator occurrences and composites as ontologically discrete units. Object categories are reconstruction controls. They state what the analysis claims to treat as one reference-bearing object and what burden follows from that claim. A domain may contain materially durable structures, but STRATA cannot establish their ontological boundaries merely by classifying them.

### 1.2 Operator Sign, Operator Name, and Operator Type

The Δ–Ψ grammar is presented through canonical signs and names. These are indispensable for reference, but they are not the operator types themselves.

An **operator sign** is the canonical symbol used in the current PMS repository reference, such as `□`.

An **operator name** is the canonical English label associated with that sign, such as `Frame`.

An **operator type** is the abstract PMS Base function to which the sign and name refer. For `□`, PMS Base defines the Frame operator type as the contextual structure that constrains and shapes impulses, with the dependencies and provided structures recorded in `PMS.yaml`.

The distinction is compact:

```text
□
=
canonical sign

Frame
=
canonical name

contextual structure that constrains and shapes impulses
=
canonical operator-type function in the current PMS Base reference
```

The equality marks relations of designation within the current repository reference. It does not collapse the three terms into one object.

```text
operator sign
≠
operator name
≠
operator type
```

A sign can be printed, copied, omitted, mistyped, or rendered in another notation. A name can be translated, abbreviated, quoted, or used ambiguously. Neither event changes the operator type. Conversely, a proposed revision of the operator type would be a revision of PMS Base and would require explicit versioned authority at that level. It could not be achieved by changing the sign, renaming the operator, or applying a STRATA operation.

The operator type is abstract in a precise methodological sense. It belongs to the governing grammar through which concrete praxis structures are reconstructed. It is not one empirical object among other empirical objects. Its dependencies in PMS Base are grammar and derivation relations; they are not material pieces located inside the type.

For this reason, STRATA does not decompose operator types.

```text
DECOMPOSE(□)
→ prohibited as a STRATA transformation
```

This prohibition does not make PMS Base immune from criticism or revision. The definition, ordering, dependency path, minimality, explanatory adequacy, or empirical usefulness of the Frame operator may be questioned at the level of PMS theory and versioned Base revision. The point is narrower: such theoretical critique is not a `DECOMPOSE` occurrence. A STRATA decomposition must begin from a concrete occurrence or composite whose reference can be preserved under finer reconstruction.

The same limit applies to all eleven types. Difference, Impulse, Frame, Non-Event, Attractor, Asymmetry, Temporality, Recontextualization, Distance, Integration, and Self-Binding remain the canonical PMS Base types in the current repository reference. Chapter 1 neither reorders them nor decomposes them nor adds another type. It establishes the type boundary required to prevent later analytical objects from being mistaken for extensions of Δ–Ψ.

#### Minimal Frame case: sign, name, type, and occurrence

Consider an illustrative source bundle describing a scheduled review session. The bundle contains a circulated agenda, a role list, and a transcript showing that only agenda-listed matters were admitted for decision during the session.

Four distinct claims can be made:

1. `□` is the canonical **operator sign**.
2. `Frame` is the canonical **operator name**.
3. The Frame **operator type** is the PMS Base function of contextual constraint and relevance structuring.
4. The documented review arrangement may be reconstructed as one concrete **Frame-typed occurrence** because, within the declared session and source basis, it constrained which matters, roles, and responses counted as relevant.

The fourth claim is not contained in the first three. Knowing the sign, name, and canonical function does not establish that the review arrangement actually performed that function. The occurrence claim requires a reference object, a source basis, a bounded context, and a stated analytical claim. It may later be strengthened, reduced, rivalled, or withdrawn without changing `□`, the name `Frame`, or the Base definition.

The distinction can therefore be represented as follows:

```text
□
→ denotes Frame

Frame
→ names the operator type

Frame operator type
→ supplied by PMS Base

review arrangement reconstructed as frame-typed
→ bounded occurrence claim supplied by the analysis
```

The review arrangement may be decomposable as a concrete occurrence: its agenda rule, role allocation, admission practice, and decision boundary may be reconstructed under finer granularity if the same reference object and adequate sources are preserved. The Frame operator type is not decomposed in that process. What becomes finer is the reconstruction of the occurrence.

### 1.3 Operator Occurrence

An **operator occurrence** is a concrete, reference-bound, context- or frame-bound, source-bound, and claim-bound structure reconstructed as expressing a PMS operator function within a specific analysis.

This definition contains several limits.

**Concrete** means that the occurrence concerns an identifiable reference object or relation rather than the abstract type. The reference may be an observed scene, a documented practice, a reconstructed institutional arrangement, a temporally extended pattern, or another source-supported praxis structure. Concrete does not require direct sensory observation, but it does require more than a free-floating example or operator label.

**Reference-bound** means that the claim identifies what it is about. An occurrence is not established by saying that “a Frame is present.” The analysis must be able to state which arrangement, relation, scene, practice, or source object is being typed. Chapter 5 later specifies the stronger continuity burden across transformation; Chapter 1 establishes only that an occurrence must have a distinguishable reference.

**Context- or frame-bound** means that the typing does not automatically extend beyond the analytical conditions under which it is supported. Chapter 2 defines frame and the other analytical coordinates. Chapter 1 uses the boundedness requirement without pre-empting those definitions.

**Source-bound** means that the occurrence claim depends on declared support. A source may underdetermine the typing, support several typings, or fail to support any admissible typing. Operator vocabulary cannot replace evidence.

**Claim-bound** means that the occurrence is reconstructed only as strongly as the supported assertion permits. A local Frame-typing does not establish a universal institutional character, a permanent structure, a person-level property, or the causal explanation of everything occurring in the scene.

An occurrence is therefore not a simple token in the sense that every empirical object must instantiate exactly one operator type. PMS operators are analytical functions within a praxeological grammar. A concrete source object may support several operator typings because it performs several structurally relevant functions, or it may support rival typings because the evidence and analytical question do not settle which function carries the claim.

The distinction between type and occurrence can be stated in a control table:

| Dimension | Operator type | Operator occurrence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Authority | defined by PMS Base | reconstructed in a specific analysis |
| Reference | abstract function in Δ–Ψ | identifiable concrete source object or relation |
| Scope | repository-reference grammar | declared context, sources, and claim |
| Revision | versioned PMS Base revision | local claim revision, reduction, rivalry, or withdrawal |
| Decomposition | prohibited as a STRATA source | possible when treated as a provisionally compressed occurrence and later criteria are met |
| Person inference | none | never authorizes a global person property or diagnosis |

The occurrence claim remains defeasible. New sources may reveal that the typed function was weaker than claimed, active only during part of the reference interval, secondary to another function, or absent. The correct response is to revise the occurrence claim. It is not to redefine the operator type so that the original typing becomes unfalsifiable.

#### Competing-typing pressure

Return to the documented review arrangement. The agenda and admission rule support a Frame-typing because they constrain relevance and role space. Suppose the same source bundle also shows that final approval is reserved to one office while all other participants may only recommend.

The arrangement may now support at least two occurrence-typing claims:

```text
Claim A:
the review arrangement functions as a Frame occurrence
because it constrains admissible matters and roles.

Claim B:
the review arrangement functions as an Asymmetry occurrence
because decision capacity and exposure are distributed unequally.
```

These claims are not revisions of the Frame or Asymmetry operator types. They are claims about the same concrete reference object under different operator typings. They may be compatible if both functions are source-supported and relevant to the analytical question. They may also compete if one reconstruction claims exclusive or primary explanatory load that the sources cannot settle.

The object model does not resolve the rivalry automatically. It requires the rivalry to remain visible.

```text
same reference object
+
competing operator typings
≠
changed PMS Base
```

A bounded local result may therefore be:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality attaches to the occurrence-typing claim, not to the canonical existence of the operator types. Further source work may support both typings, privilege one for a declared question, reduce both, or require stop. No result authorizes conversion of the occurrence into a global property of an institution or person.

The example also distinguishes multiple typing from composite formation. The fact that one reference object can be reconstructed through more than one operator function does not by itself make it a composite. A composite claim requires identifiable constituents and constitutive relations, not merely several labels applied to one undifferentiated reference.

### 1.4 Composite Structure

A **composite structure** is a selectively formed analytical object containing multiple identifiable constituents whose declared relations are constitutive of the object claimed.

The definition has four necessary elements.

**Multiplicity.** A composite contains more than one identifiable constituent. Constituents may include operator occurrences and, once the later object categories are defined, configurations, event-like objects, non-event structures, transitions, and previously warranted composites. A single occurrence does not become a composite merely because its description is detailed or supports several operator typings.

**Constitutive relation.** The constituents must be related in a way that explains why they count as one analytical object for the stated claim. Mere co-presence, textual adjacency, or inclusion in the same dataset is insufficient. A constitutive relation may concern sequence, recurrence, mutual constraint, hierarchy, branching, role distribution, dependency, coordinated function, or another source-supported connection. Chapters 3, 4, and the operational Parts later specify particular relation and formation burdens.

**Declared boundary.** The analysis must state which constituents belong to the composite and which do not. Every composite is selective. Its boundary is shaped by the analytical question and sources; it is not a claim to have captured a complete whole.

**Internal traceability.** The composite claim must preserve enough visibility of its constituents and relations that the claimed higher-level object can be reconstructed and challenged. A label that erases internal variation, conflict, or contrary material does not establish a composite.

The control distinction is:

```text
multiple items mentioned together
≠
composite structure

multiple identifiable constituents
+
declared constitutive relation
+
bounded composite claim
→
composite candidate
```

Chapter 1 defines the object category. It does not yet define the `COMPOSE` operation that forms and tests a composite. That signature belongs to Chapter 4, and the full PATH procedure belongs to Chapter 15. A claimed composite in Chapter 1 is therefore an object-model example, not an operation record and not a presumption of admissibility.

Composite structure must also remain distinct from three neighboring uses of composition.

```text
PMS operator composition
≠
Σ Integration
≠
STRATA COMPOSE
≠
composite structure as analytical object
```

PMS operator composition concerns formal or generative relations among operator types in the Base theory. `Σ` Integration is one operator type within Δ–Ψ. `COMPOSE` is the STRATA operation that forms a declared composite from source structures. A composite structure is the analytical object claimed as source or result. None of these may be substituted for another.

#### Minimal composite continuation

The review arrangement can be widened into a candidate composite only by identifying constituents and relations. Suppose the declared source bundle supports the following:

- one agenda-and-admission occurrence typed as Frame;
- one approval-allocation occurrence typed as Asymmetry;
- one recurrent routing pattern typed as Attractor;
- a relation in which the agenda channels proposals into the approval gate, while repeated routing stabilizes the arrangement across review sessions.

The proposed composite is not the list `Frame + Asymmetry + Attractor`. It is the bounded **review-governance arrangement** formed by the three identifiable occurrences and the supported channeling, allocation, and recurrence relations among them.

```text
occurrence 1: agenda and admission boundary
occurrence 2: unequal approval allocation
occurrence 3: recurrent routing pattern
constitutive relations: channeling, allocation, recurrence
bounded composite claim: review-governance arrangement
```

The example remains heterogeneous. The Frame-typed occurrence need not absorb the Asymmetry-typed or Attractor-typed occurrences. No dominant operator is required. The composite may perform a stable analytical function while its internal constituents remain different, differently weighted, or partially conflicting.

```text
stable composite function
≠
internal homogeneity
≠
single dominant operator
```

Nor does the composite become a new PMS primitive. Its identity depends on the source occurrences, the selected boundary, and the constitutive relations. If those relations are not supported, the composite claim must be reduced or failed. If the sources preserve several incompatible formation rules without warrant for choosing among them, the result may remain provisional. If the analysis can no longer trace the higher-level label to the constituents that carry it, continuation must stop at the applicable boundary.

The object category also clarifies what may later be decomposed. A composite may be reopened into its source-supported constituents and relations under finer granularity. That operation does not decompose the operator types through which the constituents were typed. It reconstructs the composite object.

#### Boundary and handoff of the core architecture

The core type architecture established in Sections 1.1–1.4 fixes the following claims:

- operator sign, operator name, and operator type remain distinct;
- PMS Base alone governs the canonical Δ–Ψ types, signs, names, functions, order, and dependencies;
- STRATA operations do not decompose or transform operator types;
- an operator occurrence is a concrete, reference-bound, context- or frame-bound, source-bound, and claim-bound typing;
- occurrence typings may be multiple, rival, provisional, reduced, or withdrawn without revising PMS Base;
- an occurrence is not a global property of a person;
- a composite requires multiple identifiable constituents and declared constitutive relations;
- a composite may remain internally heterogeneous and need not reduce to one dominant operator;
- a composite is not a list, an exhaustive whole, a PMS operator composition, `Σ` Integration, or a new PMS primitive.

Several questions remain intentionally open. Sections 1.5–1.8 define configuration, event-like object, non-event structure, and transition as object. Later sections define derived analytical objects and minimal object identification. Chapter 2 owns frame, granularity, relative level, and scope coordinates. Chapter 3 owns the stronger temporal grammar. Chapter 4 owns the three operation signatures. Chapter 5 owns the full continuity architecture. Chapter 6 owns admissibility. Chapter 7 owns the shared Transformation Record.

The present distinctions are therefore sufficient to continue the object model, but not to provisionally lock Chapter 1 as a whole.

### 1.5 Configuration

A **configuration** is a temporally located, operator-structured constellation formed relative to a declared analytical frame.

The term identifies an analytical object, not a complete description of everything that exists at a time. A configuration is constructed by selecting the structures that are relevant to a bounded praxis claim and by stating how those structures stand in relation to one another within the reconstruction. It may include operator occurrences, roles, frames, asymmetries, bindings, action corridors, event-like changes, non-event structures, and unresolved residue. It need not include every fact, actor, material condition, or possible interpretation associated with the wider domain.

The object category carries five minimum burdens.

**Temporal location.** The configuration must be located within a bounded analytical interval or position. Temporal location need not be punctual. A configuration may characterize a meeting, a review cycle, a phase of a negotiation, or another bounded duration. Chapter 3 later defines the stronger temporal grammar and the relation between configuration and state.

**Frame relativity.** The configuration must be formed relative to a stated analytical frame. This does not yet define the coordinate `frame`; Chapter 2 owns that definition. It means only that relevance is selected rather than universal. The same reference domain may support different configurations when different questions, sources, or bounded contexts are declared.

**Operator structure.** The configuration must identify the occurrences and relations that carry the claimed praxis structure. Merely stating that several operators are “present” is insufficient. The analysis must indicate which concrete structures are typed through those operators and how they constrain, enable, expose, delay, stabilize, or bind praxis within the configuration.

**Praxis relevance.** Included features must make a difference to the reconstruction of action, expectation, cost, exposure, role, access, constraint, or another praxeologically relevant dimension. A detail does not enter the configuration merely because it is observable.

**Selective incompleteness.** The configuration must remain explicitly partial. Its boundary states what the analysis includes for the present claim. It does not convert that selection into an exhaustive world model.

The control distinction is:

```text
configuration
=
bounded reconstruction of a temporally located praxis constellation

configuration
≠
complete world description
≠
static ontology
≠
event
```

A configuration may contain both realized and non-realized structures. Positive occurrences, relevant changes, delays, suspensions, and unresolved alternatives may all help constitute it. Their inclusion does not make the configuration a mere inventory. The analytical object lies in the selected constellation and the supported relations among its elements.

The category also remains distinct from **composite structure**. A configuration may be internally composite because it contains several occurrences and relations, but the two terms answer different questions. `Composite structure` identifies an object formed from multiple constituents under declared constitutive relations. `Configuration` identifies a temporally located praxis constellation. One object may satisfy both descriptions, but neither label automatically entails the other.

```text
configuration may be composite
≠
every composite is a configuration
```

A composite need not be temporally localized as a configuration; it may be a path, a distributed arrangement, or another derived object once later chapters define those categories. Conversely, a minimally reconstructed configuration may be treated as one analytical object without requiring the analyst to claim that all of its internal elements have already been independently established as constituents of a formal composition.

#### Minimal configuration continuation

Return to the bounded review-governance arrangement from Section 1.4. Suppose the sources support the following during one declared review cycle:

- an agenda rule limits which proposals may enter deliberation;
- final approval remains concentrated in one office;
- proposals are repeatedly routed through the same review channel;
- one promised reconsideration remains pending beyond the stated review window;
- participants retain the ability to submit objections, but delayed reconsideration increases the cost of doing so.

The analytical configuration is not “everything occurring in the organization.” It is the temporally located constellation of the agenda boundary, approval asymmetry, recurrent routing, pending reconsideration, and altered objection corridor during that review cycle.

```text
reference domain:
review-governance arrangement

bounded temporal location:
one declared review cycle

selected operator-structured elements:
Frame-typed agenda boundary
Asymmetry-typed approval allocation
Attractor-typed recurrent routing
candidate non-event structure around reconsideration
changed objection corridor

configuration claim:
review-cycle governance configuration
```

The result is bounded. The configuration does not establish a complete account of the institution, the motives of participants, or the future persistence of the arrangement. It does not establish that every selected element is equally important. It does not authorize a person-level inference. It does not yet establish a transition, path, trajectory, or path dependence.

A locally mapped result may be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The classification applies only to the object-model claim that these source-supported elements form a relevant configuration for the stated review cycle. Stronger temporal or causal claims require later burdens.

#### Configuration pressure: multiple valid selections

Because a configuration is selective, the same reference domain may support more than one valid configuration. One reconstruction may select the agenda boundary, approval allocation, and objection corridor because the question concerns access to review. Another may select recurrence, delay, and binding because the question concerns stabilization over time.

The plurality does not by itself mean that the two configurations contradict each other. Nor does it mean that every selection is equally warranted. Each configuration must remain answerable to its frame, sources, temporal location, and claim. Chapter 2 later supplies the coordinate distinctions needed to compare such reconstructions without treating frame, granularity, and relative level as interchangeable.

```text
multiple bounded configurations
≠
complete relativism

one selected configuration
≠
exclusive world description
```

The object model therefore permits plural reconstructions while preserving source and claim discipline.

### 1.6 Event-Like Object

An **event-like object** is a positively marked structural change treated as an analytical unit within a declared frame. It may be punctual or temporally extended, internally simple or composite, and causally entangled with other structures. The category identifies eligibility as an object of analysis; it does not yet supply the full temporal specification of an `event`, which belongs to Chapter 3.

“Positively marked” means **realized rather than non-realized**. It does not mean beneficial, desirable, morally positive, or normatively approved.

```text
positive realization
≠
positive evaluation
```

The category is needed because STRATA may later compose, decompose, or project a realized structural change without assuming that the change is a punctual causal atom. A policy may be introduced through several steps. A role allocation may shift across an extended review cycle. An access rule may be revised through a distributed set of decisions. The analytical unit can remain event-like when the sources support one bounded change object and its internal duration or composition is disclosed.

An event-like object carries at least four burdens.

**Realized change.** Some relevant structure must be supported as having changed, begun, ended, been instituted, been withdrawn, or otherwise become positively realized within the reconstruction. Mere expectation, possibility, intention, rumor, or missing information is insufficient.

**Reference and boundary.** The analysis must state what change is being treated as one object and where its boundary lies. An extended change cannot be called one event-like object merely because its parts occurred near each other in time.

**Structural relevance.** The realized change must alter a praxeologically relevant relation, role, expectation, cost, exposure, access condition, binding, or action corridor. Not every recorded occurrence warrants event-like status for every analytical question.

**Internal disclosure.** Where the object is extended or composite, the reconstruction must preserve enough of its internal constituents and relations to prevent a compressed label from concealing contrary or discontinuous material.

The category blocks several common collapses.

```text
event-like object
≠
causal atom
≠
necessarily punctual occurrence
≠
self-explanatory turning point
≠
normatively positive event
```

An event-like object may contribute to a transition, but it is not identical with a transition. It may alter one configuration, help connect two configurations, or later become a constituent of a path. None of those stronger relations follows automatically from the object label.

```text
event-like object
may contribute to
transition

but

event-like object
≠
transition
```

The category also differs from an operator occurrence. An event-like object is typed by the kind of temporal object it is claimed to be: a realized structural change. Within that object, one or more concrete structures may be typed as Frame, Asymmetry, Recontextualization, or other operator occurrences. One event-like object may therefore contain several occurrence typings without becoming a new operator type.

#### Minimal event-like continuation

Suppose the review arrangement is changed so that final approval no longer belongs exclusively to one office. The source bundle documents a revised authorization rule, the date on which it becomes effective, and the redistributed approval roles used in the next review cycle.

The realized change can be treated as one event-like object:

```text
reference object:
revision of the approval arrangement

positive realization:
new authorization rule enters into effect

structural relevance:
approval capacity is redistributed

bounded event-like claim:
reallocation of review authority
```

The object need not be punctual. Drafting, ratification, publication, and first implementation may occur over an interval. If the analysis treats them as one event-like object, it must state the formation relation that unifies them and preserve any internal differences relevant to the claim.

The event-like classification does not establish a complete causal explanation of the revision. It does not prove that the change produced every later consequence. It does not establish a transition by itself. It does not make the revision a macro-event or trajectory. A locally mapped result may therefore be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The bounded claim is that a realized, source-supported structural change is eligible as one event-like analytical object.

#### Event inflation counterpressure

Suppose a record shows that a routine review meeting occurred, but the sources do not support any relevant change in roles, rules, expectations, costs, asymmetries, bindings, or action corridors. Calling the meeting an event-like object may still be possible for another question, but it is not warranted merely because a dated occurrence exists.

```text
dated occurrence
≠
relevant structural change
```

Where the event-like label adds no praxeological purchase, the claim must be reduced. Where the record is too incomplete to establish whether any relevant change occurred, the analysis may need to stop rather than inflate chronology into structure.

This boundary prevents event language from becoming a decorative synonym for “something happened.”

### 1.7 Non-Event Structure

A **non-event structure** is a frame- and source-bound structured non-realization, delay, suspension, or blockage whose expectation relation and praxeological load are supported strongly enough for the non-realization to be treated as an analytical object.

The category depends on, but does not replace, the PMS Base Non-Event operator type `Λ`. PMS Base defines `Λ` as structured absence: meaningful failure or delay of an expected occurrence within a frame. Chapter 1 determines when a concrete structured non-realization is eligible as an object. Chapter 3 later defines the fuller temporal category `non-event`, and PATH later operationalizes its role in path reconstruction.

The distinctions are:

```text
Λ Non-Event operator type
≠
concrete Λ-typed occurrence
≠
non-event structure as analytical object category
≠
every absence
```

A supported non-event structure will ordinarily include one or more concrete structures typed through `Λ`, but the object category does not alter the operator type and does not make every source gap a Λ occurrence.

Four burdens are indispensable.

**Identifiable expected occurrence.** The analysis must state what was expected to occur. The expected object cannot remain a vague retrospective possibility invented only after the absence is observed.

**Supported expectation relation.** The expectation must be grounded in a promise, rule, deadline, role obligation, established procedure, recurrent pattern, declared plan, or another source-supported relation. Analyst preference is not an expectation frame.

**Relevant interval or condition.** The reconstruction must state the window, condition, or bounded context within which realization was expected. Without such a boundary, delay and non-realization cannot be distinguished from an event that was never due.

**Praxeological load.** The non-realization must change or maintain something relevant: costs, exposure, uncertainty, role allocation, action corridors, dependencies, asymmetries, bindings, or the availability of alternatives. Mere absence without such load does not warrant a structured non-event claim.

The minimum logic is:

```text
supported expectation
+
bounded realization condition
+
source-supported non-realization
+
praxeological load
→
non-event structure candidate
```

Missing information fails this burden.

```text
no record
≠
recorded non-occurrence

unknown event
≠
established non-event structure
```

A source archive may omit a decision record. That omission establishes a source gap. It does not establish that the decision failed to occur. The decision may have occurred without surviving documentation, may have occurred outside the inspected source set, may never have been expected, or may remain unresolved. The correct claim must track what the sources support.

A non-event structure can also contain positive events. Reminders may be sent, meetings may be held, postponements may be announced, partial measures may be taken, or repeated deferrals may be recorded. These positive occurrences do not automatically eliminate the non-event if the expected occurrence remains unrealized and continues to carry load.

```text
positive sub-events
≠
elimination of the non-event structure
```

This distinction is essential for later decomposition. Opening a non-event structure into reminders, delays, blockages, distributed non-decisions, or partial acts must preserve the reference to the expected but unrealized occurrence. If finer reconstruction redescribes every positive sub-event while losing the governing non-realization, it has changed the object rather than decomposed it.

#### Positive non-event case: promised reconsideration

In the review configuration, suppose a written decision promises reconsideration within thirty days after a documented objection. The objection is submitted. Several reminders and one scheduling exchange occur, but no reconsideration decision is made within the stated window. During the delay, the original approval remains effective and the objector bears additional procedural cost.

The sources support:

```text
expected occurrence:
reconsideration decision

expectation basis:
written thirty-day commitment

bounded condition:
thirty days after documented objection

non-realization:
no reconsideration decision within the window

praxeological load:
continued effect of the original approval
additional procedural cost
restricted alternatives during delay
```

The object may be treated as a non-event structure. The reminders and scheduling exchange are positive sub-events inside that structure; they do not substitute for the promised reconsideration.

A locally mapped result may be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The result does not establish motive, bad faith, causal exclusivity, or permanent blockage. It establishes only the bounded structured non-realization supported by the expectation relation, interval, sources, and load.

#### Missing-information countercase

Now remove the written commitment, deadline, objection record, and evidence of continued procedural effect. Suppose only that the archive contains no reconsideration decision.

The absence of a record does not support the earlier non-event structure.

```text
archive gap:
no reconsideration decision located

unsupported additions:
that reconsideration was due
that it failed to occur
that the failure changed action corridors
that delay was intentional
```

The non-event claim must be withdrawn or reduced to a source-gap claim. The local mapped result is:

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

If the analytical task requires a non-event determination and no additional source can establish the expectation relation or non-realization, continuation on that claim must stop. Stop here is not analytical failure in the pejorative sense. It is the correct protection against converting missing information into structured absence.

#### Non-event boundary: absence, expectation, and invention

Not every silence, omission, vacancy, interruption, or unrealized possibility is a non-event structure. The analyst must not infer an expectation solely from the fact that an alternative can be imagined. Counterfactual relevance requires historical or structural support for the expected occurrence or available alternative.

```text
conceivable occurrence
≠
warranted expected occurrence

analyst surprise
≠
source-supported expectation
```

The object category is therefore counterfactually sensitive but not counterfactually unconstrained.

### 1.8 Transition as Object

A **transition as object** is a relation-bearing analytical object connecting identifiable configurations through relevant order, realized changes, non-events, and changed praxis conditions. It is more than the difference between two descriptions. It is eligible as a distinct object only when the relation between the configurations is itself supported and analytically relevant.

Chapter 1 defines this object-category eligibility. Chapter 3 owns the stronger temporal definition of `transition`, its relation to state and configuration, and its place in the burden-increasing chain from configuration to sequence, path, and trajectory. Chapter 9 later supplies a PATH validity procedure. WP2 therefore states only the minimum object burden.

A transition-as-object claim requires at least five elements.

**Identifiable configurations.** The reconstruction must distinguish the relevant earlier and later configurations. The objects need not be complete, but they must be sufficiently bounded for the claimed relation to be evaluated.

**Supported temporal order.** The analysis must establish that the relevant structures belong to an ordered relation. Merely placing two descriptions side by side does not establish that one configuration followed or developed from the other in the claimed way.

**Intervening structure.** At least one source-supported event-like object, non-event structure, or other relevant change relation must explain how the configurations are connected. The burden is not necessarily a complete causal account, but the transition cannot be an empty arrow between endpoints.

**Changed praxis conditions.** The relation must preserve what changed in action corridors, costs, exposure, roles, asymmetries, expectations, bindings, accessibility, or another relevant dimension. Endpoint difference without praxeological difference does not establish a transition claim.

**Declared transition boundary.** The analysis must state which changes and non-realizations belong to the transition object and which are excluded. A long historical interval does not become one transition merely because it has a beginning and an end.

The control distinction is:

```text
earlier configuration
+
later configuration
+
endpoint difference
≠
transition as object

identifiable configurations
+
supported order
+
intervening realized and non-realized structure
+
changed praxis conditions
+
bounded relation
→
transition candidate
```

A transition may contain several event-like objects and non-event structures. It may be extended, internally composite, contested, or only partially reconstructible. It need not reduce to one causal event or one dominant operator. Its internal heterogeneity must remain visible where it matters to the claim.

```text
transition
≠
single event
≠
causal atom
≠
simple state difference
```

The transition object also does not automatically establish a sequence, path, trajectory, sedimentation, irreversibility, or path dependence. Those are burden-increasing later claims.

```text
transition candidate
≠
path
≠
trajectory
≠
path dependence
```

#### Minimal transition continuation

Consider two review-cycle configurations.

**Earlier configuration:** final approval is concentrated in one office; objections may be submitted; promised reconsideration is pending; the original approval remains effective during the delay.

**Later configuration:** a revised authorization rule redistributes approval capacity; the reconsideration procedure is replaced by a joint review step; objection access remains available but now operates through a different channel.

The source bundle also supports:

- the event-like reallocation of review authority;
- the non-event structure of the unrealized reconsideration under the earlier rule;
- an implementation interval in which the old approval remains effective while the new rule is prepared;
- changed costs and role capacities after the new rule enters into effect.

The transition object is not merely the contrast “centralized before, distributed after.” It is the bounded relation connecting the two configurations through the authority-reallocation event, the unresolved reconsideration, the implementation interval, and the altered objection corridor.

```text
earlier configuration:
centralized approval + pending reconsideration

intervening structure:
unrealized reconsideration
rule revision
implementation interval

later configuration:
redistributed approval + joint review channel

praxis change:
roles, costs, access conditions, and exposure are altered

transition claim:
reconfiguration of review authority and objection procedure
```

At the Chapter 1 level, the object-category classification may receive:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The bounded result means that the sources support a relation-bearing transition object. It does not yet certify the full temporal chain, causal sufficiency, path formation, trajectory, irreversibility, or historical determination. Those claims remain open to Chapters 3, 9, and later PATH chapters.

#### Endpoint-difference counterpressure

Suppose two undated summaries describe different approval arrangements, but the sources do not establish their order, whether they concern the same reference arrangement, what occurred between them, or how praxis conditions changed.

The analyst may report a difference between descriptions. The analyst may not yet claim a transition.

```text
description A differs from description B
≠
A transitioned into B
```

The local result is:

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

If the same-reference relation or temporal order cannot be established, the transition claim may require `mandatory_stop`. A new frame, granularity, relative level, or target function would create a new testable claim; it would not repair the unsupported transition by relabeling it.

#### Event, non-event, configuration, and transition

WP2 fixes the following object-category relations:

| Category | Minimum object claim | Does not establish |
| --- | --- | --- |
| configuration | bounded temporally located praxis constellation | complete world description, event, transition, path |
| event-like object | positively realized relevant structural change treated as one analytical unit | causal atom, full event grammar, transition, macro-event |
| non-event structure | supported structured non-realization with expectation relation, bounded condition, and praxis load | every absence, unknown event, missing information, motive |
| transition as object | supported relation connecting configurations through relevant realized and non-realized structure and changed praxis conditions | simple endpoint difference, sequence, path, trajectory, path dependence |

These categories are neither mutually reducible nor mutually exclusive in every case. A configuration may be composite. An event-like object may be internally composite. A non-event structure may contain positive sub-events. A transition may contain event-like objects and non-event structures. The analysis must state which category carries the present claim and which stronger categories remain unestablished.

```text
category relation
≠
category collapse
```

#### Boundary and handoff of the extended object categories

Sections 1.5–1.8 establish the following claims:

- a configuration is a temporally located, operator-structured, selectively incomplete praxis constellation relative to a declared analytical frame;
- a configuration is neither a complete world description nor a static ontology;
- an event-like object is a positively realized structural change and need not be punctual, causally isolated, or internally simple;
- positive realization carries no normative valence;
- a non-event structure requires a supported expected occurrence, bounded realization condition, source-supported non-realization, and praxeological load;
- missing information, an unknown event, or an absent record cannot establish a non-event structure;
- positive sub-events may occur inside a non-event structure without eliminating the governing non-realization;
- a transition as object requires identifiable configurations, supported order, intervening structure, changed praxis conditions, and a declared relation boundary;
- endpoint difference alone cannot establish a transition;
- none of these categories automatically establishes sequence, path, trajectory, sedimentation, irreversibility, path dependence, or causal completeness.

The chapter remains incomplete. WP3 must define derived analytical objects, minimal object identity across transformation, nominal-sameness failure, historical-reference pressure, and the remaining Stop or Non-Capture-capable boundary. Chapter 2 still owns analytical coordinates. Chapter 3 still owns the full temporal object chain. Chapters 4–7 still own operation signatures, continuity architecture, admissibility, and records.

The WP2 result is therefore:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality attaches to the incomplete Chapter 1 production state, not to the four definitions just established. No Chapter 1 provisional lock is claimed.

### 1.9 Derived Analytical Objects and Functions

A **derived analytical object** is an analytical object whose formation depends on one or more declared source objects, a declared STRATA operation or operation chain, and a bounded formation rule. A **derived analytical function** is a bounded contextual function attributed to an origin-typed object through a declared transformation relation. In both cases, `derived` marks traceable analytical formation. It does not mark a new PMS primitive, an ontologically higher entity, a weaker reality claim, or an automatic increase in authority.

The canonical distinction is:

```text
derived analytical object or function
≠
new PMS primitive
```

The distinction is required because STRATA must be able to form objects that are not present as primitive entries in the Δ–Ψ grammar. Paths, trajectories, macro-events, frame-functions, attractor-functions, modulating profiles, and higher-level composite functions may all become legitimate analytical results. Their legitimacy does not arise from adding them to PMS Base. It arises from a warranted derivation whose source, formation, loss, scope, and limits remain inspectable.

```text
new analytical object
≠
new base operator

higher-level function
≠
higher authority
```

#### Object, function, and shorthand

Not every useful label is a derived analytical object. Three different constructions must remain separate.

**Analytical shorthand** compresses an already identified object for convenience. It may introduce a shorter name, but it does not by itself establish a new object or function.

**Derived analytical object** forms a bounded object claim from source structures and constitutive relations. The result may be a composite, a temporally extended object, or another source-dependent reconstruction.

**Derived analytical function** states what an origin-typed object may do within a declared target context. The function remains contextual and does not replace the origin type.

```text
convenient label
≠
derived object

repeated description
≠
formation rule

derived function
≠
operator identity
```

The same expression may be used informally in several ways. The analyst must state which claim is being made. Calling a cluster of decisions “the authorization episode,” for example, may be mere shorthand. Treating that cluster as one macro-event requires a bounded formation claim. Treating it as a frame-function or attractor-function requires a separate contextual function claim. The name alone does not decide among these possibilities.

#### Minimum derivation burden

A derived-object or derived-function claim must disclose at least:

1. **source objects** — the occurrences, configurations, event-like objects, non-event structures, transitions, composites, or prior derived objects from which the result is formed;
2. **declared formation relation** — the relevant STRATA operation or operation chain, without inventing a fourth operation;
3. **selection and constitutive load** — which source structures are included and which relations make the result one bounded object or function rather than a list;
4. **analytical coordinates** — the frame, granularity, relative level, temporal scope, source scope, and claim scope relevant to the derivation;
5. **source trace** — how the result depends on the selected source structures and how a relevant source change would affect it;
6. **loss declaration** — what is preserved, compressed, excluded, uncertain, and irrecoverable;
7. **non-primitive status** — an explicit statement that the result does not extend or revise Δ–Ψ;
8. **stop condition** — the condition under which continuation of the derivation would become untraceable, type-confused, or praxeologically empty.

Chapter 1 fixes this minimum category burden. Chapters 4–7 own the operation signatures, continuity architecture, admissibility tests, and record fields needed to evaluate a concrete derivation. PATH, SUB, and RETYPE later specify operation-local procedures and stronger object-specific burdens.

The minimum relation is:

```text
identified source object or objects
+
declared operation or chain
+
formation rule
+
constitutive source trace
+
bounded coordinates and loss
→
derived-object or derived-function candidate
```

A candidate is not yet an admissible result merely because the derivation is described. It remains subject to PraxisPurchase, TraceableLoad, TypeIntegrity, continuity, contextual boundedness, counterfactual sensitivity, source and claim ceilings, Stop, and Non-Capture.

#### Derivation does not erase source identity

A derived result may possess an analytical unity not present in any single source occurrence. That unity does not erase the identity or heterogeneity of its sources. A macro-event may contain several event-like objects and non-event structures. A path may contain several transitions. A modulating profile may depend on several differently typed occurrences. A higher-level composite function may depend on conflicting components.

```text
analytical unity
≠
source homogeneity

formation
≠
retroactive type replacement
```

The source objects retain their own origin types and local identity claims. If the derived result is later decomposed, the reconstruction must return to identifiable source structures or disclose where compression has become irrecoverable. If the result is projected into a target function, the projection does not convert its sources into that function.

The term `derived` therefore carries a traceability obligation. It is not an invitation to treat the result as self-standing once formed.

#### Bounded macro-event candidate

Consider the review-cycle material developed in Sections 1.5–1.8. The source reconstruction contains:

- a promised reconsideration that did not occur within the declared period;
- continuing effectiveness of the original approval during the delay;
- a later reallocation of review authority;
- replacement of the reconsideration procedure by a joint review step;
- changed objection costs and access conditions.

An analyst may wish to treat these structures together as a **review-authority reconfiguration macro-event**. At the Chapter 1 level, that label is not enough. The following is a prose-level formation declaration, not a record schema. The proposed object must state:

```text
source objects:
non-event structure of unrealized reconsideration
+
event-like reallocation of authority
+
implementation interval
+
changed objection corridor

formation claim:
these structures jointly constitute one bounded reconfiguration object

preserved:
order, non-realization, authority change, access change

compressed:
fine-grained internal decisions and local disputes

excluded:
unrelated review cycles and later implementation effects

non-primitive status:
macro-event candidate, not a PMS operator
```

If later COMPOSE conditions, source support, and admissibility tests are satisfied, the result may become an admissible derived analytical object. Chapter 1 does not yet certify that operation. It establishes only what kind of object is being proposed and what must not be confused with that proposal.

A local category-level result may therefore be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The bounded claim is that the material can be identified as a candidate derived object with declared sources and formation burden. It does not yet establish a valid COMPOSE result, a PATH object, causal completeness, or empirical truth beyond the illustration.

#### Derivation failure and claim reduction

Suppose an analyst groups every dispute, delay, decision, and later effect from a ten-year period under the label “the governance trajectory,” but does not declare the source selection, constitutive relations, temporal boundary, excluded alternatives, or loss. The label may be suggestive, but it does not establish a derived trajectory object.

```text
large interval
+
many events
+
one retrospective label
≠
derived analytical object
```

The claim must be reduced to a descriptive grouping or analytical shorthand until the stronger formation burden is met. The local result is:

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

If no bounded formation rule can be supplied without erasing decisive conflict or inventing relations unsupported by the sources, continuation toward the stronger derived-object claim must stop.

### 1.10 Minimal Object Identity across Transformation

**Object identity** is a bounded and defeasible claim that an analytical description before, during, or after transformation continues to concern the same historical or structural reference object. Identity is not a self-evident property supplied by a name, a database identifier, a formal variable, or the analyst's intention. It must be supported relative to the transformation being claimed.

```text
object identity
=
bounded identification claim

object identity
≠
nominal sameness
≠
complete invariance
≠
immutable typing
```

Chapter 1 defines the minimum dimensions that make an identity claim intelligible.
