Chapter 5 later defines the full identity and continuity discipline required across `PROJECT_AS`, including origin type, target function, constitutive source trace, functional continuity, temporal continuity, and contextual boundedness. Chapter 7 later records those claims. Chapter 1 does not replace either chapter.

#### Minimum identity dimensions

No single criterion guarantees object identity in every case. A minimally assessable identity claim considers the following dimensions.

**Reference.** What historical or structural object is claimed to continue? The reference must be bounded enough that continuation and discontinuity can be tested. A broad label such as “the institution,” “the process,” or “the conflict” is insufficient when several possible referents exist.

**Supported typing.** What operator occurrence typing, object category, or origin typing is carried into the transformation claim? Typing may remain contested or revisable. Identity does not require immutable occurrence typing, but a type change may not be hidden as though nothing changed.

**Constitutive relations.** Which relations make the object the object relevant to the claim? Membership, mandate, dependency, temporal order, role distribution, procedural linkage, source composition, or another relation may be constitutive. Incidental similarity is not enough.

**Function where relevant.** Which source function or bounded role matters to continuation? Function may persist, be revised, be partially preserved, or cease. A functional change does not automatically destroy historical reference, but it may defeat an identity claim whose object was defined by that function.

**Temporal or historical continuity where relevant.** What source-supported relation connects the earlier and later descriptions? Continuity may be carried by succession, transfer, reconstitution, retained records, ongoing obligations, preserved membership, or another documented bridge. Mere chronological adjacency is insufficient.

**Declared boundary and uncertainty.** Which changes are compatible with the present identity claim, which would defeat it, and which remain uncertain? Identity must remain open to reduction, revision, Stop, or Non-Capture.

These dimensions are not a universal score. They do not permit one strong dimension to compensate automatically for failures in all others.

```text
same name
+
same location
+
similar function
≠
automatic identity
```

Nor does identity require every dimension to remain unchanged.

```text
changed name
or
revised function
or
altered membership
≠
automatic discontinuity
```

The burden is relational: which continuities and discontinuities matter for the object and transformation claim at hand?

#### Identity is claim-relative, not arbitrary

The same historical material may support different bounded identity claims at different analytical coordinates. A committee may remain the same legal body while no longer being the same decision configuration. A procedure may retain historical reference while performing a revised function. A recurring label may denote distinct organizations in different periods. A composite may retain its reference while several components are replaced, provided the constitutive relation relevant to the claim remains supported.

This relativity does not make identity optional or arbitrary. Each claim must declare:

- the reference object;
- the transformation or comparison under review;
- the constitutive dimensions treated as load-bearing;
- the changes treated as compatible or incompatible with continuation;
- the source basis;
- the relevant uncertainty and loss;
- the claim ceiling.

A new frame, granularity, relative level, or target function creates a new identity question. It does not retroactively repair an identity claim that already failed under its original conditions.

```text
new analytical position
=
new testable identity claim

new analytical position
≠
erasure of prior identity failure
```

#### Historical reference with revised function

Return to the review body from the running example. Suppose the sources establish all of the following:

- the later body is created by amendment of the earlier charter rather than repeal and replacement;
- case files, outstanding obligations, and pending reviews transfer continuously;
- a majority of members and the administrative office remain in place;
- the body's final-approval function is replaced by a joint-review function;
- the new procedure changes role distribution and objection costs.

The historical reference may remain preserved even though the function and configuration have changed.

```text
historical reference:
preserved through documented charter amendment, obligations, records, and institutional succession

source function:
revised from final approval to joint review

configuration:
changed

identity claim:
same historical review body under a materially revised function and configuration
```

At the Chapter 1 level, this may support:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The boundedness is essential. The result does not claim that every feature remains identical, that the former function survives unchanged, or that the new body inherits additional authority from historical continuity. It establishes only the supported reference relation needed for the present object claim.

This case fixes a central distinction:

```text
historical reference continuity
≠
functional invariance
```

Chapter 5 later determines whether a specific projection preserves the required origin type and functional continuity. Chapter 1 only establishes that revised function and preserved historical reference can coexist when the source bridge is adequate.

#### Nominal-sameness countercase

Suppose two bodies are both called the **Review Council**. The earlier body operates under Charter A, has one membership rule, possesses final approval authority, and is dissolved at the end of the period. Two years later, Charter B creates a body with the same name, a different jurisdiction, different appointment rules, no transferred obligations, and no documented succession relation.

The repeated label does not preserve reference identity.

```text
Review Council under Charter A
≠
Review Council under Charter B
```

Even if the bodies occupy the same office and perform broadly similar work, the identity claim cannot rest on nominal continuity alone. The stronger claim “the same council continued” requires reduction.

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

A weaker relation may still be admissible: the later body may be a successor, replacement, imitation, or functionally similar organization. Those are distinct claims and require their own support.

The reverse pressure also matters. If the earlier body changes its public name while its charter, obligations, records, membership continuity, and mandate remain documented, nominal difference does not by itself establish a new reference object.

```text
same name
≠
same object

changed name
≠
new object
```

#### Constitutive change and identity limits

Object identity may fail even when some historical continuity exists. If a transformation removes or replaces the relation that made the source object relevant to the claim, the analyst may not preserve identity merely by appealing to institutional lineage.

For example, suppose the object under study is specifically **the final-approval body**. If final approval is abolished and replaced by nonbinding consultation, the historical institution may continue while the function-defined object does not.

```text
same historical institution
+
loss of constitutive final-approval relation
→
historical reference may persist
but
function-defined object identity may fail
```

This is not contradiction. It shows that identity claims must name their reference and constitutive burden. “The same body” and “the same approval object” may receive different results.

Where the surviving relation is adequate only for part of the claim, the appropriate result may be:

```text
partially_admissible
```

or:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The chosen Output Class depends on whether a separable component remains supported or whether the entire claim must be narrowed. Chapter 6 later owns the full admissibility mapping.

#### Mandatory Stop and Non-Capture under unresolved succession

Consider a more difficult archive. An earlier **Coordination Council** is formally dissolved. A later body uses the same name and occupies the same office. Some staff and records transfer, but the new charter states that a new legal body is created. Minutes refer inconsistently to “continuation,” “replacement,” and “reconstitution.” The available sources do not establish whether outstanding obligations transferred, whether membership continuity was constitutive, or whether the new body inherited the earlier mandate.

Two rival identity claims remain plausible:

```text
rival A:
the later council is the same historical body under reconstitution

rival B:
the later council is a new body that inherited selected records, staff, and symbolic continuity
```

The analyst may not choose rival A because the name and office continue. The analyst may not choose rival B merely because a new charter exists. Each stronger identity claim exceeds the current source ceiling.

For the claim “the same council continued,” continuation must receive:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

The Stop attaches to that claim under the present sources. It does not prohibit all analysis. A weaker succession claim may remain available:

```text
there is a documented relation of institutional succession, transfer, or reconstitution,
while exact reference identity remains unresolved
```

If the task requires a binary classification—same object or different object—and neither option can retain the mixed continuity and discontinuity without distortion, the appropriate result is:

```text
non_capture
```

Non-Capture does not protect an unsupported identity assertion. It records:

- the captured structure: shared name, office, partial staff, and transferred records;
- the uncaptured structure: exact legal, mandate, obligation, and constitutive continuity;
- the limiting condition: conflicting and incomplete succession evidence;
- the rivals: continuation versus replacement with inheritance;
- the re-entry condition: additional charter, transfer, obligation, or membership evidence capable of discriminating the rivals.

```text
non_capture
≠
missing-information excuse
≠
permission to retain the stronger claim
```

The stronger identity claim remains stopped. Non-Capture records why the available binary object categories or source state cannot retain the mixed structure without false closure.

### 1.11 Foundational Object Distinctions and Chapter Integration

Chapter 1 now supplies the object architecture on which later STRATA operations depend. The architecture is not a hierarchy of ontological levels. It is a set of distinguishable analytical categories and identity burdens.

#### Integrated object map

| Category | Minimum Chapter 1 claim | Central prohibition | Later owner |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| operator sign | notation that denotes a canonical operator type | sign treated as empirical structure | PMS Base; Chapter 1 notation control |
| operator name | canonical linguistic label of the operator type | name treated as the type itself | PMS Base; Chapter 1 notation control |
| operator type | abstract Δ–Ψ function and dependency position | decomposition, renaming, reordering, or empirical part claim | PMS Base |
| operator occurrence | concrete, frame- and source-bound structure typed by one or more operator types | occurrence collapsed into type or treated as immutable typing | Chapters 1 and 4–7 |
| composite structure | bounded object with multiple identifiable constituents and constitutive relations | list, multiple typing, or complete totality treated as composite | Chapters 1 and 15 |
| configuration | temporally located, operator-structured, selectively incomplete praxis constellation | complete world description or static ontology | Chapters 1–3 |
| event-like object | positively realized relevant structural change treated as one bounded unit | causal atom, normative value, or necessarily punctual event | Chapters 1 and 3 |
| non-event structure | supported structured non-realization with expectation, bounded condition, and praxis load | missing information, ordinary absence, or analyst-created expectation | Chapters 1, 3, and 14 |
| transition as object | supported bounded relation connecting configurations through intervening structure and changed praxis conditions | endpoint difference, path, trajectory, or causal completeness | Chapters 1, 3, and 9 |
| derived analytical object or function | source-dependent result of a declared operation or chain with formation, trace, loss, and non-primitive status | new PMS primitive, self-standing label, or authority upgrade | Chapter 1 and operation-specific Parts |
| object identity claim | bounded, defeasible claim of same reference object across declared change | nominal sameness, complete invariance, immutable typing, or automatic continuity | Chapters 1 and 5 |

#### Foundational non-equivalences

The chapter establishes the following non-equivalences:

```text
operator sign
≠
operator name
≠
operator type

operator type
≠
operator occurrence

operator occurrence
≠
composite structure

configuration
≠
event-like object

event-like object
≠
transition as object

transition as object
≠
path

path
≠
trajectory

derived analytical object or function
≠
new PMS primitive

object identity
≠
nominal sameness

historical reference continuity
≠
functional invariance

missing information
≠
non-event structure
```

These are non-equivalences, not declarations of non-relation. An occurrence may instantiate an operator type. A composite may contain occurrences. An event-like object may alter a configuration. A transition may contain events and non-events. A path may later be formed from transitions. A derived function may depend on a composite. Object identity may persist through declared change. What is prohibited is unmarked collapse.

#### Integrated running construction

The review-cycle illustration can now be represented without category confusion.

```text
PMS Base operator types:
Frame, Attractor, Asymmetry, Binding, and others remain abstract Δ–Ψ types

operator occurrences:
concrete framing of objection relevance
concrete approval asymmetry
concrete procedural binding

composite structure:
review arrangement formed by several occurrences and constitutive relations

configuration:
earlier review-cycle constellation with centralized approval and pending reconsideration

event-like object:
realized reallocation of review authority

non-event structure:
unrealized reconsideration under a supported thirty-day expectation

transition as object:
bounded reconfiguration linking earlier and later review arrangements

derived-object candidate:
review-authority reconfiguration macro-event formed from the event, non-event, interval, and changed corridor

identity claim:
historical review body may continue while its function and configuration change,
provided the source-supported succession relation is adequate
```

Each line adds a distinct analytical burden. None inherits truth or authority from its relative complexity. The integrated construction remains open to rival typing, claim reduction, partial admissibility, Stop, and Non-Capture.

#### Transformation eligibility boundary

Chapter 1 establishes which kinds of objects may become transformation sources. It does not yet authorize every operation on every object.

```text
abstract operator type
→ may be referenced as a typing source
→ may not be decomposed or transformed by STRATA

concrete occurrence or composite
→ may become a source candidate
→ operation-specific applicability remains to Chapters 4 and later Parts

configuration, event-like object, non-event structure, transition as object
→ may become bounded source candidates
→ stronger temporal and admissibility burdens remain later

derived analytical object or function
→ may become a later source candidate
→ derivation trace and non-primitive status must remain available
```

The object model therefore prevents two opposite errors. STRATA may not act on an undefined or category-confused entity. It also may not treat the abstract operator grammar as though it were an empirical object with material parts.

#### Identity boundary and handoff

Chapter 1 has introduced the dimensions required for a meaningful identity claim:

- bounded reference;
- supported typing or origin typing;
- constitutive relations;
- function where relevant;
- temporal or historical continuity where relevant;
- declared uncertainty, loss, and identity limits.

It has not yet defined:

- complete origin-type and target-function continuity under `PROJECT_AS`;
- operation-specific preservation burdens;
- the Shared Transformation Record identity fields;
- formal admissibility thresholds;
- empirical truth or causal identity;
- a universal identity score.

Those remain owned by Chapters 4–7. The object model supplies their input categories and prevents them from adding new objects by formal convenience. Chapter 3 retains the full temporal relations among configuration, event, non-event, transition, sequence, path, and trajectory; WP3 does not define or validate that stronger temporal chain.

#### WP3 completion boundary

Sections 1.9–1.11 establish that:

- derived objects and functions require declared source objects, a STRATA operation or chain, a formation rule, source trace, coordinates, loss, non-primitive status, and a stop condition;
- derivation does not erase source heterogeneity or retroactively change source types;
- analytical shorthand, derived object, and derived function remain separate claims;
- object identity is bounded, defeasible, claim-relative, and source-dependent;
- reference, supported typing, constitutive relations, function, historical continuity, and declared limits are minimum identity dimensions rather than a universal score;
- historical reference may persist through a revised function, while a function-defined object may fail even where institutional lineage continues;
- same name is insufficient for reference identity, and changed name is insufficient for discontinuity;
- prior identity failure is not repaired by changing frame, granularity, level, or target function;
- unresolved succession may require Mandatory Stop for the stronger identity claim and Non-Capture for a forced binary classification;
- all Chapter 1 object categories are now internally integrated without defining the stronger temporal, operational, continuity, admissibility, or record architectures of Chapters 2–7.

The canonical Chapter 1 prose is now substantively complete across Sections 1.1–1.11, but the chapter is not yet provisionally locked. WP4 must synchronize the complete Reference Kernel and Formal Model handoff, assign or produce the required case coverage, run the integrated Chapter 1 completion test, validate the repository package, and determine whether provisional lock is warranted.

The WP3 result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality attaches to the pending integrated synchronization and Chapter 1 lock, not to permission to weaken the identity, non-primitive, Stop, or Non-Capture boundaries established here. No Chapter 1 provisional lock is claimed by WP3.

## 2. Frame, Granularity, and Relative Level

Every STRATA transformation requires more than an identifiable source object. It also requires a declaration of **where and how that object is being reconstructed**. A conversation, configuration, non-event structure, transition, or composite may remain the same reference object while the analysis changes what counts as relevant, how finely distinctions are drawn, or where the object is positioned relative to other objects. Those changes are analytically consequential, but they are not interchangeable.

Chapter 2 establishes the coordinate architecture needed to keep them separate. Its first work package defines three primary coordinates:

```text
frame
→ what bounds relevance and the inside/outside of the reconstruction

granularity
→ how finely the reconstruction distinguishes the object

relative level
→ where the object is positioned within a declared relation
```

The coordinates may interact, but no coordinate can be inferred merely from another. A changed frame need not change granularity. Finer granularity need not move the object to a lower relative level. A changed relative level need not change the object, frame, or resolution. Nor does any coordinate change by itself identify `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, or `PROJECT_AS`. Operation identity remains owned by Chapter 4 and the later operation chapters.

The governing coordinate rule is:

```text
frame
≠
granularity
≠
relative level
```

This is a methodological separation, not an ontology of analytical dimensions. The coordinates state how a bounded reconstruction is located and tested. They do not claim that praxis is naturally divided into universal frames, natural resolutions, or fixed layers.

### 2.1 Why These Terms Must Be Separated

Earlier or informal level-language often compresses several different analytical changes into one expression. An analyst may say that a reconstruction has “moved up,” “gone deeper,” “zoomed in,” “changed context,” or become “more macro.” Such expressions can be useful shorthand, but without declaration they may conceal at least three independent questions.

**What counts as relevant?** This is a frame question. A decision-coordination reconstruction may include agenda admission, proposal turns, approval responses, and procedural closure while excluding unrelated biographical information. A responsibility-allocation reconstruction of the same conversation may select many of the same utterances but treat different relations as relevant.

**How finely is the object distinguished?** This is a granularity question. One reconstruction may treat the conversation as a sequence of proposal, objection, and decision. Another may distinguish interruptions, repairs, delayed responses, role-addressing, and the exact placement of approval language. The second account is finer along declared dimensions, but it is not thereby truer or more authoritative.

**Relative to what does the object count as a constituent, composite, local episode, or wider formation?** This is a relative-level question. The conversation may be composite relative to its utterance-and-response constituents while also being one constituent of a quarterly governance process. Its position changes with the declared relation; the object does not acquire an absolute level property.

These questions can vary independently.

```text
stable frame
+
changed granularity
→ possible

changed frame
+
stable granularity
→ possible

stable frame and granularity
+
changed relative relation
→ possible
```

They can also change together, but joint change must be declared rather than inferred. If a finer reconstruction also introduces a new relevance boundary, both granularity and frame have changed. If a conversation is reconstructed as one constituent of a wider review process and the analysis also compresses its internal turns, relative level and granularity have changed. One label such as “macro analysis” does not adequately report either case.

Separation is required for six reasons.

**First, object identity must not be silently rewritten.** Chapter 1 establishes that coordinate change does not repair a failed identity claim. A reconstruction may change frame, granularity, or relative position while continuing to concern the same reference object. It may also reveal that the supposed reference continuity was never supported. Coordinate movement therefore requires a new testable claim; it does not erase the status of the earlier one.

**Second, operation identity must not be inflated from direction words.** A movement toward a wider composite relation may later be produced by `COMPOSE`, but relative upwardness is not itself `COMPOSE`. A movement toward finer distinctions may later satisfy `DECOMPOSE`, but granularity change is not itself `DECOMPOSE`. A changed frame may be recontextualization, simple re-bounding, or part of another operation; it is not automatically `PROJECT_AS`.

```text
changed granularity
≠
DECOMPOSE automatically

changed frame
≠
PROJECT_AS automatically

changed relative level
≠
operation identity
```

**Third, truth and authority must not be smuggled into resolution or position.** Finer granularity may expose relevant internal relations, but it may also introduce distinctions without praxeological purchase, exceed source support, fragment the reference object, or obscure the coarser function. A relatively wider object may coordinate more constituents, but wider reach does not create a higher claim ceiling. More included structure is not more authority.

**Fourth, apparent disagreement must be tested for comparability.** Two reconstructions may use different frames or granularities and therefore answer different questions. They may be locally compatible, partially comparable, or non-comparable. But coordinate difference does not automatically dissolve substantive contradiction. If both analyses make incompatible claims about the same decision-authority relation in the same bounded conversation, granularity difference cannot be used as a universal escape.

**Fifth, non-events depend on declared relevance.** An unrealized response becomes analytically relevant only relative to a supported expectation and a frame in which that expectation matters. A different frame may make another non-realization relevant or render the first outside the present claim. Frame declaration therefore helps prevent mere absence or missing information from being inflated into a non-event structure.

**Sixth, later records require coordinate clarity without creating an ontology.** The Shared Transformation Record must be able to state source and target frame, granularity, relative level, and later scope fields. These record paths mirror declared coordinates. They do not establish universal scales, select the best granularity, infer higher-level truth, or decide which operation occurred.

The minimal separation can be expressed as three questions applied to one object:

```text
reference object:
recorded project-review conversation

frame question:
which contextual boundary determines relevance?

granularity question:
which distinctions reconstruct the conversation?

relative-level question:
within which declared relation is the conversation positioned?
```

The answers may be coordinated, but each must remain independently contestable.

### 2.2 Frame

An **analytical frame** is the declared contextual boundary that determines what counts as relevant to a reconstruction, what is treated as inside or outside its present object of concern, and how far the resulting claim may reach.

A frame may be temporally, institutionally, relationally, procedurally, or functionally specified. A review meeting may be framed as a decision-coordination episode, a responsibility-allocation episode, a conflict-interruption episode, or part of an institutional review cycle. The same source materials may support more than one frame when each frame is explicitly bounded and source-supported.

The frame coordinate carries five minimum functions.

**Relevance boundary.** The frame states which distinctions matter for the present reconstruction. In a decision-coordination frame, proposal admission, objection handling, approval, deferral, and closure may be relevant. Background facts that do not bear on those relations remain outside the present frame even if they are true.

**Object boundary.** The frame helps identify what the reconstruction treats as its bounded object. It may delimit one conversation, one review phase, one institutional procedure, or another reference-bearing unit. Frame declaration does not by itself prove object identity; Chapter 1 burdens still apply.

**Inside/outside distinction.** The frame states what is included in the present analytical field and what is excluded, backgrounded, or left open. Outside does not mean unreal, unimportant in every context, or permanently irrelevant. It means outside the current bounded reconstruction.

**Non-event relevance.** The frame helps determine which expected-but-unrealized structures are analytically relevant. A promised reconsideration may be a non-event within a review-compliance frame but irrelevant to a narrowly bounded account of who spoke during the meeting. The frame does not create the expectation; source support remains necessary.

**Claim limitation.** The frame constrains what the reconstruction can warrant. A claim established within a decision-coordination frame does not automatically generalize to the institution as a whole, to the participants as persons, or to every later review process.

The analytical frame coordinate must remain distinct from the PMS Frame operator type.

```text
□
→ canonical operator sign

Frame
→ canonical PMS operator name and type

Frame-typed occurrence
→ concrete source-supported framing structure

analytical frame
→ declared contextual coordinate of a reconstruction
```

An analytical frame may be supported by one or more Frame-typed occurrences. For example, an agenda, admission rule, role boundary, or procedural threshold may concretely constrain what enters a decision. But naming `□` does not complete the analytical frame declaration, and the coordinate need not reduce to one isolated Frame-typed occurrence. The coordinate states the boundary under which the reconstruction is made; the operator occurrence is one concrete structure that may help constitute or enforce that boundary.

```text
analytical frame
≠
Frame operator type
≠
Frame-typed occurrence
```

This distinction protects both PMS Base and the reconstruction. It prevents a local analytical selection from being presented as a revision of the operator type. It also prevents the abstract operator name from substituting for a concrete account of relevance.

#### Baseline conversation frame

Consider an illustrative source bundle containing an agenda, a transcript, and meeting minutes for one project-review conversation. The declared reference object is the recorded conversation, not the project team as a whole and not the participants as persons.

A baseline frame may be declared as follows:

```text
analytical frame:
project-review decision coordination

inside the frame:
proposal admission, objections, requested reconsideration,
approval response, deferral, and procedural closure

outside the present frame:
unrelated project history, private motives, personality claims,
and institutional practices not supported by the bounded source bundle

frame-limited claim:
how the recorded conversation organized decision coordination
```

This declaration does not establish that the selected frame is the only valid frame. It establishes what the current reconstruction treats as relevant and what it does not claim.

A second reconstruction might use a **responsibility-allocation frame** while retaining the same conversation and much of the same source material. It may ask who was positioned to answer, defer, approve, or carry forward a commitment. That change would not automatically move the conversation to another relative level or make the reconstruction finer. It would change the relevance boundary.

Frame plurality therefore remains possible:

```text
same reference object
+
different declared frames
→
possible rival or complementary reconstructions
```

Plurality is not arbitrariness. A frame must still possess praxeological purchase, source support, boundedness, and a claim that can be challenged. A frame invented only to preserve a preferred conclusion, exclude counterevidence, or rescue an earlier failed claim is not made admissible by declaration.

The frame coordinate also must remain distinct from **transformation context**. Chapter 5 later defines transformation context as the context within which an operation, especially a target function, possesses analytical performance and validity. A frame bounds a reconstruction. A transformation context governs the relation and validity of a transformation. They may overlap, but neither term substitutes for the other.

### 2.3 Granularity

**Granularity** is the declared resolution of a reconstruction: the set, density, and fineness of distinctions through which the reference object is represented and tested.

Granularity answers not which context is relevant, but how much internal differentiation the analysis introduces within the declared frame. A conversation may be reconstructed as one decision episode, as a sequence of proposal–objection–closure units, or as a finer organization of utterances, interruptions, repairs, delays, role-addressing, and response timing.

The relation between coarser and finer granularity is always local to a declared comparison. A reconstruction is finer only with respect to specified distinctions. It may be finer along one dimension and coarser along another.

```text
finer in turn-taking detail
+
coarser in institutional history
→
possible
```

Granularity is therefore not necessarily a single universal line from coarse to fine. It may be multidimensional and partially ordered. To call one reconstruction finer, the analysis must state what distinctions are added, what distinctions remain stable, and what distinctions may be compressed or excluded.

A granularity declaration carries at least six burdens.

**Distinction set.** The analysis must identify the kinds of distinctions it uses. These may concern turns, roles, intervals, operator occurrences, relations, thresholds, event/non-event structure, or another bounded analytical dimension.

**Comparison basis.** “Finer” and “coarser” require a stated comparator. A turn-level account may be finer than an episode-level account with respect to interactional sequence while remaining incomparable to a role-network account along another dimension.

**Reference preservation.** A finer reconstruction must remain answerable to the same reference object when sameness is claimed. If the account shifts from the conversation to the institution without declaration, it has not merely changed granularity.

**Praxeological purchase.** Added detail must alter a relevant reconstruction, test an existing typing, expose a constitutive relation, change a warranted claim, reveal loss, or otherwise make a praxeological difference. More detail alone is not an additional praxis finding.

**Source support.** Finer distinctions require adequate access. A transcript may support turn order and wording but not private motive. Granularity cannot legitimately exceed the distinctions available in the source basis.

**Loss declaration.** Every resolution selects and compresses. A coarser account may preserve decision closure while losing interactional repair. A finer account may expose repair dynamics while backgrounding long-duration institutional recurrence. Neither account is lossless.

The controlling rule is:

```text
finer granularity
≠
deeper reality
≠
higher truth
≠
greater authority
```

A finer account can fail in several ways. It may fragment the reference object into disconnected details. It may introduce distinctions without praxeological purchase. It may treat speculative microstructure as source-supported fact. It may obscure the coarser function that made the object analytically relevant. It may also become less comparable to another reconstruction because the distinction dimensions have changed.

Conversely, a coarser reconstruction is not automatically superficial. A bounded episode-level account may preserve a decision relation that remains invisible in isolated utterance coding. Coarser resolution may be the appropriate level of distinction for one claim, while a finer account is required for another.

#### Baseline conversation granularity

Within the project-review decision-coordination frame, the baseline granularity may distinguish:

```text
1. proposal admission
2. objection
3. request for reconsideration
4. approval or deferral response
5. procedural closure
```

This is a **decision-sequence granularity**. It reconstructs the conversation through five functionally relevant units rather than every utterance or every linguistic feature.

A later WP2 or WP3 comparison may hold the frame stable while moving to a finer **utterance-and-repair granularity**, adding interruption placement, response delay, repair initiation, addressee change, and the exact relation between approval language and closure. The finer account would require a declared praxeological reason. It would not inherit truth merely because it contains more distinctions.

At the WP1 stage, the baseline establishes the coordinate without yet performing the full changed-granularity comparison owned by Section 2.9.

Granularity must also remain separate from the canonical output class `resolution_neutral`. Granularity is an analytical coordinate. `resolution_neutral` is a result that may later be assigned when a valid finer-resolution test adds distinctions but does not change the warranted praxis reconstruction or claim. The coordinate does not predict the outcome.

```text
granularity
≠
resolution-test result
```

### 2.4 Relative Level

**Relative level** is the declared position of an object within a specified relation to one or more other objects.

The term is relational in the strict sense. An object does not possess a STRATA level independently of the comparison that positions it. A conversation may count as a composite relative to its utterances, as one constituent relative to a quarterly review process, and as a peer object relative to another conversation. Each statement uses a different relation. None establishes an absolute level.

A relative-level declaration therefore requires:

1. the positioned object;
2. the comparison object or objects;
3. the relation that connects them;
4. the bounded claim for which the position matters.

The relation may be part–whole, constituent–composite, occurrence–configuration, episode–process, local function–wider arrangement, source–target, or another explicitly supported relation. The relation must be named rather than implied by spatial metaphors such as “above,” “below,” “higher,” or “deeper.”

```text
relative level
=
position within a declared relation

relative level
≠
absolute rank
≠
ontological layer
≠
authority order
```

This definition blocks several common inflations.

**No natural hierarchy.** STRATA does not assume that every object belongs to one universal ladder. Different relation families may position the same object differently.

**No completeness privilege.** A relatively wider composite may include more constituents but still omit decisive relations, compress heterogeneity, or exceed traceability. Wider is not automatically more comprehensive in the sense of better warranted.

**No authority inheritance.** A claim made about a wider arrangement does not acquire authority over its constituents merely because it is relatively wider. A local claim does not become negligible merely because it is positioned as a constituent.

**No identity inference.** Changing the declared relation does not by itself change the reference object. Conversely, calling two objects “the same level” does not establish that they are comparable or identical.

**No operation inference.** Relative movement may accompany an operation, but the operation must be independently established. `COMPOSE` often yields a relatively wider composite; `DECOMPOSE` often reconstructs relatively finer constituents; `PROJECT_AS` may relate an origin-typed source to a bounded function in another context. These tendencies are not definitions of level and do not create operation identity.

#### One conversation under different relations

The same recorded project-review conversation can occupy different relative positions.

**Relation A — utterances to conversation**

```text
comparison objects:
individual utterance-and-response units
↔
recorded project-review conversation

relation:
constituents to bounded conversational composite

relative position of the conversation:
relatively wider / composite
```

**Relation B — conversation to quarterly review process**

```text
comparison objects:
recorded project-review conversation
↔
quarterly governance review process

relation:
local episode to wider process

relative position of the conversation:
relatively narrower / constituent
```

No contradiction follows. The reference object is the same conversation; the comparison relation has changed.

**Relation C — conversation to another project-review conversation**

```text
comparison objects:
conversation A
↔
conversation B

relation:
peer episodes within one declared comparison set

relative position:
co-level for this comparison only
```

The term “co-level” here is not a universal class. It reports that neither object is positioned as constituent or composite relative to the other under the declared peer relation.

The case demonstrates the boundary rule:

```text
same object
+
different declared relations
→
different relative positions possible
```

It does not establish that every relation supports a useful level claim. If the comparison relation is vague, metaphorical, unsupported, or irrelevant to the bounded claim, the relative-level statement lacks praxeological purchase.

#### Mandatory Stop: undeclared higher-level claim

Suppose an analysis states:

> “The conversation is a higher-level structure.”

No comparison object, relation, or claim purpose is supplied. The statement may be gesturing toward composition, institutional location, abstraction, or mere importance. STRATA cannot determine which.

The appropriate result for the asserted cross-level claim is:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

The Stop applies to the unsupported level assertion. It does not deny that the conversation exists, that it may be a composite relative to utterances, or that another properly declared relation could support a level claim. Re-entry requires an identified comparator, a named relation, and a bounded claim.

A change of wording to “macro,” “systemic,” or “higher-order” does not satisfy the missing declaration.

### 2.5 Micro, Meso, and Macro

`Micro`, `meso`, and `macro` are optional shorthand for locally declared relative positions. They are not fixed classes, universal scales, natural sizes, ontological strata, developmental ranks, or authority levels.

The shorthand may be used only after the underlying relation has been stated. Its purpose is compression after clarification, not substitution for clarification.

A three-position relation may be declared as follows:

```text
micro:
utterance-and-response units

meso:
recorded project-review conversation

macro:
quarterly governance review process

relation basis:
constituent interaction units
→ bounded conversation
→ recurring review process
```

Within this one relation, the labels are intelligible. The conversation is `meso` because it is composite relative to utterance units and constituent relative to the quarterly process.

But the same object can receive another shorthand in another relation.

```text
relation:
utterance units → conversation

conversation shorthand:
macro
```

```text
relation:
conversation → quarterly review process

conversation shorthand:
micro
```

These uses are not contradictory because the relation basis differs. They demonstrate why the labels cannot be object properties.

```text
micro / meso / macro
≠
intrinsic object types
```

The shorthand carries four restrictions.

**Locality.** The comparison set and relation must be declared within the analysis. A label imported from another domain does not retain automatic meaning.

**Non-universality.** There is no fixed quantity of time, space, participants, components, or abstraction that universally defines micro, meso, or macro across PMS-STRATA.

**Non-ranking.** Macro is not more true, more complete, or more authoritative. Micro is not more concrete, more causal, or more real. Meso is not a naturally privileged balance point.

**Non-inheritance.** A label assigned under one relation cannot be carried into another transformation, case, or domain without a new declaration. A new relation creates a new testable level claim.

The shorthand can also fail to capture a relation adequately. Some structures are distributed, cyclic, overlapping, heterarchical, or differently ordered along several dimensions. Forcing them into one universal micro–meso–macro ladder may distort the very relations under study. Chapter 2 later preserves Non-Capture for such forced classifications. WP1 establishes only that the shorthand is optional and defeasible.

The correct order is therefore:

```text
identify objects
→ declare relation
→ determine relative positions
→ optionally apply micro / meso / macro shorthand
```

The incorrect order is:

```text
assign micro / meso / macro labels
→ infer relation, truth, or authority afterward
```

#### WP1 coordinate boundary and handoff

Sections 2.1–2.5 establish the core coordinate architecture:

- frame bounds relevance, inside/outside, object focus, non-event relevance, and claim reach;
- the analytical frame coordinate remains distinct from the `Frame` operator type and concrete Frame-typed occurrences;
- granularity specifies the declared distinction set and resolution of a reconstruction;
- finer granularity possesses no automatic truth, depth, authority, or admissibility priority;
- granularity may be multidimensional and comparative rather than one universal scale;
- relative level positions an object only within an explicit relation;
- one reference object may occupy different relative positions under different relations;
- micro, meso, and macro are optional local shorthand, never fixed object classes or ontological strata;
- coordinate differences do not identify a STRATA operation;
- undeclared hierarchy claims require Stop rather than invented verticality.

The baseline conversation case now has a declared reference object, analytical frame, granularity, and relative position. It does not yet have the complete Chapter 2 coordinate declaration. WP2 must define temporal scope, source scope, and claim scope. WP3 must then test stable-frame granularity change, changed-frame stability, changed relative level, multiple valid granularities, granularity conflict, and the minimal non-schema declaration. WP4 must synchronize the complete chapter, assess the bounded Formal Model handoff, run the integrated completion test, and decide the provisional Chapter 2 lock.

The WP1 result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality attaches to incomplete Chapter 2 production, not to permission to collapse frame, granularity, relative level, or micro–meso–macro shorthand while later work proceeds.

### 2.6 Temporal Scope

**Temporal scope** is the declared temporal inclusion boundary of a reconstruction: the span, entry conditions, endpoint or open continuation, relevant prior conditions, relevant later effects, and periodization uncertainty that the present analysis treats as temporally available.

Temporal scope answers a different question from temporal ordering. It does not yet state which event preceded another, whether a sequence forms a path, whether a path became a trajectory, or whether a prior structure constrained later alternatives. Those stronger temporal relations belong to Chapter 3 and PATH. Temporal scope states which portion of time is included, which temporal edges remain open, and how far the current source-supported reconstruction may extend.

```text
temporal scope
→ temporal inclusion boundary

temporal ordering
→ relation among included temporal objects
```

The distinction is mandatory:

```text
temporal scope
≠ sequence
≠ path
≠ trajectory
≠ path dependence
```

A temporal-scope declaration carries at least six burdens.

**Primary interval.** The reconstruction must state the principal interval in which the reference object is examined. For a conversation, this may be the recorded meeting from agenda admission through procedural closure. The interval may be clock-bounded, event-bounded, phase-bounded, or condition-bounded, but the basis must be declared.

**Entry boundary.** The analysis must state why the reconstruction begins where it does. A meeting may begin analytically with the first proposal rather than the first greeting if the frame concerns decision coordination. That choice is not neutral: it excludes earlier relations and must remain contestable.

**Endpoint or open continuation.** The analysis must state whether the object ends at a supported endpoint or remains open. Procedural closure may end the bounded conversation while a promised reconsideration remains unresolved beyond it. Closure of the source object does not prove closure of every relation it contains.

**Relevant prior conditions.** Earlier structures may enter as bounded preconditions when the sources support them and the claim requires them. An agenda item, prior request, or already assigned role may be included without expanding the primary object into an unlimited history.

**Relevant later effects.** Later materials may be included when they bear on the bounded claim, such as minutes recording deferral or a later decision record documenting implementation. Later effects are not automatically part of the original object; their relation to it must be stated.

**Periodization uncertainty.** Beginnings and endings may be uncertain, disputed, source-dependent, or differently useful under different claims. The analysis must preserve that uncertainty rather than convert an analytical cut into a natural temporal boundary.

Temporal scope may change while frame, granularity, and relative level remain stable. Two decision-coordination reconstructions may use the same distinction set and relation position, while one examines only the recorded conversation and another includes supported agenda preconditions and a later decision entry. Conversely, a frame may change while the temporal interval stays fixed.

Temporal scope must also remain distinct from **temporal granularity**. Temporal scope asks which span is included. Temporal granularity asks how finely temporal distinctions inside that span are drawn.

```text
one meeting included
+
turn-by-turn timing
→ narrow scope, fine temporal granularity

three-month review cycle included
+
monthly checkpoints only
→ wider scope, coarser temporal granularity
```

Neither combination receives automatic priority. A longer temporal scope is not necessarily more complete, causal, or authoritative. It may include more later material while obscuring the bounded interaction that carries the present claim. A shorter scope is not necessarily superficial; it may be exactly adequate for a local procedural claim.

```text
longer temporal scope
≠ greater truth
≠ causal completeness
≠ higher claim ceiling
```

#### Baseline conversation temporal scope

The running project-review case can be bounded as follows:

```text
primary interval:
the recorded conversation from admission of the review proposal
through the documented procedural closure

relevant prior condition:
the agenda identifies the proposal and the scheduled review purpose

relevant immediate aftermath:
the minutes record that reconsideration was deferred rather than completed

open temporal edge:
the bounded source bundle does not establish what happened after the recorded minutes
```

This declaration permits a claim about how the recorded conversation reached procedural closure and how the minutes represented its immediate disposition. It does not permit the assertion that reconsideration never occurred later.

Suppose the source bundle ends with the minutes and contains no later implementation or follow-up record. The stronger statement—“the promised reconsideration did not occur”—crosses an open temporal edge. The available result is:

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

The claim may be reduced to:

> The bounded source bundle records deferral at closure and contains no later evidence within its declared temporal scope.

That statement preserves the source gap. It does not convert absence of later material into a non-event structure. If the analysis insists on the stronger never-occurred claim without extending and supporting the temporal scope, continuation of that claim requires:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

The Stop applies to the unsupported temporal extension, not to the local reconstruction of the recorded conversation.

### 2.7 Source Scope

**Source scope** is the declared boundary of the material basis and source-supported distinctions available to a reconstruction, including what is directly supported, inferentially reconstructed, missing, uncertain, or beyond the present source ceiling.

Source scope does not merely list files. It states what the available materials permit the analysis to access. An agenda, transcript, and minutes may be the **source basis**. The **source scope** explains which relations those materials support: agenda framing, recorded turn order, wording, procedural closure, minute-level disposition, and identified gaps. The **source ceiling** states the maximum claim strength or reach those materials can bear.

```text
source basis
→ the actual supporting materials

source scope
→ the bounded structures and distinctions accessible through them

source ceiling
→ the maximum support those materials can carry
```

These terms are related but not interchangeable.

```text
source basis
≠ source scope
≠ source ceiling
```

A source-scope declaration carries at least seven burdens.

**Basis inventory.** The analysis must identify the materials on which it relies. Generic phrases such as “the record” are insufficient when different documents support different distinctions.

**Direct support.** The declaration must identify what the materials directly establish. A transcript may directly support recorded wording and turn order. Minutes may directly support how the meeting was officially summarized. An agenda may directly support scheduled purpose and admitted topics.

**Inferential reconstruction.** The declaration must mark structures inferred from relations among sources rather than directly stated in one source. A procedural deferral may be reconstructed from a request, a non-answer, closure language, and a minute entry. The inference remains revisable and cannot be relabelled as direct observation.

**Missing structure.** The declaration must name absent materials or inaccessible distinctions that matter to the claim. Missing follow-up records, off-record exchanges, undocumented criteria, or unavailable earlier meetings remain gaps rather than silently completed context.

**Uncertain structure.** Ambiguous transcription, disputed attribution, unclear timestamps, or incompatible summaries must remain uncertain. Formal cleanliness does not resolve source uncertainty.

**Cross-source relation.** Where several materials are used, the analysis must state how they relate. Minutes do not automatically override a transcript, and a transcript does not automatically reveal the institutional status of the minutes. Divergence among sources may itself be relevant.

**Exclusion and speculative edge.** The declaration must state where source-supported reconstruction ends and speculative supplementation would begin. A source gap may motivate a new inquiry; it may not be filled merely because a coherent continuation is imaginable.

Source scope is frame-sensitive but not identical to frame. The same agenda, transcript, and minutes may support a decision-coordination frame and a responsibility-allocation frame. Each frame selects different relations from the same materials. Conversely, two analyses may use the same frame while one has a transcript and the other has only minutes, producing different source scopes.

```text
same source basis
+
different frames
→ different relevant source scopes possible

same frame
+
different source access
→ different source scopes possible
```

More sources do not automatically create a broader valid claim. Added material may be irrelevant, contradictory, lower quality, or outside the declared object. Source quantity is not source adequacy, and source scope is not an authority rank.

```text
more material
≠ greater warrant automatically
```

#### Baseline conversation source scope

For the running case, the source scope may be declared as follows:

```text
source basis:
meeting agenda, recorded transcript, and approved minutes

support available:
- scheduled review purpose and admitted proposal
- recorded sequence and wording of proposal, objection,
  reconsideration request, response, and closure
- the minutes' representation of deferral

inferentially reconstructed:
- procedural deferral as the relation among the request,
  absence of completed reconsideration within the meeting,
  closure, and minute entry

missing or unavailable:
- private motives
- off-record discussion
- later follow-up or implementation records
- institution-wide recurrence beyond this conversation

uncertain:
- any relation not preserved clearly enough in the transcript
  or contradicted across the source bundle
```

This source scope supports a bounded reconstruction of the recorded conversation. It does not support person typing, private-motive claims, a general institutional pattern, or a later non-event claim.

Suppose the analyst argues:

> Because no follow-up appears in the source bundle, the organization never reconsidered the proposal.

The argument converts a source gap into a positive historical and institutional claim. The correct disposition is:

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

The reduced statement may report that no follow-up is present **within the declared source scope**. If the stronger general claim is retained despite the acknowledged gap, the relevant claim must receive:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

Again, Stop does not erase the local source-supported reconstruction. It blocks authority inheritance from bounded material into an unsupported wider claim.

Source scope also remains distinct from **source object**. The recorded conversation is the reference object. The agenda, transcript, and minutes are materials through which that object is reconstructed. A source file can itself become a reference object in another analysis, but that requires a new declaration.

### 2.8 Claim Scope

**Claim scope** is the explicitly declared range within which a tested statement applies: the reference object or claim family, frame, relevant relation, granularity, relative position, temporal reach, source-supported context, and excluded generalizations that bound what the analysis may assert.

Claim scope answers:

> Exactly what is being claimed, about which object or relation, under which declared coordinates, for which time and context, and with which exclusions?

It does not answer how strong the claim may be asserted in every respect. That is partly a **claim ceiling** question. It does not restate the entire **claim boundary**, which governs the broader permissible edge and authority of the claim. It does not determine the contextual **validity scope** of a target function, which belongs to Chapter 5.

```text
claim scope
→ where and to what the claim applies

claim ceiling
→ maximum warranted strength or reach

claim boundary
→ overall permissible edge and authority constraint

validity scope
→ context in which a target function is valid
```

The distinctions remain:

```text
claim scope
≠ claim ceiling
≠ claim boundary
≠ validity scope
```

A claim-scope declaration carries at least eight burdens.

**Claim object.** The analysis must state whether the claim concerns one occurrence, one composite, one configuration, one transition, a relation, a path candidate, a type family, or a recurring pattern. A local occurrence claim does not automatically become a type claim.

**Claim predicate.** The structural assertion must be stated precisely. “The conversation organized procedural deferral” is more testable than “the conversation was dysfunctional.”

**Coordinate binding.** The claim must be bound to its declared frame, granularity, relative relation, temporal scope, and source scope. A coordinate change creates a new claim test rather than silently extending the existing one.

**Contextual reach.** The analysis must state the contexts in which the claim is asserted and those in which it is not. A claim about one project-review conversation does not automatically apply to the project, organization, participants, or later reviews.

**Temporal reach.** The claim must state whether it concerns the recorded interval, supported preconditions, immediate aftermath, a recurring period, or an open continuation. Open temporal edges remain open.

**Generalization status.** The declaration must distinguish singular reconstruction, limited pattern claim, type claim, and broader generalization. Repetition, sampling, and rival evidence burdens increase with reach.

**Excluded attributions.** The claim must state relevant exclusions such as private motive, personality, diagnosis, normative verdict, causal completeness, legal status, or authority over persons.

**Re-entry condition.** Where a stronger claim is withheld, the analysis should state what additional sources, comparisons, or transformations would be required to test it.

Claim scope is constrained by source scope but is not identical to it. A source bundle may support many possible local claims; the present analysis selects one bounded claim. Conversely, a broad claim scope cannot be made admissible merely by declaration when the source scope or claim ceiling cannot carry it.

```text
declared broad scope
+
insufficient source support
≠ admissible broad claim
```

Claim scope is also not inherited from relative level. A claim about a relatively wider composite does not govern every constituent, and a fine-grained claim does not receive automatic superiority over a coarser claim.

#### Baseline conversation claim scope

The running case can now state a bounded claim scope:

```text
claim object:
the recorded project-review conversation

claim predicate:
the conversation organized a request for reconsideration
as procedural deferral at documented closure

frame:
project-review decision coordination

granularity:
decision-sequence units

relative position:
conversation as a composite relative to its recorded
utterance-and-response constituents

temporal reach:
from proposal admission through documented closure,
including the agenda precondition and minute-level immediate disposition

source-supported context:
the declared agenda, transcript, and minutes bundle

excluded claims:
private motives, person traits, institution-wide recurrence,
later non-occurrence, causal completeness, normative or legal verdict,
and authority over subsequent cases
```

Under the illustrative source assumptions, this is a bounded structural claim. Its proper local result may be expressed as:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The class does not certify empirical truth. It states that the reconstruction is admissible only under the declared object, sources, time, frame, granularity, relation, and exclusions.

Now consider the stronger claim:

> The organization systematically suppresses objections and never honors reconsideration commitments.

The statement generalizes from one bounded conversation to an institution-wide recurring pattern and extends beyond the available temporal and source scope. It also introduces a stronger evaluative and potentially causal attribution. The immediate result is:

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

A defensible reduced claim returns to the recorded conversation and its documented procedural relation. If the analysis refuses reduction and continues to treat the local source bundle as institution-wide authority, the unsupported generalization requires:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

Changing the label to “macro pattern,” “systemic structure,” or “higher-level finding” does not repair the source and claim mismatch. A new frame, granularity, relative relation, or transformation would create a new testable claim; it would not erase the failed broader one.

#### Scope interaction without collapse

The three scopes often constrain one another, but none can be calculated from the others.

A temporal scope may include an interval for which the source scope is uneven. The transcript may cover the meeting in detail, while the minutes provide only a compressed immediate aftermath and no later follow-up. The time range is declared, but source access differs across it. Inclusion in temporal scope therefore does not imply equal evidential access.

```text
time included
≠
time equally sourced
```

A source scope may be broad enough to support several possible claims while the present claim scope remains deliberately narrow. The agenda, transcript, and minutes may support claims about proposal handling, role-addressing, procedural closure, and official summary. A reconstruction concerned only with deferral should not absorb every accessible relation merely because the material permits them.

```text
source-access breadth
≠
required claim breadth
```

Conversely, a declared claim scope may be wider than the sources can bear. Declaration makes the intended reach visible; it does not make that reach admissible. The source ceiling and later claim audit may require reduction even when every scope field is syntactically complete.

```text
complete declaration
≠
sufficient support
```

The scopes may also change independently.

**Stable temporal and source scope, narrower claim scope.** Two analyses use the same meeting interval and the same agenda–transcript–minutes bundle. One claims only that a reconsideration request was deferred at closure. The other attempts to characterize the complete responsibility structure of the conversation. The latter requires additional relations and may exceed the declared granularity or claim ceiling even though time and sources are unchanged.

**Stable temporal and claim scope, changed source scope.** Two analyses make the same local deferral claim about the same interval. One has a transcript and minutes; the other has only minutes. Their claim wording may match, but the second reconstruction has less direct access to the interactional relations that carry the claim. Apparent wording identity does not establish equal support.

**Stable source basis and local predicate, changed temporal scope.** One reconstruction concerns only the recorded meeting. Another includes an agenda precondition and minute-level aftermath from the same bundle. The second may clarify entry and disposition without establishing a trajectory or later institutional pattern.

**Changed frame, stable scope values.** The same materials and time span may be examined under decision coordination or responsibility allocation. Identical temporal and source boundaries do not make the frames identical, and a similar claim length does not make the predicates equivalent.

These variants show why scope fields must be explicit in later records but cannot be treated as independent truth switches. A machine-readable record may verify that declarations exist and use permitted structures. It cannot determine whether the selected interval is the best periodization, whether the source inference is semantically adequate, whether the claim predicate is true, or whether omitted material would reverse the result.

The Shared Transformation Record in Chapter 7 will record source and target temporal scope, source scope, and claim scope through the existing nested paths. It will not redefine the concepts, create top-level duplicates, or infer support from field completion. Chapter 49 will later apply the fuller source- and claim-ceiling discipline, including formal-model limits and anti-overreach checks. Chapter 2 supplies the scope coordinates those later controls consume; it does not pre-empt their procedures.

A valid scope declaration therefore has a double function:

1. it makes the present reconstruction inspectable and contestable;
2. it exposes where a stronger claim would require new sources, a changed temporal boundary, a different comparison set, or a separate transformation record.

It does not grant permission to move across those boundaries. Every such movement produces a new testable claim, while the earlier result remains recorded.

#### WP2 scope boundary and handoff

Sections 2.6–2.8 establish the scope architecture required by every later coordinate declaration and transformation record:

- temporal scope declares the included time span, entry boundary, endpoint or open continuation, relevant prior conditions, later effects, and periodization uncertainty;
- temporal scope remains distinct from temporal ordering, temporal granularity, sequence, path, trajectory, and path dependence;
- source scope declares the material basis and the distinctions that are directly supported, inferentially reconstructed, missing, uncertain, or beyond the source ceiling;
- source basis, source scope, source object, and source ceiling remain non-confusable;
- claim scope declares the exact object, predicate, coordinates, temporal and contextual reach, generalization status, exclusions, and re-entry condition of a tested statement;
- claim scope remains distinct from claim boundary, claim ceiling, source scope, and Chapter 5 validity scope;
- open temporal edges and source gaps cannot be converted into positive non-event, historical, causal, or institutional claims;
- a relatively wider or finer analytical position creates no authority inheritance;
- claim reduction and Mandatory Stop remain available without erasing the valid local reconstruction.

The baseline conversation now has canonical definitions for all seven conceptual coordinate slots required by Chapter 2: reference object, frame, granularity, relative level, temporal scope, source scope, and claim scope. WP3 must test coordinate changes, multiple valid granularities, apparent and real contradiction, and the minimal explicitly non-schema declaration. WP4 must run integrated synchronization, case assignment, formal-model handoff, completion testing, and the provisional Chapter 2 lock decision.

The WP2 result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality attaches to incomplete Chapter 2 production. It does not permit later work to collapse the three scopes or to use open time, missing sources, or broader wording as substitutes for support.

### 2.9 Stable Frame, Changed Granularity

A **stable-frame granularity change** occurs when the reference object, relevance boundary, and present claim family remain declared as stable while the reconstruction changes how finely the object is distinguished.

The change is not merely a longer description. It must alter the operative distinction set. A coarse reconstruction may distinguish proposal, objection, response, and closure. A finer reconstruction of the same conversation may distinguish interruption, repair, role-addressing, delayed uptake, qualification, and the exact placement of closure language. The finer account divides or relates structures that the coarser account treated together.

```text
stable reference object
+
stable analytical frame
+
changed distinction set
→
changed granularity
```

Four burdens apply.

**Reference continuity.** The reconstruction must still concern the same bounded object or must state where the object boundary has changed. Finer detail cannot silently replace one conversation with a larger institutional history or with a collection of surrounding episodes.

**Frame stability.** The relevance rule must remain stable enough for the comparison to be meaningful. A move from decision coordination to responsibility allocation is not merely finer detail if it changes which relations count as analytically central.

**Declared granularity relation.** The analysis must state how the source and target resolutions relate. Terms such as finer, coarser, aggregated, segmented, temporally expanded, relationally enriched, or differently partitioned require a named basis. “Deeper” is not a sufficient relation.

**Loss and gain disclosure.** The finer reconstruction must state what becomes visible and what may become harder to retain. The coarser reconstruction must state what it compresses and what wider relation it preserves. Both directions have loss.

The running project-review conversation supports a controlled comparison.

At **decision-sequence granularity**, the conversation is reconstructed through:

```text
proposal admission
→ objection
→ response
→ deferral
→ procedural closure
```

At **utterance-and-repair granularity**, the same conversation may be reconstructed through:

```text
proposal wording
→ interruption
→ qualification
→ role-addressed request
→ delayed uptake
→ attempted repair
→ closure wording
→ minute-level disposition
```

The frame remains decision coordination. The reference object remains the recorded conversation. The temporal, source, and claim scopes remain local. The target reconstruction is finer because it distinguishes relations carried inside units that the source reconstruction compressed.

The finer version may reveal that what looked like one objection contained several distinct moves, or that closure followed an attempted repair rather than a single refusal. It may therefore change the warranted reconstruction. But it receives no automatic priority merely because it contains more distinctions.

```text
finer reconstruction
≠ truer reconstruction automatically
≠ more complete reconstruction automatically
≠ greater authority
```

The comparison must also preserve the coarser function. If the utterance-level reconstruction becomes a list of fragments from which proposal, objection, deferral, and closure can no longer be reconstructed, the analysis has gained local detail while losing the reference relation that made the comparison praxeologically useful.

A stable-frame granularity change is not automatically `DECOMPOSE`. Chapter 19 owns the operation-specific test. The present coordinate comparison establishes only that a finer target distinction set has been declared. `DECOMPOSE` would additionally require a compressed occurrence or composite to be opened as a finer reconstruction of the same reference object while preserving constitutive relations, identity burdens, source trace, and loss.

```text
changed granularity
+
stable frame
≠
DECOMPOSE by itself
```

Where the finer distinctions are source-supported and alter the bounded claim, the new reconstruction receives its own testable claim. Where the distinctions are valid but do not change the warranted reconstruction, the later resolution test may hand off to:

```text
resolution_neutral
```

Chapter 2 does not assign that class merely because a finer description exists. The class requires a valid comparison showing that the added distinctions produce no changed warranted reconstruction.

### 2.10 Changed Frame, Stable Granularity

A **stable-granularity frame change** occurs when the reconstruction retains a declared resolution while changing the contextual boundary that determines which relations are relevant and what the claim is about.

```text
stable reference object
+
stable distinction density
+
changed relevance boundary
→
changed frame
```

The same utterance-level record can support more than one frame. Under a **decision-coordination frame**, the reconstruction asks how proposal admission, objection, response, deferral, and closure were coordinated. Under a **responsibility-allocation frame**, it asks which role-position received a request, which role-position could respond, how responsibility was acknowledged or displaced, and what the documented closure assigned or left open.

The granularity may remain utterance-and-repair level in both analyses. The words, turn boundaries, interruptions, and response placements are distinguished with comparable density. What changes is the relevance rule.

This comparison carries five burdens.

**Frame statement.** Each reconstruction must state its bounded question rather than merely use a thematic label.

**Stable-resolution statement.** The analysis must identify the dimensions held stable. Equal word count or equal number of coded units does not prove stable granularity; the distinction rules must be comparable.

**Relevance redistribution.** The changed frame must state which relations become load-bearing, which remain modulating, and which move outside the present claim.

**Claim renewal.** A materially changed frame creates a new testable claim. It does not inherit the prior result, claim ceiling, or admissibility class.

**Non-operation statement.** Frame change alone does not identify `PROJECT_AS`, `COMPOSE`, or `DECOMPOSE`.

The responsibility-allocation reconstruction may use the same source materials, time span, and utterance-level granularity as the decision-coordination reconstruction. Yet it can reach a different bounded finding because it tests another relation. This difference need not be contradiction. The claims may be complementary, partially comparable, or non-comparable depending on their predicates.

A changed analytical frame is also not automatically PMS `Φ` Recontextualization. An analyst may re-bound relevance without claiming that an earlier structure has been embedded in a new context as an operator occurrence. Nor is the frame change automatically `PROJECT_AS`. That operation requires an origin-typed source object to receive a bounded contextual target function while origin type is preserved.

```text
changed analytical frame
≠ Φ occurrence automatically
≠ PROJECT_AS automatically
≠ new target function automatically
```

The confusion case arises when an analyst says that the conversation has been “retyped as responsibility.” If the analysis has only changed the frame under which the same utterance relations are inspected, no target function has yet been established. The proper correction is to declare the new frame and test the new claim. Operation classification remains open until Chapter 4 and the relevant operation procedure are satisfied.

### 2.11 Changed Relative Level

A **relative-level change** occurs when the same reference object is repositioned under a different declared relation or when source and target objects occupy different positions within one declared relation.

Relative level cannot change in the abstract. Every change must identify:

```text
positioned object
+
comparator or containing relation
+
relation type
+
bounded analytical purpose
```

The project-review conversation illustrates three legitimate positions.

**Composite relative to utterances.** The conversation is relatively wider than its recorded utterance-and-response constituents under a constitutive part–whole relation.

**Constituent relative to a quarterly governance process.** The same conversation is relatively narrower than the wider review process under a process-membership relation.

**Peer relative to another project-review conversation.** The conversation is laterally positioned under a comparison relation between bounded episodes.

The reference object has not acquired three intrinsic levels. Its position changes because the relation changes.

```text
same conversation
+
utterance–conversation relation
→ relatively wider

same conversation
+
```
