```text
conversation–quarterly-process relation
→ relatively narrower

same conversation
+
conversation–conversation comparison
→ peer-positioned
```

No one of these positions is ontologically higher, more real, more explanatory, or more authoritative.

A relative-level change must remain distinct from a granularity change. The conversation may be represented coarsely or finely while retaining the same constituent position inside the quarterly process. Conversely, it may move from composite-relative-to-utterances to constituent-relative-to-process while its internal distinction set remains stable.

```text
changed relative level
≠ changed granularity automatically
≠ changed frame automatically
```

Relative-level change also does not identify an operation. A wider composite may later be formed by `COMPOSE`; a constituent may later be reconstructed through `DECOMPOSE`; a source object may later receive a function in a wider context through `PROJECT_AS`. The coordinate only states position. The operation states how a source–target relation is formed or tested.

Where the comparator or relation is omitted, expressions such as “higher-level structure,” “deeper layer,” or “macro object” are not usable level declarations. The cross-level claim requires:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

Re-entry requires a named comparator, relation, bounded purpose, source support, and a renewed claim. The Stop does not deny that a valid relation may later be declared. It blocks authority and ontology from being smuggled through undeclared vertical language.

### 2.12 Multiple Valid Granularities

The same reference object and claim family may support more than one valid granularity. Valid plurality does not require that all granularities produce the same reconstruction, that one can be fully reduced to another, or that a single privileged resolution must be selected.

```text
multiple valid granularities
→ possible

multiple valid granularities
≠ equal usefulness for every claim
≠ mutual reducibility
≠ universal resolution hierarchy
```

A granularity is locally valid when it satisfies at least the following burdens:

1. the reference object and object boundary remain identifiable;
2. the frame and scopes are declared;
3. the distinction set is source-supported;
4. the selected resolution has praxeological purchase for the tested claim;
5. constitutive relations remain traceable at the level needed by that claim;
6. compression, exclusion, uncertainty, and irrecoverability are disclosed;
7. the claim ceiling is not increased merely by additional detail;
8. rival granularities remain available where they carry different relevant structure.

In the running case, the coarse decision-sequence reconstruction may be sufficient for a claim about whether the meeting reached documented procedural closure. The finer utterance-and-repair reconstruction may be necessary for a claim about how the objection was handled before closure. Each can therefore be admissible for a different bounded claim.

The coarser reconstruction preserves the wider procedural relation while compressing local interaction. The finer reconstruction preserves local repair structure while risking fragmentation or loss of the wider procedural shape. Neither loss profile can be inferred from the word “coarse” or “fine” alone.

A plurality comparison should therefore ask:

```text
What claim is each granularity testing?
What becomes visible?
What is compressed?
Which constitutive relation is preserved?
Which source burden is added?
Would the result change if the resolution changed?
```

When two valid granularities support distinct bounded claims, both may remain admissible without being merged into one total reconstruction. When a valid finer test adds no changed warranted reconstruction, the later output may be `resolution_neutral`. When each resolution captures different but incomplete structure and no integration is presently warranted, a bounded `partially_admissible` result or eventual `non_capture` may be appropriate under the governing output rules. Chapter 2 supplies the comparison architecture; Chapters 6, 25, and 52 own the full output assignment and Non-Capture burden.

Finer resolution is therefore neither the default destination nor the default remedy for disagreement. The correct stopping point may be the coarser reconstruction when further distinction lacks praxeological purchase, exceeds the sources, or obscures the relation that carries the claim.

### 2.13 Granularity Conflict

A **granularity conflict** is a comparison problem in which two reconstructions use different distinction sets and appear to support incompatible claims. The first task is to determine whether the claims are genuinely comparable. The second is to prevent granularity difference from being used to dissolve a real contradiction.

The comparability test asks:

1. Do the reconstructions concern the same reference object or explicitly related objects?
2. Do they use the same frame, or are the frames at least translatable for the tested predicate?
3. Do they assert claims about the same relation, predicate, temporal scope, and generalization boundary?
4. Is the source–target granularity relation declared?
5. Can relevant distinctions be translated, aggregated, or traced across the two resolutions without erasing constitutive structure?
6. Are the losses and exclusions of both reconstructions disclosed?
7. Would either claim change under a supported refinement or compression test?

Four broad outcomes are possible.

**Apparent contradiction through different questions.** One reconstruction claims that the conversation reached documented procedural closure. Another claims that several repair attempts remained interactionally unresolved before closure. These claims may coexist because closure of the procedure and resolution of every interactional relation are not the same predicate.

**Compatible claims at different resolutions.** A coarse account identifies objection, deferral, and closure. A fine account specifies the utterance sequence through which those relations occurred. The fine account may support, qualify, or limit the coarse account without contradicting it.

**Partial tension requiring claim reduction.** A coarse statement such as “the objection was rejected” may exceed what the fine record supports if the interaction instead shows deferral, qualification, and unresolved repair. The coarse claim may require reduction to the relation both resolutions can support.

**Substantive contradiction.** Two reconstructions may use different granularities yet assert incompatible answers to the same bounded claim. Suppose both concern the same recorded conversation, decision-coordination frame, temporal scope, and authority predicate. One states that an identified role-position possessed and exercised final decision authority. The other states that the same record contains no supported authorization or exercise by that role-position. Different resolutions do not make both claims true. The disagreement remains substantive and must be tested against source trace, typing, and claim ceiling.

```text
different granularity
≠ different question automatically
≠ contradiction automatically
≠ contradiction dissolved automatically
```

The invalid neutralization move is:

> Both analyses are correct because one is micro and the other is macro.

That statement fails unless the relations and predicates have been shown to differ in a way that permits coexistence. If both claims answer the same bounded question incompatibly, “micro” and “macro” cannot protect them from adjudication.

Where comparability cannot yet be established, the result may require claim reduction, further declaration, or a bounded Stop. Where a universal micro–meso–macro classification is forced across several locally useful but incompatible relations, `non_capture` may remain available for the forced universal classification. It may not rescue an unsupported local claim or convert unresolved contradiction into plural validity.

```text
non_capture of forced universal classification
≠ protection of failed local claim
```

### 2.14 Minimal Level Declaration

The **Minimal Level Declaration** is the smallest conceptual declaration needed to make Chapter 2 coordinates inspectable before operation-specific testing. The historical name is retained from the Structure Blueprint, but the declaration covers coordinates and scopes, not only relative level.

It is explicitly **not** the Shared Transformation Record, not YAML syntax, and not a second machine schema. The following is a readable conceptual form:

```text
reference object:
source frame:
source granularity:
source relative level and relation:
target frame:
target granularity:
target relative level and relation:
granularity relation:
temporal scope:
source scope:
claim scope:
coordinate changes declared:
operation identity:
loss and uncertainty note:
```

`operation identity` may remain **undetermined at the coordinate stage**. A complete coordinate declaration does not prove that `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, or `PROJECT_AS` occurred.

The conceptual slots map to existing Shared Transformation Record locations rather than creating new top-level fields:

| Conceptual slot | Existing record location |
| --- | --- |
| source reference object | `/source/reference_object` |
| source frame | `/source/frame` |
| source granularity | `/source/granularity` |
| source relative level | `/source/relative_level` |
| source temporal scope | `/source/temporal_scope` |
| source scope | `/source/source_scope` |
| target reference object | `/target/reference_object` |
| target frame | `/target/frame` |
| target granularity | `/target/granularity` |
| target relative level | `/target/relative_level` |
| target temporal scope | `/target/temporal_scope` |
| claim scope | `/claim/claim_scope` |
| transformation context | `/operation/shared_occurrence/transformation_context` |
| granularity relation and coordinate rationale | existing operation, relation, claim, loss, and extension locations as specified by Chapter 7 and the operation procedures |

The map is a prose handoff. It does not modify the schema, create aliases with independent authority, or require that every operation use a new field called `granularity_relation`.

#### Variant A — stable frame, changed granularity

```text
reference object:
recorded project-review conversation

source frame:
decision coordination

source granularity:
decision-sequence units

source relative level and relation:
conversation as composite relative to utterance-and-response constituents

target frame:
decision coordination

target granularity:
utterance-and-repair units

target relative level and relation:
conversation as composite relative to the same constituent family

granularity relation:
finer segmentation and relation tracing within the same bounded object

temporal scope:
recorded conversation plus declared agenda precondition and minute-level immediate disposition

source scope:
agenda, transcript, and minutes; no off-record or later implementation material

claim scope:
local claim about how objection handling contributed to documented procedural closure

coordinate changes declared:
granularity changed; frame and relative relation held stable

operation identity:
not established by the coordinate declaration; DECOMPOSE remains to be tested

loss and uncertainty note:
source version compresses local repairs; target version risks fragmenting the wider procedural relation
```

#### Variant B — changed frame, stable granularity

```text
reference object:
recorded project-review conversation

source frame:
decision coordination

source granularity:
utterance-and-repair units

source relative level and relation:
conversation as composite relative to recorded utterance-and-response constituents

target frame:
responsibility allocation

target granularity:
utterance-and-repair units

target relative level and relation:
conversation as composite relative to the same constituent family

granularity relation:
stable distinction density across the two frame declarations

temporal scope:
same bounded interval

source scope:
same agenda, transcript, and minutes bundle

claim scope:
new local claim about role-addressing, response capacity, and documented responsibility disposition

coordinate changes declared:
frame changed; granularity and relative relation held stable

operation identity:
not established; neither PROJECT_AS nor Φ is inferred

loss and uncertainty note:
each frame excludes relations central to the other and receives its own claim ceiling
```

#### Variant C — changed relative level

```text
reference object:
recorded project-review conversation

source frame:
decision coordination

source granularity:
decision-sequence units

source relative level and relation:
conversation as composite relative to utterance-and-response constituents

target frame:
decision coordination

target granularity:
decision-sequence units

target relative level and relation:
conversation as constituent relative to the quarterly governance process

granularity relation:
stable internal distinction set

temporal scope:
conversation interval with only the wider-process context supported by the declared source bundle

source scope:
agenda, transcript, and minutes; no complete quarterly-process record

claim scope:
claim limited to the conversation's documented membership and local function within the named process

coordinate changes declared:
relative relation changed; frame and granularity held stable

operation identity:
not established; COMPOSE, DECOMPOSE, and PROJECT_AS remain separate tests

loss and uncertainty note:
the target position widens contextual relation without establishing the complete process or higher authority
```

These variants satisfy the Chapter Contract requirement to display stable frame with changed granularity, changed frame with stable granularity, and changed relative level under an explicit relation. They also show why coordinate completeness is not operation completeness.

A minimal declaration fails when it uses absolute level language, omits the comparator, hides simultaneous coordinate changes, treats finer resolution as truth gain, or converts field completion into support. Where the declaration cannot state one universal level because the object occupies several locally valid positions, the analysis should retain the explicit relations. It should not force a universal classification merely to complete a template.

#### WP3 boundary and handoff

Sections 2.9–2.14 establish the comparison architecture required before Chapter 2 can be integrated:

- stable frame with changed granularity is possible and is not automatically `DECOMPOSE`;
- changed frame with stable granularity is possible and is not automatically `PROJECT_AS` or `Φ`;
- relative-level change requires an explicit comparator and relation and identifies no operation by itself;
- multiple granularities may be locally valid without truth ranking or mutual reducibility;
- apparent contradiction must be separated from substantive contradiction through an explicit comparability test;
- granularity mismatch cannot automatically neutralize incompatible answers to the same bounded claim;
- `resolution_neutral` requires a valid resolution test, not merely finer wording;
- forced universal scale classification may remain uncaptured without protecting weak local claims;
- the Minimal Level Declaration is conceptual, prose-bound, and mapped to existing nested record paths;
- every material coordinate change creates a new testable claim while prior failure remains recorded.

WP4 must now run the integrated redundancy guard, Reference and Formal Model synchronization review, case assignment, Completion Test, and provisional Chapter 2 lock decision. It may correct genuine inconsistencies, but it may not turn coordinate plurality into ontology, operation identity, or authority.

The WP3 result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality attaches to pending integrated Chapter 2 synchronization and lock. It does not weaken the coordinate-change, comparability, non-neutralization, Minimal Declaration, Stop, or Non-Capture boundaries established here.


<a id="chapter-3-configuration-event-non-event-transition-path-and-trajectory"></a>

## 3. Configuration, Event, Non-Event, Transition, Path, and Trajectory

Temporal language becomes analytically dangerous when mere succession is allowed to carry historical claims that its sources do not support. A date does not create a sequence, a sequence does not create a path, a path does not create a trajectory, and a trajectory does not by itself establish path dependence. Before PATH can operationalize temporal formations, STRATA therefore requires a controlled object grammar that states what additional burden enters at each step.

Chapter 1 established the eligibility of configurations, event-like objects, non-event structures, and transitions as analytical objects. Chapter 2 established the coordinates and scopes within which such objects are reconstructed. Chapter 3 adds the stronger temporal specification needed to relate these objects without redefining their basic object-category status. Its governing question is:

> What additional structure is required to move from a configuration and event relation to a sequence, path, trajectory, and path-dependence claim?

The full burden-increasing chain is:

```text
configuration
→ transition
→ sequence
→ path
→ trajectory
```

Path dependence is not the next object in that chain. It is an additional property claim about the historical determination of a bounded present structure.

Chapter 3 establishes that grammar across three burden layers:

```text
temporal object foundations
→ configuration, state adequacy, event, non-event, transition

ordered historical objects
→ sequence, path, trajectory

historical properties and limits
→ path dependence, sedimentation, irreversibility, unrealized alternatives
```

It does not identify `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, or `PROJECT_AS`. Temporal object classification and operation identity remain separate tests, and PATH later operationalizes these objects without creating a second definition layer.

The governing boundary is:

```text
more temporal structure
≠
more truth
≠
more authority
```

### 3.1 Configuration

A **configuration** is a bounded, temporally located reconstruction of operator-relevant relations and praxis conditions that serves as a reference point for change, continuity, or transition claims.

This formulation strengthens the Chapter 1 object category in four respects.

**First, a configuration is temporally located.** It belongs to a declared time point, interval, phase, or bounded temporal condition. Temporal location does not require that the configuration be static. A meeting, review period, or institutional arrangement may remain internally active while still functioning as one configuration for the tested claim.

**Second, a configuration is relational.** It identifies not merely items that coexist, but the relations that matter to the analysis: roles, permissions, constraints, expectations, asymmetries, accessible actions, blocked actions, relevant residues, or bindings. A list of co-present elements is not yet a configuration unless the analysis declares why their relation matters.

**Third, a configuration is selective.** It is never a complete description of everything present at a time. It includes only those structures warranted by the frame, granularity, source scope, temporal scope, and claim scope declared for the reconstruction. Selection is not automatically defect; concealed selection is.

**Fourth, a configuration is reference-bearing.** It must be identifiable strongly enough to support comparison with another configuration or to serve as one endpoint of a transition claim. Chapter 5 later specifies full continuity requirements. Chapter 3 requires only that the configuration boundary, temporal location, and relevant relations be explicit enough for the local temporal claim.

The definition can be represented as a burden statement:

```text
bounded reference object
+ declared temporal location
+ frame-relative relevant relations
+ praxis-relevant conditions and action corridors
+ source and claim scope
+ selective incompleteness disclosed
→ configuration candidate
```

A configuration may be internally dynamic. Participants may speak, proposals may be revised, and local events may occur inside it. The configuration claim holds only if those internal changes do not yet alter the relations selected as constitutive for the tested configuration boundary. Whether an internal change remains within one configuration or warrants a new configuration is therefore claim-relative and source-dependent.

```text
internal activity
≠
automatic configuration change
```

Conversely, a configuration cannot be held artificially stable merely to avoid recognizing a relevant change. If a source-supported event alters the declared roles, constraints, expectations, cost distribution, or action corridors on which the configuration claim depends, the original configuration may no longer be adequate for the later interval.

#### Running case C3-CONF-01 — initial review configuration

Consider the bounded project-review source family carried forward from Chapters 1 and 2. The available material includes:

- a circulated agenda stating that a review rule will be discussed;
- a role list identifying one approval office and several recommending participants;
- a meeting transcript;
- minutes recording the adopted disposition;
- a written commitment to reconsider the rule within thirty days.

Before the rule is adopted, the relevant configuration may be reconstructed as follows:

```text
reference object:
the project-review arrangement during the agenda-governed pre-adoption interval

temporal location:
from formal opening of the review item until the recorded adoption decision

relevant relations:
one office retains final approval;
other participants may recommend and object;
the agenda constrains admissible matters;
no adopted reconsideration procedure yet exists

action corridors:
recommend, object, revise, approve, defer

selected asymmetry:
final approval capacity is unequally distributed

selected expectation:
a decision on the proposed review rule is due within the meeting item
```

This is a configuration claim because it identifies a bounded interval and reconstructs the relevant relation set that conditions action during that interval. It does not claim to describe the whole project, the complete institution, the participants as persons, or every interaction inside the meeting.

The local result may be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The bound is essential. The reconstruction is supported as a local review configuration, not as an exhaustive organizational ontology.

#### Configuration boundary failures

A configuration claim fails or requires reduction where:

- temporal location is absent or unconstrained;
- elements are merely listed without relevant relations;
- the frame or claim scope is hidden;
- a local arrangement is treated as a total description of the institution;
- a transient occurrence is converted into a permanent object property;
- missing sources are filled with assumed roles, motives, or conditions;
- internal change is ignored even though it alters the constitutive relation set.

The non-equivalences are:

```text
configuration
≠ static ontology
≠ complete world description
≠ exhaustive time-slice inventory
≠ person type
≠ automatically a composite produced by COMPOSE
```

A configuration can later become a source object for `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, or `PROJECT_AS`, but the label `configuration` establishes none of those operations.

### 3.2 State and Configuration

A **state** is a formal or compressed shorthand for a condition attributed to a reference object at a declared analytical time or interval. A state description may be adequate where the tested task requires only one limited status variable. It becomes inadequate when relations, expectations, non-events, internal dynamics, asymmetries, or action corridors carry the claim.

```text
state
→ compressed condition description

configuration
→ richer reconstruction of relevant relations and praxis conditions
```

The distinction is functional rather than hierarchical. STRATA does not prohibit state descriptions and does not assume that configuration is always better. The required question is:

> Does the tested claim depend on structure that the state shorthand omits?

A state may be sufficient for a narrowly formal question such as:

```text
Was the review rule recorded as adopted at the end of the meeting?
```

A state declaration may answer:

```text
rule_status = adopted
```

If the claim concerns only the recorded formal status at that time, the compressed state may be adequate. Expanding the description into every role, objection, delay, and asymmetry would add detail without necessarily changing the warranted answer.

A configuration is required when the question becomes:

```text
What relations made adoption possible,
what changed in the available action corridors,
and what obligations or expectations entered the later interval?
```

The state `adopted` cannot answer those questions. It suppresses the approval asymmetry, the objection process, the adopted reconsideration commitment, the distinction between immediate formal closure and later review, and the relation between recorded adoption and continuing procedural pressure.

#### Adequacy test

A state shorthand is locally adequate only where:

1. the reference object and temporal location are declared;
2. the selected state variable is supported by the source basis;
3. omitted relations are not material to the tested claim;
4. the shorthand is not treated as a full configuration;
5. later comparison does not depend on hidden ordering, expectation, or non-event structure.

Where these conditions hold, a finer configuration reconstruction may be:

```text
resolution_neutral
```

for the narrow state claim. That result does not mean the additional relations are unreal or irrelevant in every analysis. It means they do not alter the warranted answer to the declared narrow question.

A state shorthand requires reduction where it hides relevant structural density. The analysis must then either reconstruct a configuration or reduce the claim to what the shorthand actually supports.

#### C3-CONF-01 comparison — `adopted` as state, adoption arrangement as configuration

The same review material permits two different but non-competing descriptions:

| Description | Supported use | Omitted burden |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `rule_status = adopted` | narrow formal-status claim at the recorded meeting close | roles, objections, review commitment, later expectation, cost and asymmetry relations |
| adoption configuration | relation-bearing reconstruction of the meeting-close arrangement | still selective; does not establish later implementation or institutional recurrence |

The state description is not false merely because the configuration is richer. The configuration is not automatically more authoritative merely because it contains more distinctions.

```text
less descriptive density
≠ falsehood automatically

more descriptive density
≠ truth priority automatically
```

The choice between state and configuration is therefore governed by praxeological purchase and traceable load. Where the additional relations change the tested claim, configuration is required. Where they do not, the state shorthand may remain sufficient.

The chapter's preference is conditional:

```text
STRATA prefers configuration
where structural density is required by the claim.
```

It does not impose configuration as a universal replacement for every formal state representation.

### 3.3 Event

An **event** is a temporally specified, frame-relevant, positively realized change or occurrence that bears on the declared reconstruction.

Chapter 1 defined the broader eligibility category `event-like object`. Chapter 3 adds three temporal burdens:

- the realized change must be temporally located;
- its relevance to the declared configuration or transition claim must be stated;
- its realization must be source-supported rather than inferred from expectation alone.

The minimum burden is:

```text
bounded reference occurrence
+ declared temporal placement
+ positive realization
+ frame-relative relevance
+ source support
+ claim-bound identification
→ event candidate
```

An event need not be punctual. It may unfold over an interval. It may be simple for one claim and internally composite for another. It may be causally entangled with other occurrences. None of these features removes its event status.

```text
event
≠ necessarily instantaneous
≠ necessarily simple
≠ causal atom
≠ complete causal explanation
```

Positive realization also carries no automatic normative value. An event may realize a beneficial, harmful, neutral, contested, or merely procedural change. Event status answers whether the relevant occurrence was positively realized, not whether it was good, legitimate, mature, or justified.

```text
positive realization
≠ positive evaluation
```

#### C3-EVT-01 — adoption of the review rule

The review rule's adoption is a suitable event candidate.

```text
reference occurrence:
formal adoption of the review rule

temporal placement:
during the recorded decision item, completed at the documented vote and disposition

positive realization:
the rule moved from proposal to recorded adopted status

frame-relative relevance:
the event changes the procedural conditions under which later approvals and reconsideration are handled

source support:
transcript and minutes
```

The event may be described narrowly as the recorded adoption decision. Under finer granularity, it may include proposal presentation, objection, revision, vote, announcement, and minute entry. That finer reconstruction does not automatically turn the event into a transition or prove a causal chain. It opens the event's internal structure only if a later `DECOMPOSE` occurrence is warranted.

The event changes one or more conditions relevant to later configurations, but it is not identical to the transition between them.

```text
event
→ may contribute to transition

event
≠ transition
```

The adoption event is one realized component. The transition claim must additionally identify the before and after configurations, their temporal relation, the intervening structure, and the changed praxis conditions.

#### Extended-event pressure

Suppose adoption is not complete at one instant because the proposal is approved orally, recorded in draft minutes, corrected, and formally confirmed the following day. The event may be reconstructed as an extended event if the source-supported process forms one bounded realization for the tested claim.

That decision requires:

- a declared event boundary;
- a relation among the constituent occurrences;
- a reason to treat them as one realization rather than several independent events;
- disclosed uncertainty where the completion point is contested.

The mere presence of several timestamps neither forbids nor proves one extended event.

```text
several timestamps
≠ several events automatically
≠ one extended event automatically
```

#### Event inflation failures

An event claim requires reduction or Stop where:

- only an expectation is documented but realization is not;
- a source gap is treated as evidence that an event occurred;
- a change in wording is treated as a substantive event without praxeological purchase;
- an event label is used to imply causal isolation or complete explanation;
- a local event is projected into a permanent institutional or person-level property;
- the event is inferred solely because two configurations differ.

The final failure is especially important. Endpoint difference may indicate that something changed. It does not identify which event occurred or whether the change relation is reconstructible.

### 3.4 Non-Event

A **non-event** is a temporally bounded, source-supported non-realization of an identifiable expected occurrence under a warranted expectation relation, where the non-realization carries praxeological load within the declared reconstruction.

This definition is stricter than ordinary absence. A non-event cannot be established merely because something did not appear in the record, because an analyst can imagine that it might have happened, or because a later configuration differs from an earlier one.

The minimum burden is:

```text
identifiable expected occurrence
+ warranted expectation relation
+ bounded realization condition or window
+ source-supported non-realization
+ praxeological load
+ relevance to the temporal claim
→ non-event candidate
```

Each term is necessary.

**Identifiable expected occurrence.** The analysis must state what was expected to occur. “Nothing happened” is not a non-event description.

**Warranted expectation relation.** The expectation must arise from a commitment, rule, schedule, role, recurrent procedure, explicit plan, or other source-supported relation. Analyst preference or retrospective surprise is insufficient.

**Bounded realization condition.** The analysis must identify when, under what condition, or within what interval realization was due. An open-ended possibility cannot become a non-event merely because it has not yet occurred.

**Source-supported non-realization.** The source basis must support the claim that realization did not occur within the bound. Missing records may leave the issue unknown. They do not prove non-realization.

**Praxeological load.** The non-realization must alter or preserve relevant action corridors, costs, expectations, asymmetries, residues, bindings, or continuation possibilities. Trivial non-occurrence is not automatically a STRATA object.

**Temporal-chain relevance.** The non-event must matter to the declared configuration, transition, or later chain. It is not enough that it can be named.

The core distinction remains:

```text
missing information
≠ non-event
```

Additional non-equivalences are:

```text
ordinary absence
≠ structured non-event

unknown occurrence
≠ established non-realization

analyst surprise
≠ warranted expectation

conceivable alternative
≠ expected occurrence
```

#### C3-NON-01 — promised reconsideration not realized within the stated window

The adopted review rule includes a written commitment to reconsider the procedure within thirty days. The record contains reminders, an acknowledgement of the commitment, and scheduling exchanges. No reconsideration meeting or written disposition occurs within the specified period, and the source bundle is sufficient to support that bounded non-realization.

The candidate can be reconstructed as:

```text
expected occurrence:
reconsideration of the adopted review rule

expectation relation:
written thirty-day commitment recorded in the minutes and acknowledged later

realization window:
thirty days after adoption

non-realization support:
complete scheduling and disposition records for the window show no review or written reconsideration outcome

praxeological load:
the adopted rule remains operative;
participants continue under the initial approval asymmetry;
procedural cost shifts to those seeking reconsideration;
the open commitment becomes residue rather than completed review
```

This non-event may become central to a later path or trajectory, but WP1 does not yet make that stronger claim. It establishes only the temporal non-event object and its possible relevance.

Positive sub-events do not eliminate the non-event. Reminders were sent, the commitment was acknowledged, and scheduling messages occurred. Those are realized events inside the interval. The governing expected occurrence—reconsideration—still failed to realize.

```text
positive sub-events
≠ realization of the expected governing event
```

The bounded result may be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The claim remains limited to the stated window and source-supported non-realization. It does not establish motive, bad faith, permanent refusal, future non-performance, or institution-wide practice.

#### C3-NON-02 — missing later record

Consider a weaker source bundle. It contains the original commitment but no later scheduling archive, no complete meeting calendar, and no disposition record. The analyst finds no document showing that reconsideration occurred.

That is not enough.

```text
no record found
≠ record of non-occurrence
```

The warranted result is initially:

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

The claim may be reduced to:

```text
the available source bundle does not establish whether reconsideration occurred within the stated window.
```

If the analysis insists on calling the gap a non-event, the result is:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

Re-entry requires a source basis capable of supporting bounded non-realization, not merely continued absence from the current bundle.

This Stop is positive. It preserves the difference between evidentiary uncertainty and structured non-realization. It does not imply that reconsideration did occur.

#### Non-event accumulation boundary

Several non-events may later accumulate into historical load, but repetition alone is not yet sedimentation. WP3 will define sedimentation, and PATH will operationalize cumulative non-event roles. WP1 preserves only the possibility:

```text
non-event may carry cumulative load
≠ every repeated absence is sedimentation
≠ every delay is trajectory-forming
```

### 3.5 Transition

A **transition** is a bounded, temporally ordered relation between identifiable configurations in which source-supported realized and/or non-realized structure changes the praxis conditions relevant to the declared claim.

A transition is therefore neither an empty arrow nor a synonym for one event. It is a relation-bearing object.

The minimum burden is:

```text
identifiable source configuration
+ identifiable target configuration
+ supported temporal order
+ intervening realized and/or non-realized structure
+ changed relevant praxis conditions
+ declared transition boundary
+ source trace and claim scope
→ transition candidate
```

The transition relation may include one event, several events, one or more non-events, or a combination. Causal completeness is not required. The analysis may remain agnostic about every causal mechanism while still reconstructing a supported relation between configurations. What is required is enough source-supported intervening structure to show that the transition is more than a comparison of endpoints.

```text
transition
≠ endpoint difference
≠ event
≠ complete causal explanation
≠ sequence
≠ path
```

#### Source and target configurations

A transition claim must identify what relations make each endpoint one configuration for the tested purpose. The source and target configurations need not be exhaustive, but they must be comparable under a declared frame, granularity, and claim scope.

The configurations may differ in:

- role or approval relations;
- expectations and obligations;
- available or blocked actions;
- asymmetrical cost or exposure;
- binding or residue;
- procedural conditions;
- accessible continuation possibilities.

A change in one recorded variable may be insufficient if it does not alter the praxis conditions relevant to the claim. Conversely, a transition may be warranted even where no dramatic event occurs, if a supported non-event changes the effective conditions under which later action proceeds.

#### C3-TRN-01 — transition into the adopted review arrangement

The review material supports a transition from the initial configuration `C0` to a post-adoption configuration `C1`.

```text
C0:
review proposal under the prior approval arrangement;
no adopted reconsideration procedure;
participants may recommend and object;
final approval remains concentrated

intervening structure:
proposal discussion;
objection and revision;
formal adoption event E1;
recorded thirty-day reconsideration commitment

C1:
new review rule is operative;
subsequent approvals are processed under that rule;
a bounded expectation of reconsideration now exists;
the original approval asymmetry remains but is procedurally reconfigured
```

The transition claim concerns the changed review conditions, not merely the fact that a vote occurred. The adoption event contributes to the relation. The recorded commitment changes the expectation structure. The target configuration contains a new procedural corridor and a new possible non-event condition.

The local transition relation can be stated:

```text
C0
-- discussion, revision, adoption, and recorded commitment -->
C1
```

This notation is illustrative only. The arrow does not replace the intervening source trace.

The claim may be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

because the source and target configurations, temporal order, intervening structure, and changed praxis conditions are declared. The result does not establish complete causality, institutional permanence, or later compliance.

#### Event versus transition

The adoption event `E1` and transition `T0→1` are related but non-identical.

| Event claim | Transition claim |
| --- | --- |
| the review rule was positively adopted | the review arrangement changed from `C0` to `C1` through the declared intervening structure |
| may be punctual or extended | necessarily relates configurations across temporal order |
| identifies realized occurrence | identifies changed praxis conditions and relation |
| does not require full endpoint reconstruction | requires source and target configuration burden |
| may contribute to several transitions under different claims | may contain several events and non-events |

A single event may participate in several transition claims under different frames. For example, adoption may contribute to a procedural transition and a responsibility-allocation transition. Those are new testable claims with different configuration relations. The event label alone does not authorize them.

#### C3-TRN-02 — endpoint difference without transition support

Suppose only two status snapshots survive:

```text
Day 1: rule_status = proposed
Day 30: rule_status = operative
```

No transcript, decision record, procedural history, or complete source trace connects them. The endpoints differ. A change occurred in some sense. But the source bundle does not establish:

- which event or non-event intervened;
- whether the same rule is referenced;
- how the operative status was produced;
- which praxis conditions changed;
- whether the relation is adoption, replacement, administrative correction, or another process.

The warranted result is:

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

The claim may be reduced to:

```text
the two records present different bounded statuses;
the transition relation is not established by the available sources.
```

If the analysis insists that the endpoint difference itself proves the transition, the result is:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

The Stop does not deny that a transition may have occurred. It denies that the stronger transition object has been reconstructed.

```text
state difference
≠ transition evidence automatically
```

#### Transition boundary and handoff

WP1 establishes only the transition object burden. It does not yet establish a sequence, path, or trajectory. Several transitions can be ordered without forming a path, and one transition can be represented under several valid granularities without identifying an operation.

The WP1 burden chain is therefore:

```text
configuration
→ reference point for temporal comparison

event / non-event
→ realized or structured non-realized temporal object

transition
→ supported relation between configurations through changed praxis conditions
```

The next work package must add the stronger burdens for sequence, path, and trajectory. It may not weaken the distinctions established here.

#### WP1 result

Sections 3.1–3.5 establish:

- configuration as bounded, temporally located, relational, selective, and reference-bearing;
- state as compressed shorthand whose adequacy depends on the tested claim;
- event as positively realized, temporally specified, frame-relevant change without punctuality or causal-atom inflation;
- non-event as supported structured non-realization under a warranted expectation and bounded realization condition;
- transition as a relation between configurations requiring intervening structure and changed praxis conditions;
- missing information as insufficient for non-event;
- endpoint difference as insufficient for transition;
- event and transition as non-confusable;
- local claim reduction and Mandatory Stop without invented temporal certainty.

The WP1 result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality attaches to pending Sections 3.6–3.13 and the integrated Chapter 3 lock. It does not weaken the temporal object boundaries established in this work package.

### 3.6 Sequence

A **sequence** is a selected, temporally ordered analytical series whose units and ordering basis are explicitly declared.

Sequence adds a stronger burden than the mere existence of several dated records. It requires the analysis to identify what is being ordered, why those units belong in one series for the tested claim, and which temporal relation establishes their order. It does not yet require the constitutive historical connectedness, actual-traversal burden, branch disclosure, or selective reconstruction required for a path.

```text
more than one dated item
+ explicit analytical units
+ declared ordering basis
+ supported order
+ bounded frame and claim scope
→ sequence candidate
```

The units of a sequence may be configurations, events, non-events, transitions, or a declared combination of them. Mixed units are admissible only where their roles are stated. A sequence that silently treats configurations, events, and transitions as interchangeable loses the very distinctions established in Sections 3.1–3.5.

```text
chronology
≠ sequence automatically

sequence
≠ path
≠ trajectory
≠ causal chain
```

#### Chronology and analytical order

A **chronology** records that items are dated or placed before and after one another. A sequence makes a further analytical selection. It declares which items are relevant to the claim and which ordering relation is being used.

At minimum, the ordering basis should be one of the following or another equally explicit relation:

- **source order** — the order in which records appear in a source;
- **record order** — the order in which documents, entries, or traces were produced or stored;
- **event order** — the supported temporal order of realized events;
- **transition order** — the supported order of changes between configurations;
- **expectation-window order** — the relation between an expected occurrence, its realization condition, and supported non-realization;
- **analyst presentation order** — an expository order that may assist explanation but does not by itself establish historical order.

These orders may coincide. They need not. Minutes may list agenda items in one order while the underlying events occurred in another. A later-produced summary may narrate earlier events. An analyst may present the decisive transition first and then reconstruct its preconditions. Such differences are not errors if they are declared.

```text
presentation order
≠ historical order automatically

record order
≠ event order automatically

supported before/after relation
≠ causal determination
```

A sequence claim therefore includes an ordering statement and an ordering-source statement. Where temporal order remains uncertain, the sequence may be provisional, partially ordered, or reduced to a chronology of what is securely dated.

A sequence need not force a total order where the sources support only a partial order. Two reminders may both occur after adoption and before the end of the realization window while their relation to each other remains unknown. The correct representation preserves that uncertainty.

```text
E1 precedes R1 and R2
R1 and R2 precede the end of the window
R1 ? R2
```

The unknown relation does not invalidate the entire sequence. It limits the strength of claims that depend on the internal order. A total-order assertion without support may require claim reduction even where a partial sequence remains admissible.

Sequence construction must also preserve unit identity. The same record can support several analytical units under different claims, but those units cannot be silently substituted. A meeting record may support an event claim, contribute to a transition claim, and provide a source trace for a sequence. Those are related uses, not one undifferentiated temporal fact.

```text
one record
→ may support several declared temporal claims

one record
≠ one self-interpreting temporal object
```

#### C3-SEQ-01 — ordered review series

The running project-review material supports the following bounded analytical units:

```text
C0  initial review configuration
E1  adoption of the review rule
C1  adopted-rule configuration with a recorded reconsideration commitment
N1  reconsideration not realized within the supported thirty-day window
C2  post-window configuration in which the rule remains operative and the commitment remains unresolved
```

The ordering basis is not merely the layout of the archive. It is supported event and transition order:

```text
C0
→ E1
→ C1
→ N1 within the bounded realization window
→ C2
```

This is a sequence because:

- the units are explicitly typed;
- their temporal order is source-supported;
- the selection is relevant to the claim about the review arrangement;
- the temporal and source scopes are declared;
- uncertainty about motives, complete causality, and later developments remains excluded.

The local sequence claim may be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The result does not yet establish a path. It does not state why this selected series is the actually traversed constitutive chain rather than one useful ordered presentation among several. It also does not yet state what branches, rejected continuations, omitted events, or compression losses matter.

#### Sequence insufficiency and reduction

Consider an archive list containing:

```text
Day 1   proposal document created
Day 3   agenda circulated
Day 7   meeting held
Day 9   minutes uploaded
Day 22  reminder sent
Day 30  calendar entry absent
```

The list is chronological. It may support a record sequence. But without a declared analytical unit, relation to configurations and transitions, or relevance rule, it does not establish the historical object that later PATH work would require.

The warranted reduction may be:

```text
the available materials support a bounded chronology of records;
they do not yet support a path claim.
```

A sequence remains useful even when a path cannot be established. Failure of the stronger object does not erase the weaker ordered result.

### 3.7 Path

A **path** is the selectively reconstructed, actually traversed chain of relevant configurations and transitions within a declared frame, granularity, temporal scope, source scope, and claim scope.

Path adds actual traversal and constitutive connectedness to sequence. It does not merely place units in order. It reconstructs why the selected configurations and transitions form one historically traversed chain for the tested claim.

```text
warranted sequence
+ bounded reference object
+ actually traversed configurations and transitions
+ declared selection rule
+ constitutive relation among selected units
+ source trace for traversal
+ branch, omission, and loss disclosure
→ path candidate
```

A path may contain events and non-events, but it is not a list of them. Events and non-events matter insofar as they contribute to transitions, block or defer continuations, alter the selected chain, or leave open residue. A path may also identify branch points, rejected continuations, deferred continuations, and supported unavailable alternatives. Their mere conceivability is insufficient; WP3 will specify the stronger alternative burden.

```text
sequence
≠ path

path
≠ complete chronology
≠ complete causal history
≠ trajectory
≠ path dependence
```

#### Actual traversal

“Actually traversed” is a source and claim burden. It does not require omniscient causal reconstruction. It requires enough trace to show that the selected chain was realized rather than merely possible, retrospectively plausible, or narratively convenient.

A path claim must therefore distinguish:

- **realized traversal** from a conceivable route;
- **selected relevance** from archival completeness;
- **branch point** from an imagined alternative;
- **blocked or deferred continuation** from missing information;
- **open endpoint** from completed closure;
- **path reconstruction** from teleological retrospective storytelling.

The path may remain open. An endpoint is the end of the declared reconstruction, not necessarily the end of the historical process.

A path may also include a blocked, aborted, or deferred continuation where the status is source-supported. These are not unrealized alternatives merely because they did not become the realized continuation. A blocked continuation requires evidence of a relevant route and of the condition that prevented traversal. A deferred continuation requires evidence that the route remained open but was postponed. An aborted continuation requires evidence that traversal began or was authorized and then ceased.

```text
blocked continuation
≠ missing record

deferred continuation
≠ permanent non-realization

aborted continuation
≠ merely conceivable branch
```

The distinction matters because each status changes the path claim differently. A blocked branch may reveal constraint; a deferred branch may preserve later accessibility; an aborted branch may leave sunk cost or residue. Chapter 13 will provide the full branch and alternative discipline. WP2 only requires that a path not invent branch status from silence.

#### Selection, compression, and loss

Every path is selective. Selection is not a defect if it is disclosed and justified.

A path declaration should state:

```text
included configurations and transitions
compressed intermediate structure
excluded records or branches
uncertain relations
irrecoverable gaps
open residue
```

The selection rule must possess praxeological purchase. It may select units because they change approval relations, expectation structures, asymmetric costs, action corridors, bindings, or continuation possibilities. It may not select only the items that make a preferred retrospective narrative look inevitable.

The full `COMPOSE` procedure remains the primary responsibility of Chapter 15. Chapter 3 defines the path object burden. It does not decide that every path construction is automatically `COMPOSE`, does not specify the full formation procedure, and does not replace later PATH audit.

```text
path object burden
≠ completed COMPOSE procedure

ordered source material
≠ automatic path formation
```

The path object and the claim about that object must also remain distinct. A source-supported path may be admissible while a stronger claim about why that path occurred, what it proves, or how generally it recurs fails. The failure of causal, normative, or institutional generalization does not by itself invalidate the bounded path reconstruction.

```text
warranted path object
≠ warranted causal explanation
≠ warranted recurrence claim
≠ warranted evaluation
```

#### C3-PATH-01 — the traversed review path

The review material supports a bounded path from the initial proposal arrangement to the post-window configuration.

```text
P_review:

C0  proposal under the prior approval arrangement

T0→1
  discussion, revision, adoption event E1,
  and recorded reconsideration commitment

C1  adopted review rule with a bounded expectation of reconsideration

T1→2
  reminders and acknowledgements occur,
  but the governing reconsideration event does not occur
  within the supported thirty-day window

C2  rule remains operative;
    the commitment remains unresolved;
    procedural burden shifts toward those seeking reconsideration
```

The path-selection rule is:

```text
include configurations and transitions that alter
review authority, reconsideration expectations,
procedural burden, or accessible continuation.
```

The reconstruction compresses routine scheduling messages and excludes unrelated project work. It preserves the adoption event, the expectation-bearing commitment, the supported non-event, and the changed post-window conditions because they carry the tested praxis relation.

The path is actually traversed to the extent supported by the agenda, transcript, minutes, reminder records, scheduling materials, and disposition records within the declared source scope. The path does not claim complete causality or institutional recurrence.

The local result may be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

This path remains valid without a strong path-dependence claim. It also remains possible that another locally valid path reconstruction selects a different, source-supported relation—for example, responsibility allocation rather than procedural review—provided that its frame, selection, loss, and claim scope are separately declared.

#### Same endpoint, different path

Two historical chains may reach a formally similar endpoint while carrying different relations, costs, residues, or bindings.

```text
same endpoint status
≠ same path
```

A rule may be operative after open deliberation and fulfilled reconsideration, or after adoption followed by an unresolved commitment. The endpoint label `operative` does not preserve those differences. Path reconstruction exists precisely because current-state identity can compress historically relevant structure.

This does not mean that every difference in route is relevant to every claim. The path must show why the selected difference changes the tested reconstruction.

#### C3-PATH-02 — chronology without path

Suppose the available bundle supports only the following:

```text
proposal dated Day 1
meeting dated Day 7
rule marked operative on Day 12
reminder dated Day 22
```

The dates establish order. They do not establish:

- source and target configurations;
- the transitions connecting them;
- whether the same rule is referenced throughout;
- the relation between the reminder and a warranted expectation;
- which continuation was actually traversed;
- which branches were available, blocked, or merely imagined.

The warranted result is:

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

The material may support a chronology or limited sequence. It does not yet support a path.

If the analysis insists that chronological order alone proves actual traversal and constitutive connectedness, the result is:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

Re-entry requires a stronger source trace and a declared selection rule. Changing the frame, granularity, or label does not rescue the failed path claim.

### 3.8 Trajectory

A **trajectory** is a path whose source-supported sedimented historical load conditions later meaning, costs, accessibility, role relations, asymmetries, bindings, residue, or continuation possibilities.

A trajectory is not simply a longer path, a more detailed chronology, or a visually directional line. It requires a historical load that remains active in later configurations.

```text
warranted path
+ source-supported sedimented historical load
+ traceable effect on later praxis conditions
+ bounded trajectory claim
→ trajectory candidate
```

The additional burden is therefore not duration but retained structural consequence.

```text
long path
≠ trajectory automatically

duration
≠ sedimentation

trajectory
≠ path dependence automatically
```

WP3 will define sedimentation and path dependence in their own right. WP2 uses only the minimum trajectory burden needed to distinguish a trajectory from a path: earlier path structure must remain traceably relevant to later meaning or continuation.

#### Sedimented historical load at the trajectory threshold

Historical load may be carried by, among other relations:

- recurrent stabilization that makes one continuation easier or more expected;
- accumulated asymmetric cost or exposure;
- unresolved commitments or repeated non-realizations;
- bindings that persist, harden, or become expensive to revise;
- residues that alter how later events are interpreted;
- prior branch closure that changes later accessibility;
- institutional or procedural memory preserved in records, roles, or routines.

These are candidate carriers, not automatic formulas. `Α + Θ`, `Ω + Θ`, `Ψ + Θ`, or `Λ + Θ` may support a sedimentation claim where the relevant occurrence relations are source-supported. Operator co-presence alone does not establish a trajectory.

```text
Θ occurrence
≠ trajectory

operator pairing
≠ sufficient historical proof
```

A trajectory may be reconstructed under a bounded frame even where some internal events remain uncertain. It must disclose uncertainty rather than convert it into historical necessity.

The retained load must be relevant to the tested later configuration. Historical detail that survives in an archive but does not change the claim is not automatically sedimentation. Likewise, a later actor's awareness of earlier events is neither necessary nor sufficient. Load may persist through rules, costs, role relations, available procedures, records, expectations, or residues even where no participant explicitly narrates the history. Conversely, vivid collective memory may remain rhetorically present without materially conditioning the bounded praxis claim.

```text
historical remembrance
≠ sedimented structural load automatically

archival persistence
≠ trajectory relevance automatically

participant unawareness
≠ absence of structural carry-over automatically
```

Trajectory reconstruction therefore asks not only whether the past is mentioned, but whether the path has changed the conditions under which later praxis becomes meaningful, costly, accessible, constrained, or binding.

#### C3-TRAJ-01 — sedimented review trajectory

The path `P_review` becomes a trajectory candidate only when later material supports the continuing load of the prior path.

Assume the bounded source set additionally establishes that:

- the adopted rule remains the default procedure in later review rounds;
- later requests for reconsideration are interpreted against the earlier unresolved commitment;
- participants seeking review must repeatedly restate or document a burden that was previously left open;
- approval remains concentrated while the cost of reopening the matter increases;
- later procedural choices cite or presuppose the adopted arrangement and its unresolved residue.

The trajectory may be reconstructed as:

```text
initial approval asymmetry
→ adoption of a review rule and reconsideration commitment
→ supported non-realization within the stated window
→ unresolved commitment becomes procedural residue
→ later review requests carry increased explanation and reopening cost
→ continuation possibilities remain shaped by the accumulated path
```

The trajectory claim does not assert that every later decision was caused by the initial adoption or non-event. It states that the prior path remains structurally active in later meaning, cost, and accessibility.

The local result may be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The claim is bounded to the declared review process, source set, and later interval. It does not establish institution-wide culture, motive, inevitability, or a universal development law.

#### Path versus trajectory

| Path burden | Additional trajectory burden |
| --- | --- |
| actually traversed selected chain | prior chain remains active in later praxis conditions |
| configurations and transitions connected | sedimented historical load is source-supported |
| selection, branches, compression, and loss declared | later meaning, cost, accessibility, binding, residue, or continuation changes |
| may end at a declared open endpoint | present or later configuration carries historical conditioning |
| may remain valid without strong persistence | requires traceable retained load |

A path may be historically important without qualifying as a trajectory for the tested claim. A long-running process may contain no relevant sedimentation. Conversely, a relatively short path may create a strong trajectory if a binding decision, asymmetric cost, or unresolved non-event materially structures later continuation.

The same path can also carry unequal trajectory strength for different claims. It may strongly condition procedural accessibility while only weakly conditioning substantive outcome. Trajectory status is therefore claim-relative but not arbitrary: the declared historical load must connect to the specific later relation being asserted.

```text
one path
→ may support several bounded trajectory claims

trajectory strength in one relation
≠ trajectory strength in every relation
```

This claim-relativity prevents a locally supported trajectory from expanding into an unrestricted historical master explanation.

#### C3-TELE-01 — directionality without teleology

The review trajectory may show directionality:

```text
reconsideration becomes progressively more costly to reopen;
the adopted arrangement becomes easier to reproduce;
unresolved residue becomes increasingly relevant to later review claims.
```

This directionality does not establish a goal, destiny, purpose, developmental endpoint, or inevitable continuation.

```text
directionality
≠ teleology

stabilized continuation pressure
≠ predetermined outcome
```

Several later developments remain possible within the bounded reconstruction:

- the commitment may be fulfilled;
- the rule may be revised;
- a new source may reopen the claim;
- the process may branch into a different review arrangement;
- later evidence may reduce or overturn the trajectory reconstruction.

A teleological formulation such as “the process was always moving toward permanent closure” exceeds the sources. The warranted response is:

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

The claim may be reduced to a bounded statement of directional continuation pressure. If the analysis refuses reduction and treats direction as necessary purpose or inevitable outcome, the result is:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

The Stop preserves historical openness. It does not deny that the trajectory is directional.

#### Competing trajectory pressure

The same source-supported path may support more than one bounded trajectory reconstruction under different declared claims. One reconstruction may foreground procedural sedimentation; another may foreground responsibility displacement or accumulated asymmetric cost.

```text
several trajectory candidates
≠ one unrestricted master trajectory
```

Local co-validity requires that each candidate disclose its frame, selection, support, loss, and rival relation. Under-determination must remain visible. WP3 will provide the stronger property tests and Non-Capture route where the available sources do not warrant one decisive trajectory or path-dependence claim.

#### WP2 boundary and handoff

Sections 3.6–3.8 establish the burden-increasing distinction:

```text
chronology
→ dated or ordered record

sequence
→ selected units under a declared ordering basis

path
→ actually traversed, selectively reconstructed chain
  of relevant configurations and transitions

trajectory
→ path carrying source-supported sedimented historical load
  that changes later praxis conditions
```

The progression is analytical, not automatic:

```text
chronology
≠ sequence automatically

sequence
≠ path automatically

path
≠ trajectory automatically

trajectory
≠ path dependence automatically
```

WP2 does not yet define path dependence, sedimentation as a full historical-property claim, frame-bound irreversibility, supported unrealized alternatives, or the Minimal Temporal Object Chain. Those remain WP3 responsibilities.

WP2 also does not duplicate the full `COMPOSE` procedure, PATH branch taxonomy, operation selection, continuity architecture, general admissibility judgment, or Shared Transformation Record.

The WP2 result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality attaches to pending Sections 3.9–3.13 and the integrated Chapter 3 lock. It does not weaken the sequence, path, or trajectory distinctions established here.


### 3.9 Path Dependence

**Path dependence** is a bounded property claim about the historical determination of a present or later configuration. It states that the tested configuration, relation, cost, meaning, accessibility, or continuation possibility cannot be adequately reconstructed from current conditions alone because prior order, branch selection, or traversed path remains materially relevant.

Path dependence is not a further object class after trajectory. A trajectory may be the object to which the property is attributed, but the property requires its own support.

```text
trajectory
≠ path dependence

path dependence
≠ a separate temporal object class
```

The minimum burden is:

```text
bounded present or later claim
+ warranted trajectory or historical path
+ current-conditions insufficiency
+ supported prior-order or branch relevance
+ counterfactual sensitivity to a supported alternative order
+ traceable historical carry-over
+ declared property scope and uncertainty
→ path-dependence candidate
```

Each component carries a separate duty.

- **Bounded present or later claim** identifies what is said to depend on the prior path. The property may concern reopening cost, role allocation, interpretive meaning, procedural accessibility, binding, residue, or another declared relation. It is not automatically global.
- **Warranted trajectory or historical path** prevents path dependence from being inferred from a current snapshot alone.
- **Current-conditions insufficiency** requires that the tested present relation cannot be fully reconstructed by present variables without reference to the prior path.
- **Prior-order or branch relevance** requires evidence that the order, selection, or traversal mattered rather than merely preceded the current configuration.
- **Counterfactual sensitivity** asks whether a supported alternative order or branch would materially alter the bounded claim. It does not authorize unconstrained speculation.
- **Traceable historical carry-over** identifies the mechanism or relation through which the prior path remains active.
- **Declared property scope and uncertainty** prevent a local historical dependence from becoming a general determinism claim.

Path dependence can therefore be strong in one relation and weak or absent in another. The same trajectory may strongly condition the cost of reopening a procedure while leaving the substantive outcome comparatively open.

```text
path dependence in relation R1
≠ path dependence in every relation
```

#### Current conditions and historical determination

A path-dependence claim does not require that present conditions be irrelevant. It requires that present conditions alone are insufficient for the tested reconstruction.

Suppose two review processes currently have the same formal rule, the same nominal deadline, and the same listed decision authority. One process arrived there through a fulfilled reconsideration commitment; the other through repeated deferral and an unresolved commitment. The present formal snapshot may be similar while reopening cost, expectation, and responsibility residue differ because of the traversed path.

This supports the question:

```text
Could the present bounded relation be reconstructed
without the prior order or branch history?
```

A negative answer is not enough by itself. The analysis must show why the historical difference remains load-bearing. Mere narrative richness, chronological priority, or analyst preference does not establish path dependence.

```text
past relevance
≠ path dependence automatically

historical explanation
≠ determinism
```

#### Counterfactual sensitivity without fiction

Counterfactual sensitivity is necessary because a property claim about historical dependence must distinguish meaningful path effects from decorative historical background. The counterfactual must remain source-bounded.

A supported test may compare:

- the actually traversed branch with a documented rejected branch;
- the actual order with a recorded alternative order that was available at the relevant time;
- the present configuration with another source-supported case sharing current conditions but carrying a different prior path;
- a declared restoration criterion with the historically produced cost or residue that prevents cost-equivalent return.

The test may not invent an unconstrained alternative merely because it would make the argument persuasive.

```text
analyst-imaginable alternative
≠ supported counterfactual comparator
```

Where no supported alternative order or branch can be established, the path-dependence claim may remain provisional or require reduction. The trajectory itself may still be warranted.

#### C3-PD-01 — trajectory with weak path dependence

Return to the review trajectory established in `C3-TRAJ-01`:

```text
rule adoption
→ reconsideration commitment
→ supported non-realization within the stated window
→ unresolved residue
→ later review requests carry increased reopening cost
```

The sources support a bounded trajectory because earlier path structure remains active in later procedural meaning and cost. They do not, however, establish that a different supported order would necessarily have produced a materially different later outcome. The archive contains no sufficiently comparable alternative sequence and does not show that current conditions alone could not reproduce the same reopening cost.

The correct result is split:

```text
trajectory claim
→ admissible_with_bounded_claim

strong path-dependence claim
→ claim_reduction_required
```

The trajectory is retained. The stronger property is reduced to:

> The prior path is historically relevant to the present reopening cost, but strong counterfactual dependence on that path remains unestablished.

If the analysis insists that the trajectory is necessarily path-dependent because it lasted over time or accumulated residue, the result is:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

Re-entry requires a bounded property claim, a supported comparator or alternative order, and traceable evidence that present conditions alone are insufficient.

#### Path dependence, determinism, and teleology

Path dependence does not imply that only one future remains possible. Nor does it imply that the prior path was directed toward the current configuration.

```text
path dependence
≠ determinism
≠ inevitability
≠ teleology
```

A path-dependent present can still permit several future branches. Historical determination may alter costs, accessibility, or meaning without eliminating agency, contingency, or revision.

`Θ` supports temporal ordering. It does not establish the property.

```text
Θ
≠ path dependence
```

Likewise, `Α + Θ`, `Ω + Θ`, `Ψ + Θ`, or `Λ + Θ` may identify candidate carriers of historical dependence. Their co-presence is neither a sufficient formula nor a causal proof.

### 3.10 Sedimentation

**Sedimentation** is the accumulation and persistence of historically produced structural load such that prior occurrences, relations, bindings, asymmetries, non-realizations, or stabilizations remain relevant to later praxis conditions.

Sedimentation is a property of retained historical load. It is not elapsed time, repeated mention, archival survival, or a metaphor for anything old.

```text
duration
≠ sedimentation

historical age
≠ sedimented load

repetition
≠ accumulation automatically
```

The minimum burden is:

```text
identified historical contributions
+ accumulation or persistence relation
+ source-supported carrier
+ later praxis relevance
+ declared sedimentation scope
+ loss and uncertainty disclosure
→ sedimentation candidate
```

The analysis must identify what accumulated, how it persisted, where it is carried, and which later relation it changes.

#### Candidate carriers

PMS operator relations may guide reconstruction:

| Relation | Candidate sedimented load | What remains unproven |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Α + Θ` | recurrent stabilization, expectation, routine, or default reproduction | that recurrence is inevitable or causally complete |
| `Ω + Θ` | accumulated asymmetric cost, exposure, burden, access, or responsibility displacement | moral verdict, total power explanation, or path dependence by formula |
| `Ψ + Θ` | persistent, hardened, or increasingly costly binding | voluntary endorsement, permanent commitment, or absolute irreversibility |
| `Λ + Θ` | accumulated non-realization, delay, residue, unmet expectation, or closure pressure | non-event without expectation support or missing data as absence |

These relations are prompts. A sedimentation claim still requires occurrence-level support and a later praxis effect.

Sedimentation may be carried through:

- rules and procedures;
- role expectations;
- recurring allocation of cost or exposure;
- records and institutional memory;
- commitments that remain unresolved;
- routines that make one continuation easier than others;
- residues that alter later interpretation;
- branch closure that changes accessibility;
- repeated non-realizations that reshape expectation or burden.

The carrier need not be consciously represented by every participant. Conversely, explicit remembrance does not establish structural sedimentation if the remembered history does not affect the tested later relation.

```text
participant memory
≠ sedimentation automatically

participant unawareness
≠ absence of sedimentation automatically
```

#### Accumulation, persistence, and later relevance

Sedimentation can arise through accumulation, persistence, or both.

- **Accumulation** means that several historically produced loads combine, reinforce, or compound.
- **Persistence** means that an earlier load remains active across later configurations.

A single binding decision may sediment rapidly if it changes later accessibility or cost. A long sequence may contain little or no sedimentation relevant to the claim.

```text
short path
→ may carry strong sedimentation

long duration
→ may carry no relevant sedimentation
```

The analysis must avoid treating every historical trace as cumulative. Some effects dissipate, are repaired, become irrelevant, or are replaced. Sedimentation is therefore claim-relative and defeasible.

A valid claim states both the carrier and the later effect:

> The unresolved reconsideration commitment persisted through the review record and increased the explanation and reopening burden in later rounds.

An invalid inflation states only:

> The issue had existed for a long time, therefore it was deeply sedimented.

The second formulation confuses duration with structural load.

#### Sedimentation and trajectory

Trajectory requires sufficient sedimented load to distinguish it from path. Section 3.10 now defines the property more fully, but it does not turn sedimentation into a separate STRATA operation or a new PMS primitive.

```text
sedimentation
≠ COMPOSE
≠ a new operator type
≠ automatic trajectory formation
```
