Sedimentation may be uneven. A trajectory can carry strong sedimentation in procedural burden, weak sedimentation in role identity, and no supported sedimentation in substantive outcome.

Where the sources show persistence but not later relevance, the correct response is reduction:

```text
historical persistence
→ may remain

sedimentation claim
→ claim_reduction_required
```

If the analysis uses duration or operator co-presence as sufficient proof, the claim must stop.

### 3.11 Irreversibility

**Irreversibility** in STRATA is a bounded claim that a specified earlier relation, condition, cost distribution, accessibility state, or reference position cannot be restored, neutralized, or made cost-equivalent under the declared frame, claim, object relation, and restoration criterion.

It is not a metaphysical statement that reversal is impossible in every sense.

```text
bounded irreversibility
≠ absolute irreversibility
≠ metaphysical impossibility
```

A valid irreversibility declaration requires:

```text
bounded reference object
+ declared frame and claim scope
+ identified before/after relation
+ explicit restoration criterion
+ source-supported residual difference or cost
+ uncertainty and re-entry condition
→ bounded irreversibility candidate
```

The restoration criterion is indispensable. Without it, “irreversible” can mean anything from “cannot be literally undone” to “cannot be repaired without cost” or “cannot be returned to the same institutional meaning.” These are different claims.

Possible criteria include:

- restoration of a prior formal rule;
- restoration of cost-neutral access;
- restoration of a prior expectation relation;
- restoration of a prior role distribution;
- neutralization of an accumulated burden;
- return to an equivalent reference position;
- removal of a publicly recorded commitment or exposure.

A later formal reversal may satisfy one criterion while failing another.

```text
formal reversal
≠ full restoration

exit
≠ return to untouched origin

recontextualization
≠ erasure of prior load
```

#### C3-IRR-01 — exit does not restore a cost-neutral origin

Assume the review arrangement is later formally withdrawn. The original rule no longer governs future review requests. The sources also show that:

- the unresolved reconsideration commitment remains in the record;
- participants incurred repeated explanation and documentation costs;
- later requests continue to be interpreted against the prior review history;
- the withdrawal does not reimburse or neutralize the earlier asymmetric burden;
- the process cannot return to a state in which the prior commitment and its non-realization never structured expectations.

Under the restoration criterion **cost-equivalent return to the pre-adoption review relation**, the bounded irreversibility claim may be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The claim is not that no repair, reinterpretation, or institutional change is possible. It is that formal exit does not restore an untouched and cost-neutral origin for the tested relation.

A stronger formulation such as “the process is permanently and absolutely irreversible” exceeds the frame and must be reduced.

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

If the analysis refuses to identify a restoration criterion or treats any historical occurrence as metaphysically irreversible, the result is:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

Re-entry requires a bounded criterion and evidence of the residual difference that survives attempted restoration.

#### Irreversibility and loss

Irreversibility often exposes loss, but the two are not identical.

```text
loss
≠ irreversibility automatically

irreversibility
≠ total loss
```

A relation may be irreversibly changed while substantial functions, references, or alternatives remain. Conversely, an excluded or irrecoverable detail in a reconstruction does not by itself establish historical irreversibility in the object.

The later loss architecture must therefore record what is preserved, compressed, excluded, uncertain, and irrecoverable without turning every irrecoverable analytical detail into a metaphysical claim about the source object.

### 3.12 Unrealized Alternatives

An **unrealized alternative** is a source-supported continuation, branch, order, decision, or relation that was available or conditionally available within a bounded historical situation but was not traversed.

It is not any possibility the analyst can imagine after the fact.

```text
unrealized alternative
≠ unconstrained counterfactual fiction
≠ analyst preference
≠ missing information
```

The minimum burden is:

```text
bounded historical decision or branch point
+ identifiable alternative
+ source-supported availability relation
+ relevant time and conditions
+ non-traversal status
+ later relevance to the tested claim
+ uncertainty and loss disclosure
→ unrealized-alternative candidate
```

Availability may be:

- **available** — the alternative could be selected under the documented conditions;
- **conditionally available** — it required a stated approval, resource, threshold, or prior step;
- **delayed** — selection remained possible but was moved beyond the relevant interval;
- **blocked** — an identified condition prevented traversal;
- **rejected** — a decision or recorded choice excluded it;
- **rendered costlier** — prior path structure preserved formal availability while materially altering access;
- **later unavailable** — the alternative ceased to be available because a subsequent branch, binding, or condition changed.

These statuses are not interchangeable. Chapter 13 later owns the fuller branch, abort, delay, and unavailable-alternative taxonomy. Chapter 3 defines only the minimum historical burden needed to keep unrealized alternatives distinct from fiction.

#### C3-ALT-01 — recorded rejected alternative

During adoption of the review rule, the meeting record documents two proposals:

1. adopt the rule immediately and reconsider it within thirty days;
2. defer adoption until the review criteria and reopening procedure are specified.

The second proposal is discussed, formally rejected, and therefore not traversed. The sources establish that it was available at the decision point and identify the conditions it would have changed.

The alternative can be retained as:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

Its warranted content is limited:

> Deferral pending specification was a documented available alternative that was rejected at the adoption point.

The record does not establish what would certainly have happened under that branch. It therefore does not authorize:

```text
"the later non-event would have been avoided"
"the alternative would have produced a fairer outcome"
"the trajectory necessarily depended on rejection"
```

Those stronger claims require separate evidence.

Where a source mentions only a vague wish, hypothetical future option, or analyst reconstruction, the result may be:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

or:

```text
claim_reduction_required
```

If availability is wholly unsupported, the alternative claim must stop.

#### Alternative relevance and historical closure

Unrealized alternatives matter because actual paths are selective. A path claim that suppresses supported rejected or blocked branches may make the actual sequence appear inevitable.

```text
actual traversal
≠ only historically possible traversal
```

At the same time, listing every imaginable branch destroys traceability. The chapter therefore requires a bounded alternative set tied to source-supported availability and the tested claim.

A historically available alternative may later become unavailable. Its later unavailability can contribute to path-dependence or irreversibility claims, but it does not establish them automatically.

```text
later unavailable alternative
≠ path dependence automatically
≠ irreversibility automatically
```

### 3.13 Minimal Temporal Object Chain

The **Minimal Temporal Object Chain** integrates the burdens established in Sections 3.1–3.12 without turning the chain into an automatic derivation, complete causal history, `COMPOSE` procedure, or Shared Transformation Record.

```text
configuration
→ transition
→ sequence
→ path
→ trajectory

path dependence
= separately tested property
```

Each arrow means **additional burden required**, not “the prior object necessarily becomes the next.”

| Stage | Minimum retained burden | Additional burden at this stage | What remains unestablished |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| configuration | bounded temporally located relevant relations | operator-relevant praxis conditions under declared coordinates and scopes | transition, order, traversal |
| transition | source and target configurations | supported intervening structure and changed praxis conditions | sequence, path, complete cause |
| sequence | selected temporal units | declared ordering basis and supported order | actual traversal, constitutive path |
| path | warranted sequence | actual traversal, selection rule, connectedness, source trace, branch/omission and loss disclosure | sedimented trajectory, path dependence |
| trajectory | warranted path | source-supported sedimented load affecting later praxis conditions | strong path dependence, teleology, determinism |
| path dependence | warranted historical object | current-conditions insufficiency, prior-order/branch relevance, supported counterfactual sensitivity, traceable carry-over | universal determination or inevitability |

Sedimentation, bounded irreversibility, and unrealized alternatives are property and historical-support declarations that can strengthen, qualify, or limit the chain. They are not compulsory labels for every trajectory.

#### Minimal review chain

The running case can now be stated in its minimal integrated form.

**Configuration `K0`**

```text
review criteria remain unsettled;
approval authority is concentrated;
a reconsideration request is active;
no operative review rule yet binds later requests.
```

**Event `E1` and transition `T1`**

```text
E1 = adoption of the review rule

T1 = supported change from K0 to K1 through:
     adoption,
     allocation of review authority,
     and a thirty-day reconsideration commitment.
```

**Configuration `K1`**

```text
the rule is operative;
reopening requires the new procedure;
the reconsideration commitment structures expectation.
```

**Non-event `N1` and transition `T2`**

```text
N1 = reconsideration not realized within the supported window

T2 = supported change from K1 to K2 through:
     elapsed expectation window,
     reminders and confirmations,
     bounded non-realization,
     and persistence of the operative rule.
```

**Configuration `K2`**

```text
the rule remains operative;
the commitment persists as unresolved residue;
reopening carries increased explanation and documentation burden.
```

**Sequence `S_review`**

```text
K0 < E1/T1 < K1 < N1/T2 < K2
```

This is a selected transition-order sequence. It becomes a path only when actual traversal, selection, constitutive connectedness, source trace, branch disclosure, and loss are established.

**Path `P_review`**

```text
K0
→ actual adoption branch
→ K1
→ actual bounded non-realization branch
→ K2
```

The path excludes the documented rejected deferral branch and declares that exclusion.

**Trajectory `G_review`**

```text
P_review
+ unresolved commitment persists
+ procedural and explanation costs accumulate
+ later review access remains conditioned by the prior path
→ bounded trajectory candidate
```

**Path-dependence property `PD_review?`**

The trajectory does not automatically establish the property. `PD_review?` requires evidence that current conditions alone are insufficient and that a supported alternative order or branch would materially alter the tested later relation.

Where that evidence remains incomplete:

```text
G_review
→ admissible_with_bounded_claim

PD_review
→ claim_reduction_required
```

This is the intended burden-preserving result.

#### Contract-required negative variant — chronology that fails to become a path

A second record contains only:

```text
Day 1: proposal entered
Day 12: meeting held
Day 18: rule listed as operative
Day 46: reminder sent
```

The dates may support a chronology and perhaps a limited record-order sequence. They do not identify:

- the relevant source and target configurations;
- the transition relations;
- whether the same reference object is tracked;
- actual traversal of a constitutive chain;
- a selection rule;
- branch or omission status;
- source-supported loss.

The correct reduction is:

```text
path claim
→ claim_reduction_required

retained object
→ chronology or limited sequence
```

If the analysis treats the dates as sufficient proof of a path, the result is:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

Changing the label to “trajectory,” widening the temporal scope, or moving to a higher relative level does not rescue the failed claim.

#### C3-NC-01 — competing underdetermined trajectories

Suppose the shared source set supports two bounded reconstructions of the same traversed review path:

- **procedural trajectory:** the prior path sediments reopening cost and makes the adopted arrangement easier to reproduce;
- **responsibility-residue trajectory:** the unresolved commitment sediments responsibility ambiguity and repeated explanation burden.

Both reconstructions preserve the same path but select different load-bearing relations. The sources do not warrant reducing one to the other or combining them into a single master trajectory. Nor do they support a decisive strong path-dependence claim for either.

The analysis must state:

```text
captured structure:
  actual review path,
  persistent unresolved commitment,
  later procedural and explanation burden

uncaptured structure:
  decisive priority between the two trajectory constructions,
  complete mechanism of carry-over,
  strong counterfactual path dependence

limiting condition:
  shared sources underdetermine one exclusive trajectory account

rival claims:
  procedural trajectory,
  responsibility-residue trajectory

re-entry condition:
  new sources distinguishing carrier, later effect,
  or supported alternative-order sensitivity
```

The result may be:

```text
non_capture
```

Non-Capture does not protect an unsupported master trajectory. It records that the bounded alternatives remain unresolved under the current sources.

#### Downward claim reduction

The chapter therefore fixes the following reduction route:

```text
path-dependence burden fails
→ trajectory may remain

trajectory burden fails
→ path may remain

path burden fails
→ sequence may remain

sequence burden fails
→ chronology may remain
```

A failed stronger claim is not erased by relabeling, reframing, changing granularity, moving relative level, or selecting a new target function.

```text
new temporal label
≠ rescue of failed historical claim

new transformation
= new testable claim
```

#### WP3 boundary and handoff

Sections 3.9–3.13 establish:

- path dependence as a separately tested bounded property;
- sedimentation as source-supported accumulated or persistent historical load;
- irreversibility as frame-, claim-, relation-, and restoration-criterion-bound;
- unrealized alternatives as source-supported historically available but non-traversed continuations;
- the complete Minimal Temporal Object Chain and downgrade ladder;
- Mandatory Stop and Non-Capture without protection of weak claims.

WP3 does not:

- operationalize PATH Chapters 9–13;
- define the full branch taxonomy;
- duplicate the complete `COMPOSE` procedure;
- decide operation identity;
- provide the full continuity or admissibility architecture;
- invent a second Shared Transformation Record;
- infer causality, alternative availability, path dependence, teleology, or irreversibility from timestamps or labels;
- create a new PMS primitive, fourth STRATA operation, or new Output Class.

The WP3 result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality now attaches only to the integrated Chapter 3 synchronization, completion test, formal handoff, case assignment, and provisional lock decision in WP4.

#### Chapter 3 integrated completion boundary

Sections 3.1–3.13 jointly establish the canonical temporal object grammar required by the Chapter Contract. Every stronger step carries additional burden, path dependence remains a separately tested property, stronger-claim failure may retain a warranted weaker temporal object, and Mandatory Stop or Non-Capture remain available where the stronger reconstruction cannot be supported or responsibly closed.

Chapter 3 supplies PATH with controlled definitions. It does not duplicate the `COMPOSE` procedure, decide operation identity, create a closed temporal ontology, infer historical truth from timestamps, or increase analytical authority through temporal detail.

The integrated Chapter 3 result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

Chapter 3 is provisionally locked. Later reopening requires a documented contradiction, failed case burden, ownership conflict, schema pressure, or source-supported boundary finding.

<a id="chapter-4-the-three-strata-operations-compose-decompose-and-project_as"></a>

## 4. The Three STRATA Operations: COMPOSE, DECOMPOSE, and PROJECT_AS

STRATA transformations become unreliable when an analyst reports only that an account has moved “up,” “down,” “inside,” “outside,” “wider,” “finer,” or “into another context.” Those expressions may describe a coordinate relation, a change in presentation, or a local procedural step. They do not identify what happened to the source object, what kind of target was produced, what had to remain continuous, or what loss entered the movement.

Chapter 4 therefore defines the identity of the three and only three core STRATA operations:

```text
COMPOSE
DECOMPOSE
PROJECT_AS
```

The inventory is closed within STRATA’s declared transformation grammar. The closure is not an ontological claim that every analytical act in reality belongs to one of three metaphysical movements. Comparison, validation, audit, calibration, translation, recontextualization, and record completion may all be methodologically important without becoming additional core operations. Where a proposed movement cannot be classified without distortion, STRATA must retain Stop, Non-Capture, or explicit architecture-revision pressure rather than hide a fourth operation inside ambiguous wording.

The three identities are relational:

```text
many or sequential source structures
→ new composite analytical object
= COMPOSE
```

```text
provisionally compressed occurrence or composite
→ finer relational reconstruction of the same reference object
= DECOMPOSE
```

```text
origin-typed source object
→ bounded contextual target function
= PROJECT_AS
```

Each operation occurrence has exactly one operation kind. More than one operation may be required across an analysis, but the result is then an operation chain of separately declared occurrences. A chain is not a compound fourth operation, and no link inherits admissibility from another.

Chapter 4 defines operation identity, core source–target signatures, transformation direction, level relation, operation-chain separation, non-invertibility, the integrated confusion analysis, the Minimal Operation Declaration, minimal preservation and loss duties, and central failure boundaries. The chapter does not duplicate the detailed procedures later owned by Chapters 15, 20, and 30, the continuity architecture of Chapter 5, the Admissibility Band of Chapter 6, or the Shared Transformation Record of Chapter 7.

The governing rule is:

```text
operation identity
≠ direction metaphor
≠ coordinate change
≠ temporal-object label
```

### 4.1 Why Operations Must Be Explicit

An operation claim states more than that an analysis changed. It states **how a typed source relation produced or tested a target relation under preservation and loss burdens**. Without that declaration, the same informal movement can conceal different transformations.

A move from several dated entries to one “review history” may be:

- a chronology with no transformation;
- an aggregation that counts or collects entries;
- a `COMPOSE` occurrence forming a path object;
- a summary that compresses an already formed object;
- or an unsupported retrospective label.

A move from one path description to a finer account may be:

- added description;
- a new source collection;
- a competing `COMPOSE` occurrence under a different selection rule;
- or `DECOMPOSE` of the same path occurrence.

A move from a review path to the phrase “institutional memory” may be:

- a changed frame;
- a useful analogy;
- a rhetorical label;
- or `PROJECT_AS` into a bounded orientation function in a later context.

The wording alone cannot decide among these possibilities. Operation identity depends on the source signature, target relation, reference relation, preservation duty, and characteristic loss.

#### Operation type, occurrence, and result

An **operation type** is one of the three canonical transformation forms. It supplies a general source–target signature and the family of burdens that every occurrence must satisfy.

An **operation occurrence** is one particular declared use of exactly one operation type on specified source structures under a bounded transformation context.

The **result** is the object, reconstruction, or contextual function produced or tested by that occurrence. It is not identical with the occurrence that produced it.

```text
operation type
≠ operation occurrence
≠ result object or function
```

The distinction prevents two recurrent collapses. First, the existence of the type `DECOMPOSE` does not imply that every finer description is an admissible occurrence. Second, a composite path produced by `COMPOSE` is not itself the operation `COMPOSE`; it may later become a source object for another occurrence.

#### Occurrence-level exclusivity

For one declared occurrence:

```text
exactly one operation kind
```

If the source relation forms a new composite object, the occurrence is `COMPOSE`. If it reconstructs the same compressed reference object more finely, it is `DECOMPOSE`. If it preserves the origin-typed object while assigning a bounded target function, it is `PROJECT_AS`.

An account cannot declare one occurrence as both `DECOMPOSE` and `PROJECT_AS` because a finer reconstruction later supports a contextual function. Those are two claims with different targets and failure conditions. The first may succeed while the second fails. They must therefore remain separate occurrences.

#### C4-COUNT-01 — proposed fourth operation

Suppose an analyst proposes a fourth operation called `RECONTEXTUALIZE`, defined only as “move an object into another interpretive context.” The candidate must be reduced through the closure test.

If only the analytical frame changes, the movement is a coordinate change governed by Chapter 2. It is not yet an operation.

If the source object is assigned a bounded source-dependent function in the new context while its origin type remains unchanged, the signature is `PROJECT_AS`.

If several source structures are selected and related to form a new contextual object, the signature is `COMPOSE`.

If the same compressed object is opened more finely inside the new context, the signature is `DECOMPOSE`.

If none of those relations can be established without distortion, the correct result is not an undeclared fourth operation. The analysis must retain the movement as unclassified, stop the transformation claim, or record non-capture and possible architecture-revision pressure.

```text
proposed fourth operation
→ coordinate change, existing operation, local procedure,
  audit act, output condition, or unresolved pressure
```

The local result is:

```text
no fourth operation admitted
```

This closure remains bounded to STRATA’s transformation grammar.

#### Shared identity test

Before assigning an operation kind, the analysis must answer five questions:

1. What typed source object or source set is being transformed?
2. Is the target a new composite object, a finer reconstruction of the same reference object, or a contextual function?
3. Which reference, type, relation, or function must remain recoverable?
4. Which selection, compression, exclusion, uncertainty, or irrecoverability enters?
5. What would make the proposed identity fail rather than merely make the result less useful?

If those questions cannot be answered, operation identity remains open.

```text
“higher-level”
“zoomed-in”
“reframed”
→ insufficient operation declaration
```

The appropriate disposition may be `claim_reduction_required`, `mandatory_stop`, `failed_transformation`, or `non_capture`, depending on whether a weaker retained claim remains.

### 4.2 COMPOSE

`COMPOSE` is the operation that forms a new composite analytical object from multiple or sequential source structures under declared selection, ordering, formation, preservation, and loss conditions.

Its canonical relation is:

```text
many or sequential source structures
→ new composite analytical object
```

The phrase **new composite analytical object** is decisive. `COMPOSE` does not merely place items next to one another, count them, list them in time, or summarize them under a convenient heading. The target must possess a warranted formation relation through which the selected sources become analytically legible as one bounded object for the tested claim.

#### Source and target signature

A `COMPOSE` source must contain at least two typed source structures or one declared sequential source family whose internal units are independently identifiable. The source declaration must make visible:

- the source objects and their typing;
- the reference relation that allows them to enter one candidate formation;
- the ordering rule where sequence matters;
- the selection rule;
- the constitutive relation through which the target is formed;
- relevant frame, granularity, relative level, temporal scope, source scope, and claim scope.

The target is a new composite analytical object. It is not an exhaustive totality of its sources, and it does not automatically receive a contextual target function.

```text
composite target object
≠ source set
≠ chronology
≠ target function
```

A path may be a `COMPOSE` target when configurations, events, non-events, and transitions are selected and related as an actually traversed, claim-relevant formation. Chapter 3 defines what a path claim requires. Chapter 4 identifies the transformation that forms that path from its selected source structures.

#### Preservation duty

`COMPOSE` must preserve, where constitutive:

- source reference and source typing;
- the ordering relation used by the formation;
- the relations that make the target more than a list;
- relevant heterogeneity, including non-events and branch pressure;
- enough source trace to test the composite’s identity and claim.

Preservation does not require reproducing every source detail inside the target. Composition necessarily selects and compresses. The burden is to make that compression visible and to preserve the relations on which the target claim depends.

#### Loss pressure

Typical `COMPOSE` loss includes:

```yaml
loss:
  preserved: source identities and constitutive order needed by the path claim
  compressed: local detail not required at the declared composite granularity
  excluded: non-selected branches, side relations, and out-of-scope episodes
  uncertain: incompletely supported relations among selected sources
  irrecoverable: source distinctions no longer reconstructible from the composite alone
```

The loss account prevents the target from presenting itself as a lossless sum or complete history.

#### C4-CMP-01 — composing the bounded review path

The shared source family contains:

- `K0`, the initial review configuration;
- `E1/T1`, the adoption event and transition into the operative review arrangement;
- `K1`, the adopted configuration;
- `N1/T2`, the bounded non-realization of promised reconsideration and the transition into unresolved persistence;
- `K2`, the later configuration in which the rule remains operative and reopening carries increased burden.

The operation occurrence declares:

```text
occurrence: C4-CMP-01
kind: COMPOSE
source: K0, E1/T1, K1, N1/T2, K2
ordering: supported transition order
selection: include structures that alter review access,
           expectation, persistence, or reopening burden
target: P_review, a bounded composite review path
expected praxeological difference:
  make actual traversal and accumulated branch exclusion inspectable
```

The formation rule is not “all dated material belongs together.” It is:

```text
selected configurations and transitions
+ supported order
+ actual traversal
+ constitutive relation to review access and persistence
→ bounded review-path composite
```

The target preserves the selected source references, the adoption branch, the bounded non-event, and the relation between the two transitions. It compresses turn-level detail and excludes the rejected deferral branch while declaring that exclusion.

The bounded result can be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The output is the composite path object `P_review`. No orientation, memory, frame, or authority function is assigned by this occurrence.

#### C4-CMP-02 — chronology that fails COMPOSE

A second source set contains only:

```text
Day 1: proposal entered
Day 12: meeting held
Day 18: rule listed as operative
Day 46: reminder sent
```

The entries support a chronology. They do not establish:

- source and target configurations;
- constitutive transitions;
- actual traversal of a selected review relation;
- a formation rule;
- praxeological gain from treating the entries as one path;
- branch, omission, or loss status.

Calling the list “the review path” does not create those relations.

```text
chronological order
≠ composite formation
```

For the attempted `COMPOSE` claim, the result is:

```text
failed_transformation
```

A bounded chronology may remain as a separate retained object. It does not inherit path identity from the failed composition.

#### COMPOSE failure boundary

`COMPOSE` fails, requires reduction, or must stop when:

- the target is only a list, count, chronology, or summary;
- no constitutive relation is declared or supported;
- selection erases a source feature that carries the target claim;
- actual heterogeneity is replaced by retrospective unity;
- the target cannot be distinguished from its source set;
- a target function is asserted without a separate `PROJECT_AS` occurrence;
- the proposed composite creates no traceable praxeological difference.

```text
more sources
≠ stronger composite

more compression
≠ greater unity
```

Detailed `COMPOSE` preconditions and procedure remain owned by Chapter 15.

### 4.3 DECOMPOSE

`DECOMPOSE` is the operation that reconstructs the finer relational structure of a provisionally compressed occurrence or composite while retaining that source object as the reference and test target.

Its canonical relation is:

```text
provisionally compressed occurrence or composite
→ finer relational reconstruction of the same reference object
```

The phrase **same reference object** is decisive. `DECOMPOSE` does not license an analyst to abandon the source object, choose a new formation rule, and call the resulting object a finer version of the original. Nor does it apply to abstract Δ–Ψ operator types. Only concrete occurrences and composites can be opened.

#### Source and target signature

A `DECOMPOSE` source must be:

- an identifiable occurrence or composite;
- provisionally compressed relative to a declared finer question;
- supported by source material capable of carrying finer distinctions;
- stable enough as a reference object to test continuity across reconstruction.

The target is a finer reconstruction of that same reference object. It may revise the coarser account, reject a prior source-function claim, or show no warranted difference. It is not automatically a new object merely because it contains more distinctions.

```text
finer target reconstruction
≠ new reference object automatically
≠ truth priority
```

#### Preservation and source-function test

`DECOMPOSE` must preserve or explicitly test:

- source reference identity;
- constitutive component relations;
- the relation between finer components and the coarser object;
- the status of the coarser source function or claim;
- source trace for each added distinction.

The coarser claim may be confirmed, refined, revised, partially preserved, or rejected. A successful `DECOMPOSE` occurrence is not defined by confirming the prior claim. It is defined by a warranted finer reconstruction of the same object.

#### Loss and recovery pressure

Decomposition does not recover an untouched original. It works from the compressed object and available sources. Its loss profile may include:

```yaml
loss:
  preserved: path reference and constitutive relation among internal transitions
  compressed: distinctions still below the declared finer granularity
  excluded: source material outside the same-path reconstruction
  uncertain: component relations only partially recoverable
  irrecoverable: distinctions lost in prior composition or absent from surviving sources
```

Finer reconstruction changes visibility. It may foreground relations that were previously compressed and background the composite unity that made the source useful. That shift must be declared.

#### C4-DEC-01 — opening the same review path

The source is the `P_review` path occurrence produced in `C4-CMP-01`. The finer question asks how its two transitions differ in structure and consequence.

```text
occurrence: C4-DEC-01
kind: DECOMPOSE
source: P_review
source status: provisionally compressed path occurrence
target: finer reconstruction of P_review as
        K0 → E1/T1 → K1 → N1/T2 → K2
reference rule: P_review remains the tested object
expected praxeological difference:
  distinguish adoption transition from
  persistence-through-non-realization transition
```

The decomposition reconstructs:

- `T1` as change produced through a positively realized adoption event;
- `T2` as change produced through elapsed expectation, reminders, bounded non-realization, and persistence;
- the different role of positive event and non-event structure in the same path;
- the way both transitions remain constitutive of the coarser path claim.

The target is not a second path. It is a finer relational reconstruction of `P_review`. The coarser path function—making actual traversal from `K0` to `K2` inspectable—remains supported, while its internal transition structure becomes more discriminating.

The bounded result can be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

If the added distinctions had made no warranted difference to the tested reconstruction, a separately justified result could instead be `resolution_neutral`. Finer detail alone does not force either outcome.

#### C4-DEC-02 — competing path disguised as decomposition

Suppose an analyst begins with `P_review` but then selects informal side conversations, excludes `N1/T2`, changes the formation relation from review access to interpersonal influence, and produces a different chain called `P_influence`.

The target no longer reconstructs the same path under finer granularity. It applies a new selection rule, changes the constitutive relation, and forms a competing object.

```text
new selection
+ new formation relation
+ different path identity
≠ DECOMPOSE of P_review
```

The attempted `DECOMPOSE` claim therefore yields:

```text
failed_transformation
```

`P_influence` might become the target of a new `COMPOSE` occurrence if its own sources and formation burden are satisfied. That possibility does not rescue the failed decomposition claim.

#### Operator-type boundary

The abstract operator type `Θ Temporality`, `Φ Recontextualization`, or any other Δ–Ψ type may not be decomposed. An analyst may decompose a concrete occurrence typed through one or more operators, or a composite containing such occurrences. The type itself remains the PMS Base grammar element.

```text
DECOMPOSE(Theta occurrence)
→ potentially admissible candidate

DECOMPOSE(Θ operator type)
→ prohibited
```

#### DECOMPOSE failure boundary

`DECOMPOSE` fails, requires reduction, or must stop when:

- the source is not an identifiable compressed occurrence or composite;
- the target substitutes a new reference object;
- added components are not related back to the source object;
- the analysis atomizes rather than reconstructs relations;
- new detail exceeds source support;
- finer resolution is used to evade a failed coarser claim;
- an abstract PMS operator type is treated as decomposable;
- no praxeological difference or valid resolution-neutral result can be stated.

Detailed `DECOMPOSE` procedure and resolution outcomes remain owned by Chapter 20.

### 4.4 PROJECT_AS

`PROJECT_AS` is the operation that preserves an origin-typed source object while assigning it a bounded contextual target function within a declared target context.

Its canonical relation is:

```text
origin-typed source object
→ bounded contextual target function
```

The target is a function, not a replacement object type. The source object remains what it was in its origin reconstruction. A path projected as an orientation function remains a path. A trajectory projected as a frame-function remains a trajectory. No projection creates a new PMS primitive.

#### Source and target signature

A `PROJECT_AS` source requires:

- an identifiable typed source object;
- preserved source reference and origin type;
- a declared target context;
- a bounded target function;
- a validity scope;
- constitutive source features that support the function;
- alternatives, including analogy-only or no projection.

The target relation is a source-dependent contextual function. It may foreground some source features and background others, but the function must remain sensitive to material source changes.

```text
target function
≠ target object type
≠ permanent property
≠ new primitive
```

Chapter 5 later supplies the full origin-type, functional-continuity, and transformation-context architecture. Chapter 4 fixes the operation identity that depends on those duties.

#### Preservation duty

`PROJECT_AS` must preserve:

- source reference;
- origin type;
- constitutive source trace;
- the declared target context and validity scope;
- sensitivity of the target function to source change.

The projection fails if the same function would be asserted regardless of what the source object contained. Source-indifferent usefulness is not functional continuity.

#### Loss pressure

Projection foregrounds and backgrounds. Its bounded loss profile may include:

```yaml
loss:
  preserved: source path identity, historical order, and relevant branch exclusion
  compressed: internal path complexity not required by the later orientation function
  excluded: alternative functions and out-of-context uses
  uncertain: degree to which later participants rely on the source path
  irrecoverable: none claimed merely from projection; prior losses remain inherited and visible
```

The target function may cease when the context changes. That cessation does not alter the origin type.

#### C4-PROJ-01 — review path as bounded orientation function

A later process-review meeting must decide which procedural safeguards require explicit monitoring. The source object is `P_review`, origin-typed as a bounded path formed from the earlier review process.

```text
occurrence: C4-PROJ-01
kind: PROJECT_AS
source: P_review
origin type: path
target context: later process-review meeting
target function:
  bounded orientation function for identifying where
  promised reconsideration and reopening burden require monitoring
validity scope:
  this meeting and its declared procedural review question
```

The load-bearing source features are:

- the adopted rule;
- the documented promise of reconsideration;
- the bounded non-event;
- the persistence of the rule;
- the later increase in reopening burden.

If those features were materially absent or contradicted, the orientation function would weaken or fail. The function is therefore source-dependent rather than merely rhetorically apt.

The source remains `P_review`, origin type `path`. It does not become “Frame,” “Memory,” “Attractor,” or any other PMS operator type.

The bounded result can be:

```text
admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

No authority to prescribe a decision, classify persons, or generalize beyond the declared meeting follows from the projection.

#### C4-PROJ-02 — label substitution without function trace

Suppose the same path is called “the organization’s institutional memory,” but the analysis does not declare:

- a target context;
- a bounded function;
- a validity scope;
- constitutive source features;
- source-change sensitivity;
- alternatives to the label.

The phrase may be suggestive, but rhetorical fit does not establish `PROJECT_AS`.

```text
apt label
≠ bounded target function
```

Where the resemblance remains useful and clearly non-typed, the result may be:

```text
analogy_only
```

If the analyst insists that the label replaces the path’s origin type or applies globally, the projection claim fails and continuation may require `mandatory_stop`.

#### Recontextualization boundary

A changed frame can alter what features of the source are relevant without assigning a target function. Likewise, a concrete `Φ`-typed occurrence may support recontextualization in PMS without constituting `PROJECT_AS`.

```text
changed frame
≠ PROJECT_AS automatically

Φ occurrence
≠ PROJECT_AS automatically
```

Projection begins only when the origin-typed source object is claimed to perform a bounded function in the target context and that function remains traceable to the source.

#### PROJECT_AS failure boundary

`PROJECT_AS` fails, requires reduction, or must stop when:

- target context or validity scope is missing;
- the target is only a new label or analogy;
- the claimed function is source-indifferent;
- constitutive source trace is absent;
- the function is generalized beyond its context;
- origin type is replaced or retrospectively overwritten;
- a new PMS primitive or authority claim is inferred;
- alternatives and no-projection remain unexamined.

Detailed `PROJECT_AS` procedure remains owned by Chapter 30.

#### WP1 core-signature boundary and handoff

Sections 4.1–4.4 establish:

- the explicit operation-identity requirement;
- the bounded three-operation closure;
- occurrence-level exclusivity;
- the core source, target, preservation, loss, confusion, and failure signature of each operation;
- common-source-family positive and negative examples;
- the prohibition on decomposing operator types or replacing origin type.

At the WP1 checkpoint, Sections 4.1–4.4 did not yet define direction, relative-level tendencies, or the full operation-chain architecture. Sections 4.5–4.7 now supply those duties without revising the three core signatures.

The following remain outside WP1 and WP2:

- non-invertibility;
- the integrated operation-confusion analysis;
- the complete Minimal Operation Declaration;
- detailed operation procedures;
- the Shared Transformation Record;
- the full continuity or Admissibility Band architecture.

The WP1 result is:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

The provisionality attaches to the remaining Sections 4.5–4.10, integrated synchronization, formal-model handoff, case assignment, completion test, and provisional lock decision.

### 4.5 Transformation Direction

Every operation has a source–target direction, but that direction is not an independent operation identity. The arrow in an operation signature states which relation is treated as source and which relation is produced or tested as target. It does not state that the target is ontologically higher, epistemically deeper, normatively better, historically later, or practically more authoritative.

The three core directions are:

```text
COMPOSE
many or sequential source structures
→ new composite analytical object
```

```text
DECOMPOSE
provisionally compressed occurrence or composite
→ finer relational reconstruction of the same reference object
```

```text
PROJECT_AS
origin-typed source object
→ bounded contextual target function
```

These arrows are directional signatures, not rank arrows. They identify a transformation relation by its source and target burdens. No operation direction is ontological.

#### Five directions that must remain separate

Operation analysis may involve several different senses of direction. They must not be collapsed:

1. **operation direction** — the source–target relation that identifies `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, or `PROJECT_AS`;
2. **coordinate direction** — a declared change or stability in frame, granularity, relative level, temporal scope, source scope, or claim scope;
3. **temporal direction** — before/after order within the source or target object;
4. **claim direction** — movement from a narrower claim to a wider claim, or from a stronger claim to a reduced claim;
5. **authority direction** — any purported increase in truth, legitimacy, decision power, or application standing.

Only the first identifies an operation. The second is governed by Chapter 2, the third by Chapter 3, the fourth by claim and admissibility control, and the fifth is never inherited from transformation structure.

```text
operation direction
≠ coordinate direction
≠ temporal direction
≠ claim direction
≠ authority direction
```

A `COMPOSE` occurrence may be temporally retrospective, a `DECOMPOSE` occurrence may analyze a later-produced composite, and `PROJECT_AS` may assign a function in a context contemporaneous with the source analysis. Temporal order therefore cannot substitute for operation identity.

Likewise, the operation arrow cannot be read as an inverse instruction. Reversing the verbal direction does not automatically yield another valid operation. The full non-invertibility burden remains owned by Section 4.8, but the present boundary is already mandatory:

```text
reverse arrow wording
≠ inverse operation
```

#### Direction is relational, not intrinsic

An object does not possess an intrinsic “upward,” “downward,” or “sideways” direction. Direction appears only in a declared transformation occurrence relative to a source, target, reference relation, and analytical purpose.

The same `P_review` path may be:

- the target of `COMPOSE` when formed from configurations and transitions;
- the source of `DECOMPOSE` when opened into those constitutive transitions;
- the source of `PROJECT_AS` when assigned a bounded orientation function.

No contradiction follows. The object is not intrinsically composite-upward, decomposable-downward, or projectable-sideways. Different occurrences ask different source–target questions.

#### Coordinate stability does not block operation

An operation can occur while frame, relative level, and several scopes remain stable.

`C4-CMP-01` composes a path from selected temporal structures inside the same bounded project-review frame. The new target object may be relatively wider than each source unit, but no frame change is required.

`C4-DEC-01` opens `P_review` at finer granularity while retaining the same reference object, frame, and bounded project-review purpose.

`C4-PROJ-01` changes contextual function, but it need not change the source object’s granularity or erase its original relative position.

Conversely, a coordinate can change without any operation. A frame may shift, a claim may narrow, or a source scope may expand while no new composite, finer same-object reconstruction, or contextual target function is asserted.

```text
coordinate stability
≠ absence of operation

coordinate change
≠ presence of operation
```

#### C4-CONF-01 — direction language without signature burden

An analyst describes a three-step movement:

```text
“move upward to the review history”
“zoom down into the decision”
“reframe it for the next meeting”
```

The phrases appear to map neatly onto `COMPOSE`, `DECOMPOSE`, and `PROJECT_AS`, but none supplies the required source–target relation.

“Move upward” does not identify the selected source structures, formation relation, composite target, or loss account.

“Zoom down” does not establish that the same compressed reference object is being reconstructed rather than replaced by a new selection.

“Reframe” does not establish a bounded target function, preserved origin type, source dependence, or validity scope.

The correct result is not three operation occurrences. It is:

```text
operation identity remains open
```

The direction language may be retained as informal navigation only. Any transformation claim requires `claim_reduction_required`; if the analyst refuses to supply one of the three signatures and nevertheless asserts operation identity, continuation requires `mandatory_stop`.

### 4.6 Operation and Level Relation

The three operations have common relative-level and granularity tendencies, but those tendencies are neither necessary nor sufficient conditions.

```text
COMPOSE
→ often relatively wider

DECOMPOSE
→ often finer in granularity

PROJECT_AS
→ primarily changes contextual function
```

The word **often** is load-bearing. It blocks three false equivalences:

```text
relative upwardness
≠ COMPOSE

relative downwardness
≠ DECOMPOSE

changed context or level
≠ PROJECT_AS
```

#### COMPOSE and relative width

A composite target commonly stands relatively wider than each selected component under a declared part–whole or occurrence–path relation. `P_review`, for example, is wider than `K0`, `T1`, or `N1/T2` when the comparison relation is component-to-path.

That relative width does not make `P_review` ontologically higher. It also does not prove that `COMPOSE` occurred. A category label, chronology, or statistical aggregate may be wider than an item while failing the constitutive formation burden.

`COMPOSE` can also operate among structures already treated at the same relative level. Several peer review episodes may be composed into a bounded comparative configuration if a warranted constitutive relation forms a new object. The operation is identified by composite formation, not by a mandatory ascent through a level hierarchy.

#### DECOMPOSE and finer granularity

`DECOMPOSE` commonly introduces finer granularity, but a finer representation alone remains insufficient. The target must reconstruct the same reference object and reconnect the finer distinctions to its coarser identity or claim.

The relative level can remain stable while granularity changes. Opening `P_review` into `T1` and `T2` may still treat the path itself as the focal object, now represented with greater relational differentiation.

A decomposition can also reveal units positioned differently under a newly declared comparison relation. That coordinate result does not retroactively define the operation. It is an additional Chapter 2 declaration attached to a `DECOMPOSE` occurrence already identified by same-object reconstruction.

#### PROJECT_AS and contextual function

`PROJECT_AS` is least well described by vertical language. Its defining change is functional:

```text
origin-typed source object
→ bounded function in target context
```

The target context may be at a relatively wider, narrower, or peer position. A local path may function as an orientation device in a project-wide review; a project-wide trajectory may function as a bounded comparison device in one local audit; or a source object may perform a function within a peer context.

None of these relations changes the origin type. Nor does a cross-level projection acquire authority from its target context.

```text
cross-level function
≠ cross-level type transfer
≠ authority inheritance
```

#### Operation/coordinate declaration matrix

For every occurrence, the operation identity and coordinate relation should be stated on separate axes:

| Question | Operation axis | Coordinate axis |
| --- | --- | --- |
| What happened to the source relation? | new composite, finer same-object reconstruction, or bounded target function | not decided here |
| Did frame change? | does not identify operation | declare stable or changed frame |
| Did granularity change? | may support but does not define identity | declare stable, finer, coarser, or differently selective |
| Did relative level change? | may accompany any operation | declare focal unit, comparator, relation, and purpose |
| Did scope change? | may affect source or claim burden | declare temporal, source, claim, and validity scope |
| Did authority increase? | never follows from operation | prohibited as inherited result |

The matrix prevents operation semantics from being smuggled into coordinate fields and prevents coordinate declarations from being treated as a completed transformation test.

#### Same operation, different level relations

Two occurrences may share one operation type while differing in relative-level relation.

A `COMPOSE` occurrence can form a path from local temporal units, while another `COMPOSE` occurrence forms a peer-level comparison object from several paths. Both are `COMPOSE` only if each establishes its own formation relation and loss account.

A `DECOMPOSE` occurrence can open a composite into finer components, while another opens a compressed occurrence into relational roles without changing its declared relative level. Both remain `DECOMPOSE` only if reference continuity holds.

A `PROJECT_AS` occurrence can assign a local orientation function in a wider meeting context, while another assigns a peer-comparison function in a parallel review context. Both remain `PROJECT_AS` only if origin type, constitutive source trace, target function, and contextual boundedness hold.

Therefore:

```text
same operation type
≠ same coordinate movement
```

and:

```text
same coordinate movement
≠ same operation type
```

#### Stop against vertical authority

No operation licenses the following inference:

```text
wider target
→ more authoritative claim

finer target
→ truer claim

higher relative position
→ superior practical standing

later target context
→ retroactive validity of source claim
```

Where a level description is used to increase authority without independent support, the operation claim must be separated from the authority claim. The latter requires reduction or stop even if the former remains admissible.

### 4.7 Operation Chains

An **operation chain** is an ordered sequence of two or more separately identified operation occurrences whose source–target handoffs are explicitly declared.

```text
operation occurrence 1
→ declared handoff
→ operation occurrence 2
→ possible further handoff
```

A chain is not a compound operation type. It does not permit one occurrence to carry multiple kinds, and it does not merge local losses or results into one undifferentiated verdict.

```text
operation chain
≠ fourth operation
≠ one multi-kind occurrence
≠ inherited admissibility
```

#### Minimum chain architecture

Every link requires:

- a unique occurrence identity;
- exactly one operation kind;
- a source object, occurrence, reconstruction, or function available at that link;
- a target object, reconstruction, or function produced or tested at that link;
- an independent justification and expected praxeological difference;
- its own selection, preservation, alternatives, loss, and uncertainty account;
- a local result mapped to one canonical Output Class;
- an explicit handoff relation to the next link.

The target of one link may become the source of another only under a declared availability and identity claim. Mere textual succession is not a handoff.

```text
prior target exists in prose
≠ valid successor source automatically
```

A successor may use:

- the prior target object;
- a finer reconstruction produced by the prior link;
- the prior operation occurrence itself as a compressed object of analysis;
- or the original source family under a new operation claim.

Those possibilities must not be conflated.

#### Link-local validity and result retention

Each link is a new testable claim.

```text
earlier admissible link
≠ later admissible link
```

```text
later admissible link
≠ rescue of earlier failed link
```

A chain therefore retains a result vector rather than replacing local outputs with one global label:

```text
chain result:
  link 1: local Output Class
  link 2: local Output Class
  link n: local Output Class
  integrated summary: bounded relation among retained results
```

An integrated summary may state that the chain is partially admissible, stopped at a later link, or non-captured at a handoff. It may not erase the component results.

If an earlier link fails, the alleged target of that link cannot be silently treated as an established successor source. A later operation may still be tested directly on the original source family or on another independently warranted object, but that is a new chain architecture, not rescue of the failed target.

If a later link fails, an earlier admissible target remains available within its own claim boundary unless the later test reveals a source defect that independently reopens the earlier claim. Failure does not propagate merely because links are adjacent.

#### C4-CHAIN-01 — COMPOSE followed by PROJECT_AS

The first occurrence is the already bounded path formation:

```text
C4-CMP-01
kind: COMPOSE
sources: K0, E1/T1, K1, N1/T2, K2
target: P_review
local result: admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The second occurrence uses `P_review` as an origin-typed path source:

```text
C4-PROJ-01
kind: PROJECT_AS
source: P_review
origin type: path
target function: bounded orientation function
                  in the later process-review meeting
local result: admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

The chain is:

```text
COMPOSE(C4-CMP-01)
→ P_review
→ PROJECT_AS(C4-PROJ-01)
→ bounded orientation function
```

The composite path and the contextual function remain non-interchangeable. The second link adds no retroactive evidence that the first composition was correct. It requires the path to be independently available and the function to remain source-dependent.

The chain-level tendency is:

```text
independent link results retained
```

#### C4-CHAIN-02 — DECOMPOSE followed by PROJECT_AS

The first link opens `P_review` into a finer reconstruction that distinguishes adoption-through-event from persistence-through-non-realization:

```text
C4-DEC-01
kind: DECOMPOSE
source: P_review
target: finer reconstruction of P_review
local result: admissible_with_bounded_claim
```

A later meeting asks whether that finer reconstruction can function as a bounded diagnostic orientation for distinguishing safeguards against immediate adoption pressure from safeguards against deferred reconsideration failure.

```text
C4-CHAIN-02-B
kind: PROJECT_AS
source: finer reconstruction of P_review
origin type: finer path reconstruction
target function: bounded diagnostic orientation
                  for this safeguard-comparison question
```

The `PROJECT_AS` link must identify which finer source distinctions carry the function. It cannot claim success merely because the decomposition was informative.

```text
DECOMPOSE success
≠ PROJECT_AS success
```

Where the function remains source-sensitive and bounded, both local results may be `admissible_with_bounded_claim`. If the function does not depend on the distinction between `T1` and `T2`, the later link requires reduction or failure while the finer reconstruction remains available.

#### C4-CHAIN-03 — admissible earlier link, failed later link

Assume `C4-CMP-01` validly forms `P_review`. A later analyst projects it as a universal “institutional memory function” across all future organizational decisions, without a bounded target context, source-change sensitivity, or validity scope.

The chain records:

```text
link 1 — COMPOSE:
  result: admissible_with_bounded_claim

link 2 — PROJECT_AS:
  result: failed_transformation
  retained weaker possibility: analogy_only
```

The failed projection does not invalidate the bounded path formation. Conversely, the path’s admissibility does not lend validity to the universal function.

```text
admissible path retained
+ universal projection rejected
```

This is the required later-link failure pattern.

#### Required chain-family stress set

The following families are all possible only as separately declared occurrences:

| Chain family | Legitimate handoff question | Central confusion to prevent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `COMPOSE → PROJECT_AS` | can a formed composite perform a bounded contextual function? | function treated as part of composition |
| `COMPOSE → DECOMPOSE` | can the formed composite be opened as the same reference object? | decomposition treated as undoing composition |
| `DECOMPOSE → COMPOSE` | can selected outputs of a finer reconstruction form a new composite? | recomposition treated as automatic restoration |
| `DECOMPOSE → PROJECT_AS` | can the finer reconstruction perform a bounded function? | informativeness treated as function proof |
| `PROJECT_AS → DECOMPOSE` | can a compressed projection occurrence be opened into its source–function relations? | target function treated as an abstract decomposable type |
| `COMPOSE → PROJECT_AS → DECOMPOSE` | can a formed object be projected, then can that concrete projection occurrence be opened more finely? | three links collapsed into one “vertical transformation” |

For `PROJECT_AS → DECOMPOSE`, the source of the second link is the **concrete projection occurrence or a compressed projection composite**, not the abstract target-function category. The decomposition tests how source features, contextual conditions, and foreground/background selections carry that occurrence.

For `DECOMPOSE → COMPOSE`, the second link is a new composition claim. It requires a new selection and formation relation. It is not an automatic reversal of the first link.

For `COMPOSE → DECOMPOSE`, successful opening of the composite does not restore untouched pre-composition sources. The full non-invertibility interpretation remains Section 4.8.

#### Collapsed-chain boundary

A single declaration such as:

```text
kind: DECOMPOSE + PROJECT_AS
```

is invalid. It hides two targets, two preservation burdens, two loss profiles, and two possible failures.

Before the chain can be evaluated, the analyst must separate:

1. the finer same-object reconstruction;
2. the contextual function claim;
3. the handoff between them.

Refusal to separate the links requires `mandatory_stop`. The full `C4-STOP-01` and unresolved-identity `C4-NC-01` confusion cases remain reserved for WP3, where operation confusion and the Minimal Operation Declaration are integrated.

#### WP2 direction-and-chain boundary and handoff

Sections 4.5–4.7 establish:

- source–target direction without ontological, epistemic, normative, or authority ascent;
- separation of operation direction from coordinate, temporal, claim, and authority direction;
- common but non-defining level and granularity tendencies;
- independent operation and coordinate declarations;
- operation chains as ordered sets of exclusive occurrences;
- explicit source–target handoffs;
- link-local validation, loss, uncertainty, and Output Class retention;
- all required chain-family patterns;
- preservation of earlier admissible results when a later link fails.

At the WP2 checkpoint, Sections 4.5–4.7 did not yet define the three canonical non-invertibility statements, the integrated confusion matrix, or the complete Minimal Operation Declaration. Sections 4.8–4.10 now supply those duties without revising the earlier operation identities or chain rules.

The following remain outside WP1–WP3:

- the complete Shared Transformation Record;
- detailed operation procedures;
- the full continuity and Admissibility Band architectures;
- integrated synchronization, formal-model handoff, case assignment, completion testing, and the Chapter 4 provisional-lock decision.

The historical WP2 result remains:

```text
admissible_but_provisional
```

### 4.8 Non-Invertibility

STRATA operations are directional but not losslessly reversible. A later movement that resembles a reversal is a **new operation occurrence with a new source, target, preservation burden, loss account, and testable claim**. It cannot be presumed to recover a neutral or untouched original.

The three canonical statements are:

```text
DECOMPOSE(COMPOSE(X))
≠ X
```

```text
COMPOSE(DECOMPOSE(X))
≠ X
```

```text
PROJECT_AS(X)
≠ X as a new origin type
```

These statements do not say that every source feature is destroyed, that no comparison with earlier sources is possible, or that all transformation is metaphysically irreversible. They say that selection, ordering, compression, reconstruction, foregrounding, contextualization, and loss make the later occurrence analytically non-identical to the untouched source relation.

```text
non-invertibility
≠ total loss
≠ permission to ignore preservation
≠ metaphysical irreversibility
≠ proof that source recovery is impossible
```

#### `DECOMPOSE(COMPOSE(X)) ≠ X`

`COMPOSE` does not merely place source structures into a container. It selects source units, declares an ordering or other constitutive relation, forms a target object, foregrounds some relations, compresses others, and excludes still others. Even when the source files remain available, the composite occurrence has already established a new analytical object and a bounded formation claim.

A later `DECOMPOSE` occurrence therefore begins from the composed object as its reference and test target. It asks how that object can be reconstructed more finely while remaining identifiable. The decomposition may reconnect the target to original source units, but it does so through a new selection of distinctions and a new reconstruction context.

#### C4-NINV-01 — decomposing the composed review path

`C4-CMP-01` forms `P_review` from `K0`, `E1/T1`, `K1`, `N1/T2`, and `K2`. The composition preserves the bounded review relation and actual traversal while compressing conversational detail, rival segmentation, and some uncertainty about minor intervening acts.

`C4-DEC-01` later opens `P_review` into the adoption transition and the non-realization transition. The target can be a warranted finer reconstruction of the same path, but it is not the untouched pre-composition source family:

```text
original source family:
  multiple typed records under their original source contexts

COMPOSE target:
  P_review under one declared formation relation

later DECOMPOSE target:
  finer reconstruction of P_review
  under a new reconstruction question
```

The decomposition may preserve source references and recover distinctions. It cannot erase the earlier selection, restore excluded source possibilities automatically, or make the composite occurrence never have happened.

A correct local conclusion is:

```text
same reference object retained
+ finer reconstruction warranted
+ untouched source state not restored
```

This supports a bounded non-invertibility account, not an absolute irrecoverability claim.

#### `COMPOSE(DECOMPOSE(X)) ≠ X`

A valid `DECOMPOSE` occurrence changes what distinctions are analytically available. It may separate relations that the coarser object compressed, reveal uncertainty, revise a boundary, or show that the earlier claim requires reduction. A later `COMPOSE` occurrence must then make a fresh selection and formation claim from those available outputs.

Recomposition is therefore not an automatic undo command. Even where the analyst selects components similar to those previously compressed, the new occurrence has:

- a later transformation context;
- an explicit awareness of the finer distinctions;
- a new selection rule;
- a new formation relation;
- a new loss profile;
- and a new local result.

#### C4-NINV-02 — recomposing after decomposition

After `C4-DEC-01`, the analyst possesses a finer reconstruction distinguishing `T1` from `T2`. The analyst then selects both transitions and composes a new “safeguard-failure path” focused on how immediate procedural safeguards and delayed reconsideration safeguards interact.

That target may be warranted, but it is not automatically `P_review` restored. The focal relation has changed from the bounded review traversal to a safeguard-specific formation rule.

```text
DECOMPOSE output
→ selected finer distinctions

new COMPOSE occurrence
→ new formation relation
→ new composite claim
```

If the analyst instead intends to reassert `P_review`, that reassertion still requires a fresh occurrence-level declaration and loss account. Nominal sameness of the label does not establish identity of the composite occurrence.

#### `PROJECT_AS(X) ≠ X as a new origin type`

`PROJECT_AS` does not perform a reversible type conversion because no origin-type conversion is admissible in the first place. It assigns a bounded target function while preserving source reference and origin type.

For `P_review`:

```text
origin type: path
projected target function: bounded orientation function
```

The function may later end, be withdrawn, be revised, or fail in another context. None of those changes reveals that the source had become a new origin type and then changed back.

#### C4-NINV-03 — function does not become type

`C4-PROJ-01` allows `P_review` to function as a bounded orientation device in a later process-review meeting. Suppose a later analyst writes:

```text
P_review became an Orientation operator,
then reverted to a path after the meeting.
```

The statement is inadmissible. No new PMS operator or origin type was created. The correct relation is:

```text
P_review remained a path
while performing one bounded target function
within one declared context
```

Removing the function leaves the origin-typed source object without that target function. It does not reverse a type transformation.

If the analyst insists that the projected function replaced the origin type, Type Integrity fails and the claim requires `mandatory_stop` or `failed_transformation` according to whether continued analysis still depends on the prohibited replacement.

#### Preservation and loss under apparent reversal

Every reverse-looking movement must reassess the full loss structure:

```yaml
loss:
  preserved:
  compressed:
  excluded:
  uncertain:
  irrecoverable:
```

The analyst may not copy the prior loss record mechanically. A later decomposition can recover some compressed distinctions while introducing new exclusions. A recomposition can preserve newly visible relations while compressing others. Withdrawal of a projection can remove a target function while leaving historical effects of the projection occurrence relevant to a later claim.

Therefore:

```text
reverse-looking movement
= new testable claim
= new loss assessment
= no presumed restoration
```

### 4.9 Operation Confusion

Operation confusion occurs when a change in wording, coordinate, detail, arrangement, or context is presented as one of the three operations without satisfying its source–target signature. It also occurs when two operations are collapsed into one occurrence or when uncertainty between two plausible operation identities is hidden by choosing the more convenient label.

The primary confusion matrix is:

| Presented movement | Plausible but insufficient reading | Required operation test | Central failure risk |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| dated entries gathered under one heading | chronology or aggregation | does a constitutive relation form a new composite object? | false `COMPOSE` |
| more sentences or finer labels added | description or annotation | is the same compressed occurrence or composite reconstructed relationally? | false `DECOMPOSE` |
| new source selection forms a rival path | competing formation | is reference identity retained, or is this a new `COMPOSE` claim? | competing path disguised as `DECOMPOSE` |
| object placed in another frame | recontextualization | is a bounded target function declared and source-carried? | false `PROJECT_AS` |
| source given an evocative name | analogy or renaming | does the label identify a function with validity scope and sensitivity? | label substitution |
| “higher,” “lower,” or “zoomed” movement | coordinate metaphor | which source–target signature is actually present? | direction substituted for identity |
| two kinds in one declaration | collapsed chain | can the movement be separated into exclusive occurrences and handoffs? | hidden loss and failure |
| abstract operator type “opened” | type analysis | is the source a concrete occurrence or composite? | prohibited type decomposition |

#### Mandatory separation test

Before assigning an operation kind, the analyst must answer seven questions:

1. **What is the declared source object or source set?**
2. **What is the target relation: composite object, finer same-object reconstruction, or contextual function?**
3. **Is reference identity retained, newly formed, or merely assumed?**
4. **Which preservation duty is constitutive for this claim?**
5. **Which loss enters because of selection, compression, reconstruction, or foregrounding?**
6. **Is the apparent movement only a coordinate change, label, analogy, audit act, or procedural step?**
7. **Does the account require more than one operation occurrence?**

If the answers identify no canonical signature, operation identity remains open. If they identify several sequential signatures, the account must be split into a chain. If the evidence cannot decide between two materially different signatures, the uncertainty must remain visible.

```text
classification pressure
≠ permission to force one operation
```

#### C4-STOP-01 — collapsed decomposition and projection

An analyst supplies one declaration:

```text
operation kind: DECOMPOSE + PROJECT_AS
source: P_review
target: finer diagnostic frame-function
result: admissible
```

The declaration collapses two different questions:

1. Is `P_review` reconstructed more finely as the same path?
2. Can that finer reconstruction perform a bounded diagnostic function in a later context?

The collapsed record hides:

- the target of the decomposition;
- the target function of the projection;
- the handoff between them;
- two separate preservation burdens;
- two separate loss profiles;
- and the possibility that one link succeeds while the other fails.

Continuation under one multi-kind occurrence is inadmissible:

```text
mandatory_stop
```

The stop is lifted only when the analyst separates the occurrences and supplies an independently testable declaration for each. The earlier material is not discarded; it is reorganized into a form that can carry local results.

#### C4-NC-01 — same-object decomposition or new-path composition unresolved

A later source bundle introduces additional meeting notes. Some notes appear to clarify the internal relation between `T1` and `T2`; others suggest that the analyst’s new selection foregrounds responsibility allocation rather than the earlier review-path relation.

Two operation identities remain plausible:

```text
candidate A:
  DECOMPOSE P_review
  because the same path is opened more finely

candidate B:
  COMPOSE a responsibility-residue path
  because a new selection and formation relation creates a new object
```

The available sources do not establish whether the responsibility relation was constitutive of `P_review` or introduced only by the later selection. Choosing `DECOMPOSE` would falsely preserve identity; choosing `COMPOSE` would prematurely deny continuity.

The correct output is:

```text
non_capture
```

The Non-Capture record must state:

```text
captured:
  additional responsibility-relevant distinctions exist

uncaptured:
  whether they refine P_review or form a new path

limiting condition:
  constitutive relation and identity evidence are insufficient

rival claims:
  DECOMPOSE candidate
  COMPOSE candidate

re-entry condition:
  source or comparison evidence that resolves the formation relation
```

Non-Capture does not validate either strong operation claim. Nor is it equivalent to missing information alone: the present material positively supports a structured ambiguity between rival transformation identities, while refusing false closure.

#### Output discipline under confusion

Operation confusion may lead to different canonical outputs:

- `analogy_only` where an apt similarity remains but no target function is established;
- `resolution_neutral` where a valid finer reconstruction produces no claim-relevant change;
- `claim_reduction_required` where a narrower operation claim remains supportable;
- `mandatory_stop` where continued collapsed or prohibited classification would corrupt the analysis;
- `failed_transformation` where the tested signature is not met;
- `non_capture` where no adequate retained operation claim resolves the constitutive ambiguity.

No output creates a fourth operation, and no Stop or Non-Capture result protects an unsupported strong claim.

### 4.10 Minimal Operation Declaration

The Minimal Operation Declaration is the smallest conceptual declaration that makes an operation occurrence testable before the complete Shared Transformation Record is populated. It is a prose-bound control form, not a second schema and not a substitute for Chapters 5–7.

A minimally adequate declaration contains:

```text
operation occurrence identity
operation kind
source reference object or source set
source object typing
source coordinates and scopes
target reference object or target function
target coordinates and validity scope
transformation context
justification
expected praxeological difference
selection or formation rule
preservation duties
loss profile
alternatives
uncertainty
local result and continuation boundary
```

#### Existing Shared Record mapping

The declaration maps to existing paths:

```text
/source/reference_object
/source/object_typing
/source/frame
/source/granularity
/source/relative_level
/source/temporal_scope
/source/source_scope

/operation/occurrence_id
/operation/kind
/operation/justification
/operation/expected_praxeological_difference
/operation/selection_rule
/operation/transformation_context
/operation/details

/target/reference_object
/target/object_typing
/target/contextual_function
/target/frame
/target/granularity
/target/relative_level
/target/temporal_scope
/target/validity_scope

/loss/preserved
/loss/compressed
/loss/excluded
/loss/uncertain
/loss/irrecoverable
```

Alternatives, uncertainty, stop conditions, claim boundary, governance, and local result use the already owned Shared Record families. Chapter 4 does not define their complete serialization.

The following parallel fields remain prohibited:

```text
source_type
target_type
source_level
target_level
operation_declaration
compound_operation_kind
```

Conceptual shorthand may use ordinary-language labels, but machine implementation must return to the existing nested paths.

#### Operation-specific declaration burden

The common declaration does not erase signature-specific differences.

For `COMPOSE`, `/operation/details` must be capable of carrying the selected source set, ordering where relevant, formation relation, composite target identity, compression, and excluded alternatives.

For `DECOMPOSE`, it must carry the compressed source occurrence or composite, same-reference claim, finer distinctions, relation to the coarser object, recoverability limits, and whether the coarser claim is retained, reduced, revised, or rejected.
